News UK Interview Prep: answers explained

An easier-to-remember companion to the Multidots question bank (35 questions). Same facts as the model answers, rewritten with plain-English explanations, analogies and examples.

How each answer is laid out

Start with the one-line answer (say it first in the interview), then give the explanation, then one example. The boxes are there to help you remember:

Picture this: an everyday analogy Example: something concrete you can say or show Remember it as: a short memory hook Your story: links it to your own work

Speaking formula (60 to 90 seconds): headline, then 2 or 3 points, then 1 example. If you blank, use "What, why, how, and how I test it."

Practice repo: real code for every question

Every answer below has a "Real code in this repo" box. All the code lives in one plugin, with one folder per question: plugins/news-uk-practice/src/qNN-*/. Each folder holds that question's PHP, its block (if any) and its tests. The snippets on this page are copied from those files by bin/build-explainer.py, so they match exactly.

Practice plan status ("Before the interview" in the question bank): all done and green.

  • Sample plugin with a dynamic block (block.json, render.php, apiVersion 3) and an InnerBlocks block: Q1, Q2.
  • PHPUnit for render.php, Jest + RTL for the edit component, Playwright with @wordpress/e2e-test-utils-playwright: 136 PHPUnit tests (296 assertions), 32 Jest tests, 11 Playwright tests, all passing.
  • Minimal block theme (theme.json, templates, parts, pattern) with a core block bound to post meta: Q4, Q6, Q7.
  • PHPCS (WordPress-Extra + WordPress-VIP-Go), ESLint and tsc: all clean.
  • Server-driven examples checked by hand in Chrome on the local site: Q4, Q8, Q25, Q31, Q35 (see "Seen running" notes).

The first real runs found real bugs (in tests, config and two code paths). Each is explained under its question; they make good "how I test" stories.

Printing always includes every code section, even collapsed ones (about 140 A4 pages). On screen:

Run everything: npm run build, npm run lint:js, npm run typecheck, npm run test:unit, then in the container composer lint and phpunit, then WP_BASE_URL=http://localhost:8080 PW_CHANNEL=chrome npm run test:e2e. Local admin login: newsuk / password.

1. WordPress core, Gutenberg and Full Site Editing

The core of the interview, alongside testing. FSE isn't on your CV, so practise this section the most.

Q1.How do you approach Gutenberg block development? Walk us through building a custom block.

"I scaffold with create-block, describe everything in block.json, register it from a plugin, and usually render it in PHP. I lean on block supports for styling, and I ship it with tests."

Picture thisA block is like a product in a shop. block.json is the label on the box: name, ingredients (attributes), what it supports, and which files are inside. WordPress reads the label and knows how to stock the shelf. You never have to explain it twice in JS and PHP.

The steps, in the order you'd actually do them:

  1. Scaffold: npx @wordpress/create-block article-teaser gives you a working block, build tooling and block.json.
  2. Describe it in block.json, the single source of truth: name, attributes, supports, editor script, styles, render file.
  3. Register it in PHP with register_block_type( __DIR__ . '/build/article-teaser' ). It lives in a plugin, because if the client changes theme their content shouldn't break.
  4. Pick static or dynamic. For news content that depends on live data, go dynamic (render.php). See Q2.
  5. Use supports (colour, spacing, typography) instead of building your own controls. The block then picks up the design tokens from theme.json for free.
  6. Editor UI: useBlockProps, InspectorControls for the sidebar, @wordpress/components for controls, useSelect to read data.
  7. Be safe: escape in render.php, translate strings, use accessible markup.
  8. Test: PHPUnit for render.php, Jest + RTL for the edit component, one Playwright test for insert-and-publish.
{
  "$schema": "https://schemas.wp.org/trunk/block.json",
  "apiVersion": 3,
  "name": "md/article-teaser",
  "title": "Article Teaser",
  "category": "widgets",
  "attributes": { "postId": { "type": "integer" } },
  "supports": { "html": false, "spacing": { "padding": true } },
  "textdomain": "md",
  "editorScript": "file:./index.js",
  "style": "file:./style-index.css",
  "render": "file:./render.php"
}
Example"An Article Teaser block: the editor picks a post in the sidebar, which saves postId. render.php looks up that post and prints the title, image and link. If the post gets a new headline, every teaser updates automatically, because nothing was frozen into the HTML."
Remember it asScaffold, Describe, Register, Render, Style, Test. ("Some Developers Really Read Style Tests")
Your storyZiff Davis product blocks: custom blocks fed by the shared GraphQL catalogue, with per-site overrides in WP Admin. Cover three points: where the logic lived (plugin), how it rendered (dynamic), how you tested it.
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q01-article-teaser/

The Article Teaser follows the steps above exactly: block.json describes it, load.php registers it from the plugin, render.php renders it on the server, and supports gives colour, spacing and font size with no custom controls. The comments in edit.js explain every piece, including why useBlockProps() is needed.

edit.js: useBlockProps, InspectorControls, useSelect plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q01-article-teaser/edit.js
/**
 * Q1, step 6 "Editor UI": what the editor sees and uses inside the block editor.
 *
 * The edit component only runs in the editor. It never decides what readers see:
 * this is a dynamic block, so render.php builds the front-end HTML (Q2).
 * Its job is to let the editor set the block's attributes (postId, showImage)
 * and to show a preview.
 */

// __() marks a string for translation (Q1, step 7 "Be safe": translate strings).
// The second argument is the text domain, which must match block.json "textdomain".
import { __ } from '@wordpress/i18n';

// useBlockProps: the props every block's outer element needs (see below).
// InspectorControls: renders its children in the editor's right-hand sidebar.
import { useBlockProps, InspectorControls } from '@wordpress/block-editor';

// Ready-made, accessible UI from @wordpress/components. Using these instead of
// hand-written inputs gives the standard WordPress look, keyboard support and labels.
import {
	PanelBody,
	Placeholder,
	TextControl,
	ToggleControl,
} from '@wordpress/components';

// useSelect reads data from a @wordpress/data store and re-renders this
// component when that data changes (Q1, step 6: "useSelect to read data").
import { useSelect } from '@wordpress/data';

// core-data is the store that talks to the REST API for posts, users, terms...
import { store as coreStore } from '@wordpress/core-data';

/**
 * WordPress calls Edit with the block's current attributes and a setter.
 *
 * @param {Object}   props
 * @param {Object}   props.attributes    Values defined in block.json "attributes".
 * @param {Function} props.setAttributes Saves new values. Like React's setState,
 *                                       but stored in the block (and the post).
 */
export default function Edit( { attributes, setAttributes } ) {
	const { postId, showImage } = attributes;

	// Fetch the chosen post so the editor sees its title as a preview.
	// - getEntityRecord() fires a REST request the first time and caches the result
	//   in the store, so other blocks asking for the same post don't refetch.
	// - It returns undefined while loading, then the post object.
	// - [ postId ] is the dependency list: the selector re-runs only when postId changes.
	const post = useSelect(
		( select ) =>
			postId
				? select( coreStore ).getEntityRecord(
						'postType',
						'post',
						postId
					)
				: null,
		[ postId ]
	);

	// Input fields give strings; the attribute is declared as "integer" in
	// block.json. Convert here, and clear it (undefined) when the field is emptied,
	// so we never save NaN or "" into a number attribute.
	const onChangePostId = ( value ) => {
		const id = parseInt( value, 10 );
		setAttributes( { postId: Number.isNaN( id ) ? undefined : id } );
	};

	// Defined once, used in two places: the sidebar and the empty-state placeholder.
	// The __next* props opt in to the current component styles (they silence
	// deprecation warnings and will become the default).
	const idControl = (
		<TextControl
			__next40pxDefaultSize
			__nextHasNoMarginBottom
			label={ __( 'Post ID', 'news-uk' ) }
			type="number"
			value={ postId ?? '' }
			onChange={ onChangePostId }
		/>
	);

	return (
		/*
		 * Why useBlockProps() is needed:
		 * The block editor has to be able to find, select and style this block.
		 * useBlockProps() returns the props that make that work, and they must be
		 * spread on the block's OUTERMOST element:
		 * - className "wp-block-news-uk-article-teaser" (generated from the block name)
		 *   plus classes from block supports, e.g. "has-accent-background-color",
		 * - inline styles from supports (padding, colours) so the editor preview
		 *   matches the front end,
		 * - the data-block id, the "Block: Article Teaser" accessibility label,
		 *   focus handling and the ref the editor uses to select the block.
		 * With apiVersion 3 (block.json) the editor does NOT add a wrapper for you,
		 * so without this the block can't be clicked/selected and supports don't apply.
		 * On the PHP side, get_block_wrapper_attributes() in render.php is the twin
		 * of this: same classes and styles on the front end.
		 */
		<div { ...useBlockProps() }>
			{ /* Sidebar settings (Settings tab in the block inspector). */ }
			<InspectorControls>
				<PanelBody title={ __( 'Settings', 'news-uk' ) }>
					{ idControl }
					<ToggleControl
						__nextHasNoMarginBottom
						label={ __( 'Show image', 'news-uk' ) }
						checked={ !! showImage }
						onChange={ ( value ) =>
							setAttributes( { showImage: value } )
						}
					/>
				</PanelBody>
			</InspectorControls>

			{ /* Empty state: no post chosen yet, so ask for one in the block itself. */ }
			{ ! postId && (
				<Placeholder
					label={ __( 'Article Teaser', 'news-uk' ) }
					instructions={ __(
						'Enter the ID of the post to link to.',
						'news-uk'
					) }
				>
					{ idControl }
				</Placeholder>
			) }

			{ /* A post is chosen but the REST request hasn't returned yet. */ }
			{ postId && ! post && <p>{ __( 'Loading…', 'news-uk' ) }</p> }

			{ /*
			 * Preview of the title. React escapes text automatically, so no
			 * escaping is needed here (unlike render.php, where we must escape).
			 * title.raw exists when the user can edit the post; otherwise fall
			 * back to the rendered title.
			 */ }
			{ post && (
				<p className="news-uk-article-teaser__title">
					{ post.title?.raw || post.title?.rendered }
				</p>
			) }
		</div>
	);
}
render.php: escape everything, return nothing for bad input plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q01-article-teaser/render.php
<?php
/**
 * Server-side render for news-uk/article-teaser (Q1, step 4 "dynamic"; Q2).
 *
 * WordPress includes this file every time the block appears on a page, with
 * three variables already set:
 *
 * @var array    $attributes Block attributes (postId, showImage, plus any
 *                           supports values such as colours and padding).
 * @var string   $content    Inner content. Unused: this block has no InnerBlocks.
 * @var WP_Block $block      The block instance (gives context, e.g. the current postId).
 *
 * Whatever this file prints becomes the block's HTML. Printing nothing removes
 * the block from the page.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

// Variables here are global-scope, so they get a prefix (news_uk_) to avoid
// clashing with other code. PHPCS (WordPress rules) enforces this (Q9).
$news_uk_post = empty( $attributes['postId'] ) ? null : get_post( (int) $attributes['postId'] );

// Refuse bad input (what Q11's tests check): missing, deleted, draft, private
// or trashed posts render nothing, so readers never see a broken or secret link.
if ( ! $news_uk_post || 'publish' !== $news_uk_post->post_status ) {
	return;
}
?>
<?php
/*
 * get_block_wrapper_attributes() is the PHP twin of useBlockProps() in edit.js.
 * It outputs class="wp-block-news-uk-article-teaser ..." plus the classes and
 * inline styles from block supports (colour, padding, font size), so the front
 * end matches the editor. The extra 'class' is merged in. Its output is
 * already escaped by core, hence the phpcs:ignore.
 */
?>
<div <?php echo get_block_wrapper_attributes( [ 'class' => 'news-uk-article-teaser' ] ); // phpcs:ignore WordPress.Security.EscapeOutput.OutputNotEscaped -- Escaped by core. ?>>
	<?php if ( ! empty( $attributes['showImage'] ) && has_post_thumbnail( $news_uk_post ) ) : ?>
		<?php
		// Core builds a safe <img> with srcset/sizes (responsive images, Q18).
		// loading="lazy": teasers are rarely the first thing on screen.
		echo get_the_post_thumbnail( $news_uk_post, 'thumbnail', [ 'loading' => 'lazy' ] );
		?>
	<?php endif; ?>
	<?php
	/*
	 * Escape late, at the point of output (Q1 step 7, Q9):
	 * - esc_url() for URLs in attributes,
	 * - esc_html() for text. A title like "<script>" is shown as text, not run.
	 * The title and link are read now, so a headline edit shows up on every page
	 * that uses this teaser without anyone re-saving those pages.
	 */
	?>
	<a class="news-uk-article-teaser__link" href="<?php echo esc_url( get_permalink( $news_uk_post ) ); ?>">
		<?php echo esc_html( get_the_title( $news_uk_post ) ); ?>
	</a>
</div>
index.js: what each block.json key means plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q01-article-teaser/index.js
/**
 * Q1: the editor-side entry point for the Article Teaser block.
 *
 * block.json is the single source of truth (Q1, step 2). JSON can't hold
 * comments, so here is what each key in ./block.json means:
 * - "apiVersion": 3     Current block API. The editor canvas runs in an iframe
 *                       and the block must use useBlockProps() itself.
 * - "name"              "namespace/block-name". Stored in post content as
 *                       <!-- wp:news-uk/article-teaser {...} /-->. Never rename.
 * - "attributes"        The block's data and its types. Saved in that comment.
 * - "supports"          Features WordPress adds for free (Q1, step 5): here
 *                       colours, padding and font size, using theme.json tokens.
 *                       "html": false hides "Edit as HTML" (no static HTML).
 * - "textdomain"        For translations (__() in JS, __() in PHP).
 * - "editorScript"      This file, built to build/q01-article-teaser/index.js.
 * - "style"             CSS loaded on the front end AND in the editor, and only
 *                       on pages that contain the block.
 * - "render"            PHP file that builds the front-end HTML: a dynamic block.
 *
 * The same block.json is read by PHP (register_block_type in load.php) and by
 * JS (imported below), so names and attributes are never defined twice.
 */
import { registerBlockType } from '@wordpress/blocks';
import metadata from './block.json';
import Edit from './edit';

// Importing the stylesheet tells the build (wp-scripts) to compile style.scss
// into style-index.css, the file block.json "style" points to.
import './style.scss';

registerBlockType( metadata.name, {
	edit: Edit,
	// Dynamic block (Q2): save() returns null, so post_content stores only the
	// block comment with its attributes, and render.php builds the HTML on every
	// request. Changing the markup later needs no deprecations (Q3).
	save: () => null,
} );
block.json: the single source of truth plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q01-article-teaser/block.json
{
	"$schema": "https://schemas.wp.org/trunk/block.json",
	"apiVersion": 3,
	"name": "news-uk/article-teaser",
	"title": "Article Teaser",
	"category": "widgets",
	"description": "Links to another article. Rendered on the server so it always shows the current title.",
	"attributes": {
		"postId": { "type": "integer" },
		"showImage": { "type": "boolean", "default": false }
	},
	"supports": {
		"html": false,
		"color": { "background": true, "text": true },
		"spacing": { "padding": true },
		"typography": { "fontSize": true }
	},
	"textdomain": "news-uk",
	"editorScript": "file:./index.js",
	"style": "file:./style-index.css",
	"render": "file:./render.php"
}
load.php: register from the build folder plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q01-article-teaser/load.php
<?php
/**
 * Q1, step 3 "Register it in PHP".
 *
 * The register_block_type() call reads build/q01-article-teaser/block.json and, from it:
 * - registers the block on the server (needed for render.php and REST),
 * - registers editorScript and style, and enqueues them only where needed,
 * - wires "render": "file:./render.php" as the render callback.
 *
 * It points at build/, not src/: build/ holds the compiled JS/CSS that
 * `npm run build` produces, plus a copy of block.json and render.php.
 *
 * Why in a plugin and not the theme: content must survive a theme change.
 * If the block lived in the theme, switching themes would break every post
 * that uses it.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

namespace News_UK_Practice\Q01;

// Blocks must be registered on 'init' (not earlier), so translations, post
// types and the block registry are ready.
add_action(
	'init',
	function () {
		\News_UK_Practice\register_block( 'q01-article-teaser' );
	}
);

How it's tested: PHPUnit in q11-dynamic-block-test, Jest + RTL in q22-rtl-edit-test, Playwright in q23-playwright. All pass.

Q2.When do you choose a static block and when a dynamic block? What are the trade-offs?

"Static if the content is final when the editor clicks Save. Dynamic if it depends on live data or the markup might change later. For News UK, anything data-driven would be dynamic by default."

Picture thisStatic is a printed newspaper: the words are fixed on the page the moment it's printed. Dynamic is a TV screen in a shop window: the frame stays the same, but what it shows is fetched fresh each time someone looks.
StaticDynamic
Where the HTML comes fromJS save() writes it into post_contentsave returns null; PHP (render.php) builds it on every request
Good forA styled callout, a quote box: content known at save timeLatest articles, author bio, paywall state, prices: anything that changes after publishing
SpeedVery fast, no PHP workCosts PHP per request, so cache queries in the object cache. The edge cache helps too.
Changing markup laterPainful: needs deprecations or every old post shows "invalid block"Easy: edit render.php and every post updates

Hybrid: a dynamic block with InnerBlocks saves only <InnerBlocks.Content />. render.php receives that as $content and wraps it. For example, a "Live Blog Entry" where the editor writes the paragraphs and PHP adds the live timestamp and wrapper.

Example"A 'Related Articles' block on The Times. If it were static, the three links would be frozen. When one of those articles is unpublished, you'd have a dead link in thousands of posts. As a dynamic block it always shows current, published articles."
Remember it as"Will it still be true tomorrow?" If yes, static. If not sure, dynamic. And never an unclear mix: a block either has a real save or it's dynamic.
Your storyYour product/affiliate blocks: prices and links change after publishing, so they render from live, cached data.
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q02-static-vs-dynamic/

One of each, side by side. Feature Card is static: save() writes its HTML into post_content. Live Blog Entry is the hybrid: save() stores only the inner paragraphs, and render.php wraps them with a live <time> on every request.

Static: feature-card/index.js (save writes the HTML) plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q02-static-vs-dynamic/feature-card/index.js
/**
 * Q2, STATIC example: Feature Card, a box that holds other blocks.
 *
 * Static means save() below produces the final HTML when the editor clicks
 * Save, and that HTML is stored in post_content. On the front end WordPress
 * just prints it: no PHP runs for this block (fast, Q2 table "Speed").
 * The price: if save() changes later, old posts no longer match and need a
 * deprecation (Q3).
 *
 * It is also the practice plan's "one block with InnerBlocks".
 */
import { registerBlockType } from '@wordpress/blocks';
import { useBlockProps, useInnerBlocksProps } from '@wordpress/block-editor';
import metadata from './block.json';
import './style.scss';

// Only these blocks can go inside the card: keeps the design intact
// (the "locked design" reason in Q8).
const ALLOWED_BLOCKS = [ 'core/heading', 'core/paragraph', 'core/image' ];

// What a new card starts with: a heading and a paragraph, each with a hint.
const TEMPLATE = [
	[ 'core/heading', { level: 3, placeholder: 'Card heading' } ],
	[ 'core/paragraph', { placeholder: 'Card text' } ],
];

function Edit() {
	// useInnerBlocksProps merges two sets of props onto ONE element:
	// - useBlockProps(): makes this element the block's selectable wrapper
	//   (classes, supports styles, editor wiring; see q01 edit.js),
	// - the inner-blocks props: makes the same element the drop zone where
	//   child blocks are rendered and edited.
	// Using one element (instead of a wrapper div + an <InnerBlocks /> div)
	// keeps the markup flat, so CSS like gap/flex applies to the children.
	const innerBlocksProps = useInnerBlocksProps(
		useBlockProps( { className: 'news-uk-feature-card' } ),
		{ allowedBlocks: ALLOWED_BLOCKS, template: TEMPLATE }
	);
	return <div { ...innerBlocksProps } />;
}

// The static part: this exact markup (plus the children's saved HTML) is
// written into post_content. The .save() variants are the save-time versions
// of the same hooks: they output the classes/styles and the children's HTML.
function save() {
	return (
		<div
			{ ...useInnerBlocksProps.save(
				useBlockProps.save( { className: 'news-uk-feature-card' } )
			) }
		/>
	);
}

registerBlockType( metadata.name, { edit: Edit, save } );
Hybrid: live-entry/index.js saves only InnerBlocks plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q02-static-vs-dynamic/live-entry/index.js
/**
 * Q2, HYBRID example: Live Blog Entry, a dynamic block with InnerBlocks.
 *
 * - Editors write normal paragraphs inside it (InnerBlocks).
 * - save() stores ONLY those inner blocks: <InnerBlocks.Content />.
 * - render.php gets them as $content and wraps them with the live parts
 *   (the <article> wrapper and a <time>), on every request.
 *
 * So the editor's words are saved like a static block, while the wrapper
 * can change any time without deprecations.
 */
import { registerBlockType } from '@wordpress/blocks';
import {
	useBlockProps,
	useInnerBlocksProps,
	InnerBlocks,
} from '@wordpress/block-editor';
import { useEffect } from '@wordpress/element';
import { __ } from '@wordpress/i18n';
import metadata from './block.json';

// A new entry starts with one empty paragraph.
const TEMPLATE = [
	[ 'core/paragraph', { placeholder: 'What just happened?' } ],
];

function Edit( { attributes, setAttributes } ) {
	const { timestamp } = attributes;

	// Stamp the entry once, when it is first inserted, so its time doesn't
	// change every time the post is edited. The deps list names everything
	// the effect reads (Q20: useEffect dependencies).
	useEffect( () => {
		if ( ! timestamp ) {
			setAttributes( { timestamp: Math.floor( Date.now() / 1000 ) } );
		}
	}, [ timestamp, setAttributes ] );

	// Same pattern as the Feature Card: one element is both the block wrapper
	// (useBlockProps) and the place the paragraphs go (useInnerBlocksProps).
	const innerBlocksProps = useInnerBlocksProps(
		useBlockProps( { className: 'news-uk-live-entry' } ),
		{ template: TEMPLATE }
	);

	return (
		<div { ...innerBlocksProps }>
			{ /* Editor-only preview of the time. The real <time> is from render.php. */ }
			<p className="news-uk-live-entry__time">
				{ timestamp
					? new Date( timestamp * 1000 ).toLocaleTimeString()
					: __( 'Now', 'news-uk' ) }
			</p>
			{ /* We added our own child above, so render the inner blocks explicitly. */ }
			{ innerBlocksProps.children }
		</div>
	);
}

registerBlockType( metadata.name, {
	edit: Edit,
	// Hybrid: save only the inner blocks' HTML. render.php receives it as $content.
	save: () => <InnerBlocks.Content />,
} );
Hybrid: live-entry/render.php receives them as $content plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q02-static-vs-dynamic/live-entry/render.php
<?php
/**
 * Server-side render for news-uk/live-entry. The editor's paragraphs arrive
 * as $content; PHP adds the wrapper and a machine-readable timestamp.
 *
 * @var array    $attributes Block attributes.
 * @var string   $content    Saved inner blocks HTML.
 * @var WP_Block $block      Block instance.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

// No text written yet: render nothing rather than an empty box with a time.
if ( '' === trim( $content ) ) {
	return;
}

// Use the time stamped when the entry was inserted; fall back to the post's
// publish time for entries created before the attribute existed.
$news_uk_time = empty( $attributes['timestamp'] ) ? get_post_time( 'U', true ) : (int) $attributes['timestamp'];
?>
<?php
/*
 * The wrapper and <time> are built here, at request time, so their markup can
 * change in a deploy without touching saved content.
 * - datetime="" holds a machine-readable ISO 8601 time (for SEO and scripts),
 * - the visible text uses the site's time format and timezone (wp_date).
 * $content is the inner paragraphs, already rendered and escaped by their
 * own blocks, so it is printed as is.
 */
?>
<article <?php echo get_block_wrapper_attributes( [ 'class' => 'news-uk-live-entry' ] ); // phpcs:ignore WordPress.Security.EscapeOutput.OutputNotEscaped -- Escaped by core. ?>>
	<time class="news-uk-live-entry__time" datetime="<?php echo esc_attr( gmdate( 'c', $news_uk_time ) ); ?>">
		<?php echo esc_html( wp_date( get_option( 'time_format' ), $news_uk_time ) ); ?>
	</time>
	<?php echo $content; // phpcs:ignore WordPress.Security.EscapeOutput.OutputNotEscaped -- Already-rendered inner blocks. ?>
</article>
PHPUnit test plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q02-static-vs-dynamic/tests/class-live-entry-test.php
<?php
/**
 * Q2: the hybrid block keeps the editor's paragraphs and adds the wrapper.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

namespace News_UK_Practice\Tests;

use WP_UnitTestCase;

/**
 * Live Blog Entry tests.
 */
class Live_Entry_Test extends WP_UnitTestCase {

	/**
	 * Inner paragraphs are wrapped in an article with a <time>.
	 */
	public function test_wraps_inner_blocks_with_timestamp() {
		$html = do_blocks(
			'<!-- wp:news-uk/live-entry {"timestamp":1767225600} --><!-- wp:paragraph --><p>Polls have closed.</p><!-- /wp:paragraph --><!-- /wp:news-uk/live-entry -->'
		);

		$this->assertStringContainsString( '<article', $html );
		$this->assertStringContainsString( '<p>Polls have closed.</p>', $html );
		$this->assertStringContainsString( 'datetime="2026-01-01T00:00:00+00:00"', $html );
	}

	/**
	 * An entry with no text renders nothing.
	 */
	public function test_empty_entry_renders_nothing() {
		$this->assertSame( '', trim( do_blocks( '<!-- wp:news-uk/live-entry --><!-- /wp:news-uk/live-entry -->' ) ) );
	}
}

How it's tested: tests/class-live-entry-test.php checks the wrapper, the paragraphs and the timestamp.

Q3.What are block deprecations? Walk us through changing the markup of a static block safely.

"A deprecation is a record of how a block used to be saved, so the editor can recognise old content and upgrade it instead of calling it broken."

Picture thisThe editor is a strict checker. When it opens a post it re-runs today's save() and compares the result with what's stored, like comparing a signature. If you changed save(), old posts no longer match and you get "This block contains unexpected or invalid content." Deprecations are like keeping your old signatures on file: "if it looks like this, it's still me, here's how to update it."

How to change markup safely:

  1. Copy the current save, attributes and supports into a new entry at the top of the deprecated array. It's a frozen snapshot: paste the old code, never import the live function.
  2. Change the real save to the new markup.
  3. If the data shape changed (for example url renamed to href), add migrate( attributes, innerBlocks ). Use isEligible() if you need to force an upgrade on blocks that still validate.
  4. Add a Jest test: parse the old markup and assert the block is valid and migrated.
const v1 = {
  attributes: { url: { type: 'string' } },
  save: ( { attributes } ) => <a className="cta" href={ attributes.url }>Read</a>,
  migrate: ( { url } ) => ( { href: url } ),
};
registerBlockType( 'md/cta', { ...metadata, edit, save, deprecated: [ v1 ] } );

Gotcha: posts only upgrade when someone opens and saves them. Until then the front end still serves the old HTML, so your CSS has to support both versions for a while.

Remember it as"Freeze, Change, Migrate, Test." Newest deprecation first. If the markup keeps changing, go dynamic and the problem disappears.
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q03-deprecations/

A static CTA block whose v1 saved <a class="cta" href=url>Read</a>. v2 renamed url to href and made the text editable. The test first serializes content with the old v1 code, then checks that the current block opens it as valid and migrates the data. That proves old posts won't show "invalid content".

deprecated.js: frozen snapshot + migrate() plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q03-deprecations/deprecated.js
/**
 * Frozen snapshots of how this block used to be saved. Newest first.
 * Never import the live save() here: these must not change.
 */
const v1 = {
	attributes: {
		url: { type: 'string' },
	},
	save( { attributes } ) {
		return (
			<a className="cta" href={ attributes.url }>
				Read
			</a>
		);
	},
	// The data shape changed: url became href, and the text is now stored.
	migrate( { url } ) {
		return { href: url, text: 'Read' };
	},
};

export default [ v1 ];
settings.js: the current (v2) save() plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q03-deprecations/settings.js
// v2 markup: renamed url -> href, editable text, new class.
// Changing this again means FIRST copying this version (attributes + save)
// into the top of deprecated.js, then editing it ("Freeze, Change, Migrate, Test").
function save( { attributes } ) {
	return (
		<a
			{ ...useBlockProps.save( { className: 'news-uk-cta__link' } ) }
			href={ attributes.href }
		>
			{ attributes.text }
		</a>
	);
}

// deprecated: the list of old versions the editor tries when today's save()
// doesn't match the stored HTML. See deprecated.js.
export const settings = { ...metadata, edit: Edit, save, deprecated };
Jest: old content is upgraded, not broken plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q03-deprecations/test/deprecated.test.js
/**
 * Q3, step 4 "Add a Jest test": prove old content is upgraded, not broken.
 *
 * Instead of pasting old HTML by hand, the test REGISTERS v1 and serializes a
 * block with it, so the fixture is exactly what v1 produced. Then it registers
 * the real (v2) block and parses that HTML like the editor would when an old
 * post is opened.
 */

/**
 * WordPress dependencies
 */
import {
	registerBlockType,
	unregisterBlockType,
	createBlock,
	serialize,
	parse,
} from '@wordpress/blocks';

/**
 * Internal dependencies
 */
import { settings } from '../settings';
import deprecated from '../deprecated';

const NAME = 'news-uk/cta';

// Produce content exactly as v1 of the plugin saved it: register a block that
// has only v1's attributes and save(), serialize, then unregister it again.
function savedByV1( attributes ) {
	const [ v1 ] = deprecated;
	registerBlockType( NAME, {
		apiVersion: 3,
		title: 'CTA v1',
		category: 'design',
		attributes: v1.attributes,
		save: v1.save,
	} );
	const html = serialize( createBlock( NAME, attributes ) );
	unregisterBlockType( NAME );
	return html;
}

describe( 'CTA deprecations', () => {
	afterEach( () => unregisterBlockType( NAME ) );

	// The core promise: an old post opens cleanly and its data is migrated
	// (url -> href) by migrate() in deprecated.js.
	it( 'upgrades v1 content instead of marking it invalid', () => {
		const oldHtml = savedByV1( { url: 'https://example.com/story' } );
		expect( oldHtml ).toContain(
			'<a class="cta" href="https://example.com/story">Read</a>'
		);

		registerBlockType( NAME, settings );
		const [ block ] = parse( oldHtml );

		expect( block.isValid ).toBe( true );
		expect( block.attributes ).toEqual( {
			href: 'https://example.com/story',
			text: 'Read',
		} );
		// The editor logs each successful upgrade.
		expect( console ).toHaveInformed();
	} );

	// After the editor upgrades it, the next save writes v2 markup.
	it( 'saves the new markup once upgraded', () => {
		const oldHtml = savedByV1( { url: 'https://example.com/story' } );
		registerBlockType( NAME, settings );
		const [ block ] = parse( oldHtml );

		expect( serialize( block ) ).toContain(
			'<a class="news-uk-cta__link" href="https://example.com/story">Read</a>'
		);
		expect( console ).toHaveInformed();
	} );

	// Guard against the opposite bug: v2 content must NOT be run through migrate().
	it( 'parses current content as valid without migrating', () => {
		registerBlockType( NAME, settings );
		const html = serialize(
			createBlock( NAME, { href: '/x', text: 'Go' } )
		);
		const [ block ] = parse( html );

		expect( block.isValid ).toBe( true );
		expect( block.attributes ).toEqual( { href: '/x', text: 'Go' } );
	} );
} );

How it's tested: 3 passing: the upgrade is valid, the new markup is saved, and current content isn't re-migrated.

✓ Builds with wp-scripts✓ Jest passes

Q4.What is the Block Bindings API, and how does it replace ACF-style custom fields?

"Block Bindings (WordPress 6.5+) let a core block, like a Heading or Image, take its value from a data source such as post meta, so you get custom fields without ACF and without writing a custom block."

Picture thisA mail-merge letter. The letter (template) has a placeholder that says "insert Dear {first name}". You write the letter once, and the value is filled in from the spreadsheet (post meta) for each recipient. Block Bindings plug a core block's attribute into a data field the same way.
  • What can be bound: paragraph and heading content, image url/alt/title, button url and text.
  • Built-in sources: core/post-meta (registered post meta) and core/pattern-overrides.
  • 6.7+: editors can edit bound meta right inside the block, and there's a UI to connect bindings.
  • Custom sources: register_block_bindings_source() with a get_value_callback, e.g. a byline built from author data, or a cached value from an external service.
// 1. Register the data properly (typed, sanitised, in REST, permission-checked)
register_post_meta( 'post', 'standfirst', [
  'type' => 'string', 'single' => true, 'show_in_rest' => true,
  'sanitize_callback' => 'sanitize_text_field',
  'auth_callback' => fn() => current_user_can( 'edit_posts' ),
] );

<!-- 2. Bind a plain core paragraph to it in the template -->
<!-- wp:paragraph {"metadata":{"bindings":{"content":{"source":"core/post-meta","args":{"key":"standfirst"}}}}} /-->
Example"A news article has a 'standfirst' (the summary under the headline). With ACF you'd add a field group and a custom template. With bindings I register standfirst as post meta and bind a normal Paragraph block to it in the single-post template. No custom block, the data is in REST, and editors type it straight into the page."
Remember it as"Register the meta, bind the block." Register it, bind it, done: fewer custom blocks, cleaner content, data in REST.
Your storyLink it to your screening answer: you build Gutenberg solutions in React without ACF. Multidots and News UK both expect post meta + bindings.
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q04-block-bindings/

The same standfirst field, built three ways, so you can compare them directly:

  1. ACF way A: an ACF field group, plus a dynamic block whose render calls get_field().
  2. ACF way B: a core Paragraph bound to ACF's own acf/field bindings source (ACF 6.3+).
  3. Native way (what the question means): register_post_meta() plus a core Paragraph bound with core/post-meta. No plugin and no custom block, and the data is in REST.
There's also a custom bindings source (news-uk/byline) for a value that is computed rather than stored. In the theme, templates/single.html uses the native way. A custom template, "Q4: ACF vs Block Bindings" (templates/q04-acf.html), shows all three on one page. Pick it in the post's Template panel.

ACF way: field group in code (acf.php) plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q04-block-bindings/acf.php
function register_acf_fields() {
	// ACF is a plugin: if it is deactivated, do nothing instead of fatal-erroring.
	if ( ! function_exists( 'acf_add_local_field_group' ) ) {
		return;
	}

	// The PHP equivalent of building a field group in the ACF admin screen.
	// In code it is versioned, reviewed and deployed like everything else;
	// field groups clicked together in the admin live only in one database.
	acf_add_local_field_group(
		[
			'key'          => 'group_news_uk_standfirst',
			'title'        => 'Standfirst (ACF)',
			'show_in_rest' => true,
			'fields'       => [
				[
					'key'               => 'field_news_uk_acf_standfirst',
					'label'             => 'Standfirst (ACF)',
					'name'              => 'acf_standfirst',
					'type'              => 'text',
					'allow_in_bindings' => 1, // Needed for the acf/field bindings source.
				],
			],
			// "Show this group on posts". The outer array is OR, the inner is AND.
			'location'     => [
				[
					[
						'param'    => 'post_type',
						'operator' => '==',
						'value'    => 'post',
					],
				],
			],
		]
	);
}
add_action( 'acf/init', __NAMESPACE__ . '\register_acf_fields' );
ACF way A: block render calls get_field() plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q04-block-bindings/acf-block/render.php
<?php
/**
 * Server-side render for news-uk/acf-standfirst (Q4, "ACF way A").
 *
 * A plain dynamic block whose render calls ACF's get_field(). This is how
 * sites on the FREE ACF plugin show a field in a block theme.
 *
 * (ACF PRO also has "ACF Blocks": block.json with an "acf" key and a
 * renderTemplate, no React needed. That feature is PRO-only: with free ACF
 * such a block renders nothing, which is why this one uses core's "render".)
 *
 * Compare with the native way, which needs no custom block at all: a core
 * Paragraph bound to registered post meta.
 *
 * @var array    $attributes Block attributes (unused).
 * @var string   $content    Inner content (unused).
 * @var WP_Block $block      Block instance.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

// block.json "usesContext": [ "postId" ] gives the post being shown
// (the current article in the single template).
$news_uk_post_id = $block->context['postId'] ?? get_the_ID();

// get_field() is ACF's reader. It looks up the field by name for this post
// (ACF stores it as ordinary post meta, 'acf_standfirst', plus a hidden
// '_acf_standfirst' row pointing at the field key).
$news_uk_standfirst = function_exists( 'get_field' ) ? get_field( 'acf_standfirst', $news_uk_post_id ) : '';

// Empty field (or ACF inactive): output nothing rather than an empty <p>.
if ( ! $news_uk_standfirst ) {
	return;
}
?>
<p <?php echo get_block_wrapper_attributes( [ 'class' => 'news-uk-standfirst news-uk-standfirst--acf' ] ); // phpcs:ignore WordPress.Security.EscapeOutput.OutputNotEscaped -- Escaped by core. ?>>
	<?php echo esc_html( $news_uk_standfirst ); ?>
</p>
ACF way A: editor registration (placeholder in the Site Editor) plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q04-block-bindings/acf-block/index.js
/**
 * Q4, "ACF way A": editor side of the get_field() block.
 *
 * Every block needs a JS registration, even a server-rendered one. Without
 * it, PHP knows the block (so the front end works) but the editor doesn't,
 * and shows "Your site doesn't include support for this block".
 *
 * In the Site Editor a template is not tied to one post, so there is no
 * get_field() value to show. Display a labelled placeholder instead, the same
 * way core shows bound paragraphs ("Add Standfirst") in templates.
 */
import { registerBlockType } from '@wordpress/blocks';
import { useBlockProps } from '@wordpress/block-editor';
import { __ } from '@wordpress/i18n';
import metadata from './block.json';

function Edit() {
	return (
		// useBlockProps: the block's selectable wrapper (see q01 edit.js).
		<p
			{ ...useBlockProps( {
				className: 'news-uk-standfirst news-uk-standfirst--acf',
			} ) }
		>
			{ __( 'ACF standfirst (get_field)', 'news-uk' ) }
		</p>
	);
}

// Dynamic: render.php reads the field for the post being viewed.
registerBlockType( metadata.name, { edit: Edit, save: () => null } );
Native way, step 1: register the meta (native.php) plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q04-block-bindings/native.php
function register_meta() {
	register_post_meta(
		'post',
		'news_uk_standfirst',
		[
			'type'              => 'string',  // Typed: REST validates it, and get_post_meta returns a string.
			'single'            => true,      // One value per post (not a list).
			'show_in_rest'      => true,      // Required for bindings, the editor, and headless clients (Q24).
			'default'           => '',        // What get_post_meta() returns when nothing is saved.
			'sanitize_callback' => 'sanitize_text_field', // Clean on the way IN: strips tags and line breaks.
			// Who may change it via REST/the editor: only people who can edit this post.
			'auth_callback'     => fn( $allowed, $meta_key, $post_id ) => current_user_can( 'edit_post', $post_id ),
			'label'             => __( 'Standfirst', 'news-uk' ), // Shown in the bindings UI (6.7+).
		]
	);
}
add_action( 'init', __NAMESPACE__ . '\register_meta' );
Native way, step 2: bind core paragraphs in the template (single.html) themes/news-uk-theme/templates/single.html

<p class="news-uk-byline has-small-font-size"></p>
<!-- /wp:paragraph -->

Custom source: news-uk/byline plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q04-block-bindings/native.php
function register_byline_source() {
	// A source is "where a bound value comes from". core/post-meta is one;
	// this adds our own. Any block attribute that supports bindings can use it.
	register_block_bindings_source(
		'news-uk/byline',
		[
			'label'              => __( 'Byline (author and reading time)', 'news-uk' ),
			'get_value_callback' => __NAMESPACE__ . '\get_byline',
			// Ask for the postId context, so the callback knows WHICH post it is
			// rendering for (in a template, the post being viewed).
			'uses_context'       => [ 'postId' ],
		]
	);
}
add_action( 'init', __NAMESPACE__ . '\register_byline_source' );

/**
 * Bindings callback: "By Jane Smith · 4 min read".
 *
 * @param array     $source_args    Arguments from the block markup (unused).
 * @param \WP_Block $block_instance The bound block.
 * @return string|null Null tells core to keep the block's own content.
 */
function get_byline( array $source_args, $block_instance ) {
	$post = get_post( $block_instance->context['postId'] ?? 0 );

	if ( ! $post ) {
		return null;
	}

	return sprintf(
		/* translators: 1: author name, 2: minutes */
		__( 'By %1$s · %2$d min read', 'news-uk' ),
		get_the_author_meta( 'display_name', $post->post_author ),
		reading_time_minutes( $post->post_content )
	);
}

PHPUnit: meta, binding, custom source, both ACF ways plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q04-block-bindings/tests/class-block-bindings-test.php
<?php
/**
 * Q4: post meta bindings, the custom byline source, and the ACF comparison.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

namespace News_UK_Practice\Tests;

use WP_UnitTestCase;

/**
 * Block Bindings tests.
 */
class Block_Bindings_Test extends WP_UnitTestCase {

	/**
	 * Re-register the meta before each test.
	 *
	 * Gotcha: WP_UnitTestCase::tear_down() calls unregister_all_meta_keys()
	 * after EVERY test, so meta registered on 'init' only exists for the first
	 * test in the whole run. Tests that rely on registered meta (REST exposure,
	 * sanitising, bindings) must register it again in set_up().
	 */
	public function set_up() {
		parent::set_up();
		\News_UK_Practice\Q04\register_meta();
	}

	/**
	 * Render block markup as if it sat in a template for $post_id.
	 *
	 * @param string $markup  Block markup.
	 * @param int    $post_id Post the template is showing.
	 * @return string
	 */
	private function render_for_post( $markup, $post_id ) {
		$block = new \WP_Block(
			parse_blocks( $markup )[0],
			[
				'postId'   => $post_id,
				'postType' => 'post',
			]
		);

		return $block->render();
	}

	/**
	 * The meta is registered for REST, which bindings and the editor need.
	 */
	public function test_standfirst_meta_is_in_rest() {
		$meta = get_registered_meta_keys( 'post', 'post' );

		$this->assertArrayHasKey( 'news_uk_standfirst', $meta );
		$this->assertTrue( (bool) $meta['news_uk_standfirst']['show_in_rest'] );
	}

	/**
	 * Input is sanitised on save.
	 */
	public function test_standfirst_is_sanitised() {
		$post_id = self::factory()->post->create();
		update_post_meta( $post_id, 'news_uk_standfirst', '<script>x</script>Rates held' );

		$this->assertSame( 'Rates held', get_post_meta( $post_id, 'news_uk_standfirst', true ) );
	}

	/**
	 * A core paragraph bound with core/post-meta shows the meta value.
	 */
	public function test_paragraph_binding_renders_standfirst_meta() {
		$post_id = self::factory()->post->create();
		update_post_meta( $post_id, 'news_uk_standfirst', 'Rates held at 4%' );

		$html = $this->render_for_post(
			'<!-- wp:paragraph {"metadata":{"bindings":{"content":{"source":"core/post-meta","args":{"key":"news_uk_standfirst"}}}}} --><p></p><!-- /wp:paragraph -->',
			$post_id
		);

		$this->assertStringContainsString( '<p>Rates held at 4%</p>', $html );
	}

	/**
	 * The custom source builds the byline from author and content.
	 */
	public function test_custom_byline_source() {
		$author  = self::factory()->user->create( [ 'display_name' => 'Jane Smith' ] );
		$post_id = self::factory()->post->create(
			[
				'post_author'  => $author,
				'post_content' => str_repeat( 'word ', 450 ),
			]
		);

		$html = $this->render_for_post(
			'<!-- wp:paragraph {"metadata":{"bindings":{"content":{"source":"news-uk/byline"}}}} --><p>fallback</p><!-- /wp:paragraph -->',
			$post_id
		);

		$this->assertStringContainsString( 'By Jane Smith · 3 min read', $html );
	}

	/**
	 * ACF way A: a dynamic block whose render calls get_field().
	 */
	public function test_acf_get_field_block_renders_value() {
		if ( ! function_exists( 'update_field' ) ) {
			$this->markTestSkipped( 'ACF is not active.' );
		}

		$post_id = self::factory()->post->create();
		update_field( 'acf_standfirst', 'Read by get_field()', $post_id );

		$html = $this->render_for_post( '<!-- wp:news-uk/acf-standfirst /-->', $post_id );

		$this->assertStringContainsString( 'Read by get_field()', $html );
		$this->assertStringContainsString( 'news-uk-standfirst--acf', $html );
	}

	/**
	 * ACF way B: the bound paragraph reads the ACF field through acf/field.
	 */
	public function test_acf_field_binding_renders_value() {
		if ( ! function_exists( 'update_field' ) ) {
			$this->markTestSkipped( 'ACF is not active.' );
		}

		$post_id = self::factory()->post->create();
		update_field( 'acf_standfirst', 'Set with ACF', $post_id );

		// Difference worth knowing: ACF's acf/field source ignores the block's
		// postId context and reads the GLOBAL post (acf_get_valid_post_id()).
		// core/post-meta uses block context instead. On a real page the global
		// post is set, so visit the post here like a real request would.
		$this->go_to( get_permalink( $post_id ) );

		$html = $this->render_for_post(
			'<!-- wp:paragraph {"metadata":{"bindings":{"content":{"source":"acf/field","args":{"key":"acf_standfirst"}}}}} --><p></p><!-- /wp:paragraph -->',
			$post_id
		);

		$this->assertStringContainsString( 'Set with ACF', $html );
		$this->assertSame( 'Set with ACF', get_post_meta( $post_id, 'acf_standfirst', true ), 'ACF also stores it as post meta.' );
	}
}
Playwright: front end + typing into a bound block plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q04-block-bindings/e2e/block-bindings.spec.js
/**
 * Q4: the single template's bound paragraphs show post meta and the custom
 * byline source on the front end.
 */

/**
 * WordPress dependencies
 */
const { test, expect } = require( '@wordpress/e2e-test-utils-playwright' );

test.describe( 'Q4 Block Bindings', () => {
	test.beforeAll( async ( { requestUtils } ) => {
		await requestUtils.activateTheme( 'news-uk-theme' );
	} );

	test.afterEach( async ( { requestUtils } ) => {
		await requestUtils.deleteAllPosts();
	} );

	test( 'standfirst meta and byline render in the single template', async ( {
		page,
		requestUtils,
	} ) => {
		const post = await requestUtils.createPost( {
			title: 'Rates decision',
			status: 'publish',
			content:
				'<!-- wp:paragraph --><p>Body text.</p><!-- /wp:paragraph -->',
			meta: { news_uk_standfirst: 'The Bank holds rates at 4%.' },
		} );

		await page.goto( `/?p=${ post.id }` );

		await expect( page.locator( '.news-uk-standfirst' ) ).toHaveText(
			'The Bank holds rates at 4%.'
		);
		await expect( page.locator( '.news-uk-byline' ) ).toHaveText(
			/^By .+ · 1 min read$/
		);
	} );

	test( 'editor edits bound meta by typing into the block', async ( {
		admin,
		editor,
		page,
		requestUtils,
	} ) => {
		const post = await requestUtils.createPost( {
			title: 'Editable binding',
			status: 'draft',
		} );
		// editPost() (unlike visitAdminPage) also turns off the editor's welcome
		// guide, whose modal would block clicks on a fresh install such as CI.
		await admin.editPost( post.id );

		// A core Paragraph bound to the meta, in the post content. (Bound blocks
		// in the TEMPLATE are read-only in the post editor: WordPress disables
		// template blocks there. Editing bound meta in place (6.7+) is for blocks
		// the editor can edit, like this one.)
		await editor.insertBlock( {
			name: 'core/paragraph',
			attributes: {
				metadata: {
					bindings: {
						content: {
							source: 'core/post-meta',
							args: { key: 'news_uk_standfirst' },
						},
					},
				},
			},
		} );

		// A bound block's accessible name isn't "Block: Paragraph", so locate it
		// by block type. It shows the meta's label ("Add Standfirst") as placeholder.
		const bound = editor.canvas.locator( '[data-type="core/paragraph"]' );
		await expect( bound ).toHaveAttribute( 'aria-label', /.+/ );
		await bound.click();
		await page.keyboard.type( 'Typed in the block' );
		await editor.saveDraft();

		// The text went into post meta, not into the paragraph's own content.
		const saved = await requestUtils.rest( {
			path: `/wp/v2/posts/${ post.id }`,
			params: { context: 'edit' },
		} );
		expect( saved.meta.news_uk_standfirst ).toBe( 'Typed in the block' );
		expect( saved.content.raw ).not.toContain( 'Typed in the block' );
	} );
} );

Trade-off in one line: ACF is quicker for complex UIs (repeaters, galleries) but adds a plugin and keeps field definitions in ACF. Native meta + bindings uses core only, puts the data in REST, and reuses core blocks. The ADR in Q26 records this decision.

Things the real runs taught (good interview material):

  • "ACF Blocks" are ACF PRO only. A block.json with an "acf" key renders nothing on free ACF, so way A uses a normal dynamic block that calls get_field().
  • Every block needs a JS registration, even a server-rendered one. The first version of way A had no editor script: the front end worked (PHP knew the block), but the Site Editor showed "Your site doesn't include support for the news-uk/acf-standfirst block". A small index.js fixes it. In a template there is no specific post, so it shows a labelled placeholder, just as core shows "Add Standfirst" for the bound paragraphs.
  • ACF's acf/field source reads the global post, not the block's postId context. core/post-meta uses block context, so it also works inside a Query Loop.
  • Bound blocks in the template are read-only in the post editor (WordPress disables template blocks there). Editing bound meta in place (6.7+) works for bound blocks in the post content: the Playwright test types into one and checks the text went to post meta, not post content.
  • WP_UnitTestCase unregisters all meta keys after every test, so the test re-registers the meta in set_up().

Seen running: Demo post on the "Q4: ACF vs Block Bindings" template, in Chrome: all three standfirsts render. ACF way A (get_field block), ACF way B (acf/field binding), and the native core/post-meta binding. In the Site Editor (Appearance → Editor → Templates → "Q4: ACF vs Block Bindings") the template opens with no block warnings: way A shows its placeholder and the bound paragraphs show their field labels.

How it's tested: PHPUnit: 6 tests (registration, sanitising, the bound paragraph, the byline source, both ACF ways). Playwright: front-end render, and editing bound meta by typing into the block.

✓ PHPUnit passes✓ Playwright passes (Chrome)✓ Checked in Chrome

Q5.How do you add front-end interactivity to a block without shipping React to readers?

"The Interactivity API: PHP renders normal HTML with a few data-wp-* attributes, and a tiny shared script brings it to life. Readers get fast, cacheable HTML and very little JavaScript."

Picture thisThe HTML is a light switch already wired into the wall. The data-wp-on--click attribute says "this switch controls that light". The small store is the fuse box. You don't ship a whole power station (React) to every reader just to flip one light.
<div data-wp-interactive="md/accordion" <?php echo wp_interactivity_data_wp_context( [ 'open' => false ] ); ?>>
  <button data-wp-on--click="actions.toggle" data-wp-bind--aria-expanded="context.open">Show more</button>
  <div data-wp-bind--hidden="!context.open">Hidden text</div>
</div>

// view.js (loaded via "viewScriptModule" in block.json)
import { store, getContext } from '@wordpress/interactivity';
store( 'md/accordion', { actions: { toggle() { const c = getContext(); c.open = ! c.open; } } } );
  • Why: the HTML is server-rendered, so it works with SEO and edge caching. The JS runtime is small and shared across all blocks. Initial state can be set in PHP with wp_interactivity_state().
  • Small one-offs: a plain viewScript with vanilla JS is fine. No jQuery in new code.
  • Accessibility: real <button>s, keep aria-expanded in sync, manage focus, test with the keyboard.
Remember it as"HTML first, sprinkle behaviour." Directives in markup, a small store in a script module.
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q05-interactivity/

An Accordion block, built twice so you can compare. Interactivity API version: render.php prints normal HTML with data-wp-* directives. WordPress processes them on the server, so the first HTML already has hidden and aria-expanded="false", and a 141-byte view.js script module only handles the click. Classic version (classic-accordion/): the same accordion done the pre-6.5 way, with a plain-JS viewScript that finds the markup and wires it up by hand. Both share one editor UI (edit.js). Store anatomy (save-for-later/): a "Save for later" block that uses every part of a store: shared state, getters (derived state, with matching PHP closures), a plain action, an async generator action, init and watch callbacks, context, getElement() and getConfig(). The follow-up questions M54–M61 in section 8 walk through it.

Interactivity: render.php, directives in server HTML plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q05-interactivity/render.php
<?php
/**
 * Server-side render for news-uk/accordion.
 *
 * Directives (data-wp-*) are processed on the server too, so the first HTML
 * already has the right hidden/aria-expanded values: works without JS for SEO
 * and edge caching, and does not flash.
 *
 * @var array    $attributes Block attributes.
 * @var string   $content    Inner content (unused).
 * @var WP_Block $block      Block instance.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

// Nothing to reveal: render nothing rather than a button that opens an empty panel.
if ( '' === trim( $attributes['body'] ?? '' ) ) {
	return;
}

// A unique id per accordion, so aria-controls points at the right panel even
// when there are several accordions on one page.
$news_uk_panel_id = wp_unique_id( 'news-uk-accordion-' );

/*
 * How the directives below work (Q5, "light switch wired into the wall"):
 *
 * data-wp-interactive="news-uk/accordion"
 *     "This element and its children are controlled by the store named
 *     news-uk/accordion" (the store() in view.js).
 *
 * wp_interactivity_data_wp_context( [ 'isOpen' => false ] )
 *     Prints data-wp-context='{"isOpen":false}': state that belongs to THIS
 *     accordion only, so two accordions on a page open independently.
 *
 * data-wp-on--click="actions.toggle"
 *     On click, run actions.toggle from the store (flips context.isOpen).
 *
 * data-wp-bind--aria-expanded="context.isOpen"
 * data-wp-bind--hidden="!context.isOpen"
 *     Keep these attributes in sync with context.isOpen. The runtime updates
 *     them whenever the value changes; no manual DOM code.
 *
 * WordPress also processes these directives in PHP before sending the page, so
 * the first HTML already says aria-expanded="false" and has "hidden" on the panel.
 * Readers without JS, search engines and the edge cache all get correct HTML.
 *
 * Accessibility: a real <button> (keyboard and screen reader for free),
 * aria-expanded kept in sync, aria-controls linking button and panel.
 */
?>
<div
	<?php echo get_block_wrapper_attributes(); // phpcs:ignore WordPress.Security.EscapeOutput.OutputNotEscaped -- Escaped by core. ?>
	data-wp-interactive="news-uk/accordion"
	<?php echo wp_interactivity_data_wp_context( [ 'isOpen' => false ] ); // phpcs:ignore WordPress.Security.EscapeOutput.OutputNotEscaped -- Escaped by core. ?>
>
	<button
		type="button"
		aria-controls="<?php echo esc_attr( $news_uk_panel_id ); ?>"
		data-wp-on--click="actions.toggle"
		data-wp-bind--aria-expanded="context.isOpen"
	>
		<?php echo esc_html( $attributes['label'] ); ?>
	</button>
	<div id="<?php echo esc_attr( $news_uk_panel_id ); ?>" data-wp-bind--hidden="!context.isOpen">
		<?php
		// body comes from a RichText field, so it may contain safe inline HTML
		// (bold, links). wp_kses_post() keeps that and strips anything unsafe.
		echo wp_kses_post( $attributes['body'] );
		?>
	</div>
</div>
Interactivity: view.js, the whole front-end script plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q05-interactivity/view.js
/**
 * Q5: the accordion's ENTIRE front-end JavaScript (about 140 bytes built).
 *
 * Loaded as a script module (block.json "viewScriptModule") only on pages that
 * contain the block. The markup already exists (render.php); this only says
 * what happens on click. The shared @wordpress/interactivity runtime is loaded
 * once per page and used by every interactive block, instead of each block
 * shipping its own React.
 */
import { store, getContext } from '@wordpress/interactivity';

// The store name must match data-wp-interactive="news-uk/accordion" in render.php.
store( 'news-uk/accordion', {
	actions: {
		// Called by data-wp-on--click="actions.toggle".
		toggle() {
			// getContext() returns the data-wp-context of the accordion that was
			// clicked (not a global), so each accordion keeps its own isOpen.
			const context = getContext();
			// The context is reactive: changing it makes the runtime update every
			// data-wp-bind--* that reads isOpen (aria-expanded, hidden).
			context.isOpen = ! context.isOpen;
		},
	},
} );
Classic: render.php, plain HTML hooks, open by default plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q05-interactivity/classic-accordion/render.php
<?php
/**
 * Server-side render for news-uk/accordion-classic (Q5, the pre-6.5 way).
 *
 * Progressive enhancement: the HTML works on its own (the panel is visible
 * and the button does nothing). view.js then hides the panel and wires up the
 * button. Compare with ../render.php, where data-wp-* directives describe the
 * behaviour in the markup itself and the server already renders the closed state.
 *
 * @var array    $attributes Block attributes.
 * @var string   $content    Inner content (unused).
 * @var WP_Block $block      Block instance.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

if ( '' === trim( $attributes['body'] ?? '' ) ) {
	return;
}

$news_uk_panel_id = wp_unique_id( 'news-uk-accordion-classic-' );
?>
<div <?php echo get_block_wrapper_attributes(); // phpcs:ignore WordPress.Security.EscapeOutput.OutputNotEscaped -- Escaped by core. ?>>
	<?php
	// Plain HTML hooks for the script to find: a class on the button, and
	// aria-controls pointing at the panel. No behaviour is described here.
	?>
	<button type="button" class="news-uk-accordion-classic__toggle" aria-expanded="true" aria-controls="<?php echo esc_attr( $news_uk_panel_id ); ?>">
		<?php echo esc_html( $attributes['label'] ); ?>
	</button>
	<div id="<?php echo esc_attr( $news_uk_panel_id ); ?>" class="news-uk-accordion-classic__panel">
		<?php echo wp_kses_post( $attributes['body'] ); ?>
	</div>
</div>
Classic: view.js, find, read, listen, sync by hand plugins/news-uk-practice/src/q05-interactivity/classic-accordion/view.js
/**
 * Q5, the pre-Interactivity-API way: a block "viewScript" in plain JS.
 *
 * block.json "viewScript" makes WordPress enqueue this file on the front end,
 * only on pages that contain the block (since WordPress 5.9). Before that,
 * themes and plugins used wp_enqueue_script() on every page, often with jQuery.
 *
 * The pattern: wait for the DOM, find every instance of the block, read the
 * state from the markup, attach listeners, and keep the DOM in sync by hand.
 * Compare with ../view.js, where the runtime does the finding and syncing.
 */

/**
 * Wire up every classic accordion inside `root`. Safe to call twice.
 *
 * @param {Document|Element} root Where to look (the whole document by default).
 */
export function initAccordions( root = document ) {
	root.querySelectorAll( '.news-uk-accordion-classic__toggle' ).forEach(
		( button ) => {
			// Avoid double listeners if called again (e.g. after AJAX inserts).
			if ( button.dataset.ready ) {
				return;
			}
			button.dataset.ready = 'true';

			const panel = root.querySelector(
				`#${ button.getAttribute( 'aria-controls' ) }`
			);
			if ( ! panel ) {
				return;
			}

			// The state lives in the DOM itself, so every change must update
			// both attributes by hand: aria-expanded and hidden.
			const setOpen = ( open ) => {
				button.setAttribute( 'aria-expanded', open ? 'true' : 'false' );
				panel.hidden = ! open;
			};

			// Server HTML is open (works without JS); close it once JS runs.
			// Readers may see a brief flash of the open panel before this.
			setOpen( false );

			button.addEventListener( 'click', () => {
				setOpen( button.getAttribute( 'aria-expanded' ) !== 'true' );
			} );
		}
	);
}

// Classic scripts load in the <head> or footer, so wait for the DOM if needed.
if ( document.readyState === 'loading' ) {
	document.addEventListener( 'DOMContentLoaded', () => initAccordions() );
} else {
	initAccordions();
}

	},
	"supports": { "html": false },
	"textdomain": "news-uk",
	"editorScript": "file:./index.js",
