8. More questions News UK has asked

The extra questions from the end of the question bank, with short answers. Many build on Q1–Q35, so each answer points back to the full one, or to the code in this repo that shows it. Say the bold line first, then pick two or three points.

Blocks and the editor

M1.What are the differences between apiVersion 1, 2 and 3 in block.json?

"Each version moved more responsibility to the block itself. Version 3 is the current one: it's what lets the editor run in an iframe."

  • 1 (legacy): the editor wrapped every block in its own extra <div>. Blocks didn't control their outer element.
  • 2 (WP 5.6): the block's own element is the wrapper, via useBlockProps(). Lighter DOM, and the editor markup matches the front end.
  • 3 (WP 6.3): same as 2, plus the block declares it works in the iframed editor (its styles and scripts load inside the iframe). The post editor only switches to the iframe when every registered block type is v3 (not just the blocks on the page), and WordPress 6.9 logs a deprecation for anything lower. See I4 for migrating a block.
In this repoEvery block uses "apiVersion": 3. The Jest run actually printed the 6.9 warning for a test block registered without it (Q3), which is a nice detail to mention.
Remember it as"Editor wraps, block wraps, block works in the iframe."

M2.Explain block.json in depth. Which fields matter most, and why register blocks from metadata?

"block.json is the block's single source of truth, read by both PHP and JS. Registering from metadata means WordPress registers the assets, loads them only where the block is used, and handles translations for me."

  • Identity: name (never rename it: it's stored in every post), title, category, apiVersion, textdomain.
  • Data: attributes, usesContext / providesContext.
  • Behaviour: supports (colour, spacing, typography, interactivity, html), parent / ancestor / allowedBlocks.
  • Assets: editorScript, script, viewScript, viewScriptModule, style, editorStyle, and render for a PHP render file.
  • Why metadata: one definition instead of two that drift apart; front-end assets enqueue only on pages that contain the block; strings in block.json are translatable; the block can be listed in the Block Directory. On sites with many blocks, WordPress 6.7+ can load them all from one compiled manifest (wp_register_block_metadata_collection()) for speed.
In this repoq01-article-teaser/index.js explains each key of its block.json in comments (JSON can't hold comments).

M3.How do block attributes work? Where are they stored and what are the common pitfalls?

"Attributes are the block's data, typed in block.json. Most are stored as JSON in the block's comment delimiter; some are sourced from the saved HTML."

  • Storage: no source means JSON in <!-- wp:name {...} -->. With a source (attribute, html, text, query), the value is parsed out of the saved markup instead. Post meta is the third place, now done with Block Bindings (Q4), not the old meta source.
  • Pitfall: defaults aren't saved. A value equal to the default isn't written to the comment, so changing a default later silently changes every old block that used it.
  • Pitfall: sourced attributes are tied to markup. Change the selector or the save markup and old content breaks, so you need a deprecation (Q3).
  • Pitfall: types. A string saved into an integer attribute fails validation. Convert input first (Q1 parseInt) or use TypeScript (Q21).
  • Pitfall: wrong home for data. Attributes live inside post_content, so they can't be queried or reused across posts. Data that needs that belongs in post meta.

M4.How do you use InnerBlocks? How do you control what can be nested and how it is saved?

"InnerBlocks makes my block a container for other blocks. I control what goes in with allowedBlocks, template and templateLock, and in save I output InnerBlocks.Content."

  • Edit: useInnerBlocksProps( useBlockProps(), { allowedBlocks, template, templateLock } ) puts the wrapper and the drop zone on one element.
  • Controlling nesting: allowedBlocks (which children), template (what a new block starts with), templateLock: "all", "insert" or "contentOnly" (text editable, layout locked). From the child side, block.json parent / ancestor.
  • Save: static blocks save useInnerBlocksProps.save(). Dynamic blocks save only <InnerBlocks.Content /> and render.php receives the children as $content.
In this repoQ2: Feature Card (static, with allowedBlocks and a template) and Live Blog Entry (dynamic, wraps $content).

M5.How does server-side rendering work for blocks, and how do you preview dynamic blocks in the editor?

"A dynamic block's save returns null; on every request WordPress calls its render callback or render.php with the attributes, content and block instance. In the editor I either use ServerSideRender or rebuild the preview in React."

  • On the server: do_blocks() parses post_content, and for each dynamic block calls the render with $attributes, $content and $block (which carries context such as postId).
  • Preview option 1, ServerSideRender: the editor calls the block-renderer REST endpoint and shows the real PHP output. Exact match, but one request per change.
  • Preview option 2, React: fetch the data with useSelect and draw it. Instant and interactive, but two implementations to keep in step.
  • Gotcha: a template in the Site Editor has no specific post, so context-dependent blocks need a placeholder there.
In this repoQ8 Product Box uses ServerSideRender. Q1 Article Teaser draws its own preview with useSelect. The Q4 get_field block shows a placeholder in the Site Editor.

M6.How do you use block patterns? What are synced patterns and pattern overrides?

"A pattern is a ready-made group of blocks editors insert and then own. A synced pattern is one shared copy: edit it once, it changes everywhere. Pattern overrides let editors change chosen parts of a synced pattern per instance."

  • Registering: drop a PHP file with a header into the theme's patterns/ folder (auto-registered), or call register_block_pattern() from a plugin.
  • Unsynced (regular): inserted as plain blocks, no link back. Good for starting layouts.
  • Synced (formerly "reusable blocks", stored as wp_block posts): one source, so a change shows up wherever it's used.
  • Pattern overrides (6.6+): inside a synced pattern, mark blocks as overridable (e.g. a heading and an image). The layout stays shared, and each instance has its own text. It's built on Block Bindings with the core/pattern-overrides source.
In this repoQ7: the "Key points" pattern (core blocks only) and the theme's patterns/hero.php.

M7.What are your best practices for block development?

"Core first; block.json as the source of truth; blocks in a plugin; dynamic for data; supports instead of custom controls; escape late; accessible markup; and tests at the right layer."

  • Try variations, styles, patterns and bindings before a custom block (Q7, Q8).
  • apiVersion 3, useBlockProps, @wordpress/components for UI, translated strings.
  • Dynamic rendering for anything data-driven; cache expensive queries (Q2, Q15).
  • Small attributes, typed; never rename a block; deprecations for static markup changes (Q3).
  • Load front-end JS only where needed (Interactivity API, viewScriptModule, Q5).
  • PHPUnit for render.php, Jest for edit/save, one Playwright journey; PHPCS + ESLint in CI.

M8.What can clients edit within the Site Editor?

"Anyone with the edit_theme_options capability (administrators by default) can edit templates, template parts, global styles, navigation menus, patterns and pages. Editors normally can't open it at all."

  • Global Styles: colours, typography and spacing, within what theme.json allows.
  • Templates and template parts: header, footer, single post and so on.
  • Navigation, patterns, pages (page content can also be edited inside the Site Editor).
  • The theme decides the limits: theme.json can turn off custom colours or font sizes, and block locking can fix the layout.

M9.Explain the template schema, and why template-level editing is typically restricted from clients.

"Templates are block markup: files in the theme's templates/ and parts/ folders, chosen by the template hierarchy. A customised copy is saved as a wp_template post that overrides the file. Template edits affect every page of that type at once, so they're kept to a few trusted people."

  • Schema: templates/single.html, templates/index.html, custom templates listed in theme.json customTemplates, parts in parts/ declared in templateParts. Edits go into the database (wp_template / wp_template_part) and win over the file until reset.
  • Why restrict: one change hits thousands of articles, including ad slots, analytics, structured data and accessibility landmarks. Database copies also drift from the theme code that's reviewed and deployed.
  • How: capabilities (only admins get edit_theme_options), locked blocks, contentOnly template locking, theme.json restrictions, and keeping template changes in code reviews.
In this repoThe theme's templates/single.html and the custom template q04-acf.html registered in theme.json. In the post editor, template blocks are read-only (we hit this in the Q4 Playwright test).

Testing

M10.Explain the general concepts of unit testing.

"A unit test checks one small piece of behaviour in isolation, quickly and the same way every time, so I can change code without fear."

  • Arrange, Act, Assert: set up, run the one thing, check the result.
  • Isolation: replace slow or external dependencies with test doubles (stubs return canned data, mocks check how they were called, fakes are simple working versions).
  • Test behaviour, not implementation: assert on outputs and effects, so a refactor doesn't break the tests.
  • Fast and deterministic: no network, no real time, no shared state between tests.
  • Coverage is a signal, not the goal: it shows what's untested, not whether the tests are good.

M11.Which JavaScript unit testing libraries do you use?

"Jest as the runner (through @wordpress/scripts), React Testing Library to render components the way a user sees them, user-event for interactions and jest-dom for readable assertions."

  • @wordpress/jest-preset-default sets up jsdom and Babel; @wordpress/jest-console fails a test on unexpected console warnings.
  • RTL's rule: query by role and label (getByRole( 'checkbox', { name: 'Show image' } )), which also checks accessibility.
In this repo32 Jest tests: Q3 (deprecations), Q13 (save output), Q20 (React component with fake timers), Q22 (RTL on an edit component), Q5 (a plain DOM viewScript, and an Interactivity API store with the runtime mocked, M61), and a custom @wordpress/data store (section 9, I13).

M12.How do you write and maintain unit tests over time?

"Tests are code: reviewed in the same PR, kept fast, fixed or deleted when they rot, and every bug fix comes with a test that would have caught it."

  • Run them in CI on every PR; a red test blocks the merge.
  • Fix flaky tests immediately; a flaky suite gets ignored.
  • Test behaviour so refactors don't need test rewrites; use data providers to cover edge cases in one test.
  • Delete tests for removed features; keep test names readable as documentation.
ExampleWhen the REST endpoint accepted a 301-character standfirst, the fix came with the failing test cases that found it (Q14).

M13.How do you unit test PHP code without loading WordPress?

"Keep business logic in pure functions or classes that don't call WordPress, and test those with plain PHPUnit. Where code must call WordPress functions, mock them with Brain Monkey or WP_Mock."

  • Design for it: pass data in and return data out; keep hooks and database calls at the edges.
  • Mocking WordPress: Brain Monkey or WP_Mock stub get_option(), apply_filters() and friends, and let you assert that hooks were added.
  • Trade-off: these run in milliseconds but can't catch integration problems, so keep some WP_UnitTestCase tests too.
In this repoQ12: reading_time_minutes() tested with a plain TestCase. Q28's merge() and Q31's repair class are kept pure for the same reason.

M14.How do you test block registration, save output, transforms and deprecations in Jest?

"Register the block in the test, then use the @wordpress/blocks API exactly as the editor does: getSaveContent for save, serialize and parse for round trips, switchToBlockType for transforms, and parsing old markup for deprecations."

  • Registration: registerBlockType( name, settings ), then getBlockType( name ) has the expected attributes and supports.
  • Save: getSaveContent( name, attributes ) returns the exact HTML.
  • Round trip: parse( serialize( block ) ) must give isValid: true.
  • Transforms: switchToBlockType( createBlock( 'core/paragraph', {...} ), 'my/block' ).
  • Deprecations: parse markup produced by the old version; assert it's valid and the attributes were migrated.
In this repoQ3 generates the old markup by registering v1 in the test, so the fixture is exactly what v1 saved. Q13 tests save output and the round trip.

M15.How does wp-scripts test-unit-js work, and how do you configure it?

"It runs Jest with WordPress's preset: jsdom, Babel for JSX and modern JS, and test files in test/ folders or named *.test.js. A jest.config.js in the project root extends or overrides the preset."

  • Flags pass through to Jest: --watch, --coverage, -t "name", or a path to run one folder.
  • Typical overrides: roots, setupFiles (globals jsdom lacks, like ResizeObserver), and transforming ESM-only packages in node_modules.
In this repojest.config.js spreads the preset, adds setup globals and jest-dom, and compiles ESM dependencies. A real gotcha: two copies of @wordpress/element broke RichText.Content in Jest until it was deduped (Q13).

M16.How do you approach WordPress integration testing?

"WP_UnitTestCase with a real WordPress and a throwaway database: create data with factories, run the real code path (hooks, REST, block rendering) and assert on the result. Each test is rolled back."

  • Use do_blocks() for render output, rest_do_request() for endpoints, go_to() for template context, and pre_http_request to fake outbound HTTP.
  • Know the framework's resets: meta keys are unregistered after every test, and data providers run before the transaction.
In this repoMost of the 136 PHPUnit tests are integration tests. Q10 lists the setup gotchas found on the first run.

M17.Explain E2E testing concepts.

"An end-to-end test drives a real browser against a running site, the way a user would, to prove the whole stack works together. They're slow and costly, so I keep a few for the critical journeys."

  • Set up data through the API, act through the UI, assert what the reader sees.
  • Each test is independent: its own data, cleaned up afterwards.
  • Run in CI with traces and screenshots on failure.
In this repo11 Playwright tests (Q4, Q5, Q23, Q32, and the FAQ block in section 9), run in Chrome locally and in CI.

M18.Which E2E testing libraries have you used for JavaScript applications?

"Cypress, and Playwright with @wordpress/e2e-test-utils-playwright, which is what Gutenberg itself uses."

  • Playwright: multiple browsers, auto-waiting, real iframe support (the editor canvas), traces, parallel runs.
  • The WordPress utils add fixtures: admin, editor, page, requestUtils.
Your storySay which one you used where. The explainer's Q23 note says Cypress is the E2E tool on your CV, and the concepts transfer. The Playwright suite in this repo is your hands-on evidence.

M19.How do you keep E2E tests reliable, and how do you add accessibility checks?

"Wait for state, never for time; locate by role; set up through the API; isolate tests; and look at traces when they fail. For accessibility, run axe on key pages and test keyboard paths."

  • Reliability: web-first assertions (await expect( locator ).toBeVisible()), role locators, no sleep, clean data per test, retries only in CI and with traces kept.
  • Hide what changes between runs: e.g. use admin.editPost() rather than raw URLs, because it switches off the welcome guide modal.
  • Accessibility: @axe-core/playwright (new AxeBuilder( { page } ).analyze()) to fail on violations, and keyboard tests for interactive parts.
In this repoThe Q4 test passed locally but failed in CI because the welcome guide covered the editor; switching to admin.editPost() fixed it. The Q5 accordion test checks the keyboard (Enter) and aria-expanded.

M20.Talk us through Jest: test structure, mocking, assertions and test execution.

"describe and it for structure, beforeEach for setup, jest.fn and jest.mock for doubles, expect matchers for assertions, and the CLI for running a subset, watching or collecting coverage."

  • Structure: describe() groups, it() / test() cases, beforeEach / afterEach.
  • Mocking: jest.fn() for callbacks such as setAttributes; jest.mock( '@wordpress/data', ... ) for modules; jest.spyOn; fake timers.
  • Assertions: toBe, toEqual, toHaveBeenCalledWith, and jest-dom's toBeInTheDocument, toBeDisabled.
  • Execution: npm run test:unit -- --watch, -t "name", --coverage.
In this repoQ22 shows module mocks (@wordpress/block-editor, useSelect) and jest.fn() for setAttributes.

M21.How do you test asynchronous behaviour such as loading and error states in a component?

"Control the async source, then assert each state: the loading state first, then the result or the error, using findBy queries or waitFor. For timers, fake them."

  • Mock the data layer so you decide when it resolves or rejects (e.g. apiFetch returning a rejected promise for the error state).
  • await screen.findByText( 'Budget day' ) waits for the result; waitFor() for other conditions.
  • Debounces and polling: jest.useFakeTimers() and jest.advanceTimersByTime().
In this repoQ22 tests the "Loading…" state; Q20 uses fake timers to prove the debounce cancels stale timers.

M22.How do you test REST APIs with PHPUnit, and with a GUI tool such as Postman?

"In PHPUnit I call the real REST server with rest_do_request and check one status code per case. In Postman I keep a collection of requests with an environment for each site, plus assertions, and run it with Newman in CI."

  • PHPUnit: build a WP_REST_Request, set the user with wp_set_current_user(), assert status and data: 200, 400, 401, 403, 404.
  • Postman: authenticate with an Application Password (Basic auth) for server-to-server, or a cookie plus X-WP-Nonce; pm.test() scripts check status and body; Newman runs the collection headless.
In this repoQ14 has one PHPUnit test per status code.

M23.What type of code testing techniques do you use?

"The pyramid: static analysis at the base, many unit tests, some integration tests, a few E2E journeys, plus accessibility checks and manual exploratory testing before a release."

  • Static analysis: PHPCS (WordPress + VIP rules), ESLint, TypeScript.
  • Unit, integration (WP_UnitTestCase, REST), E2E (Playwright).
  • Data providers for edge cases, regression tests for every bug, test doubles at boundaries (pre_http_request).
  • Where it fits: visual regression, contract tests for external APIs, load tests before big events.

M24.Have you written the unit tests just now, or do you always follow this practice?

"Testing is part of how I deliver, not something added for the interview: on my VIP projects PHPUnit and linting ran in CI. For this sample I wrote the tests alongside the code, and they caught real bugs."

Your storyBe honest about which projects had which tests. Then make it concrete with this repo: the first real run found a REST validation bug, a deprecated VIP helper and several setup issues, all fixed with tests (Q10, Q14). That shows the habit better than any claim.

M25.Have you done visual regression testing? If yes, which tools have you used?

"Visual regression compares screenshots against approved baselines to catch unintended visual changes. The tools I'd reach for are Playwright's toHaveScreenshot, BackstopJS, Percy or Chromatic."

  • Make it stable: fixed viewport, fixed data, hidden dynamic areas (ads, dates), animations off.
  • Keep it to key templates and components, or the baselines become noise.
Your storyOnly claim tools you've used in production. If you haven't, say so, then show you know how you'd add it: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot() on the article template would fit straight into this repo's suite.

M26.Will Playwright cover the Jest and PHPUnit tests?

"No. They test different layers. Playwright proves the journey works end to end; it's too slow and too coarse to cover every edge case that unit and integration tests check in milliseconds."

  • Example: the Article Teaser has six "renders nothing" cases (draft, private, trashed, missing, zero, no ID). That's one PHPUnit data-provider test; in Playwright it would be six slow browser runs.
  • When a Playwright test fails, it tells you something is broken, not where. Lower-level tests pinpoint it.
In this repo136 PHPUnit and 32 Jest tests against 11 Playwright tests: the pyramid in practice (Q12).

M27.Which part do you think is difficult to unit test?

"Code with hidden dependencies: global state, direct database calls, remote HTTP, the current time, and UI that depends on the whole editor's data stores. The fix is to create seams."

  • WordPress gives useful seams: pre_http_request for HTTP, filters for configuration, factories for data.
  • In JS, mock the store hooks (useSelect) or use a test registry.
  • Time and randomness: pass them in, or use fake timers.
In this repoThe Q25 sync is testable only because the HTTP call goes through wp_remote_post(), so pre_http_request can fake every failure mode.

M28.How do you determine which functions need a PHPUnit test, or which parts of a function are not tested?

"By risk: business logic, security (permissions, sanitising, escaping), data transformations, anything that has broken before, and public APIs. Then a coverage report shows the lines and branches nothing exercises."

  • Coverage: phpunit --coverage-html with Xdebug or PCOV; read it for untested branches, not for a percentage target.
  • Thin glue code (one add_action) usually isn't worth a dedicated test; the integration tests cover it.

M29.Demonstrate awareness of the testing pyramid.

"Many fast unit tests, fewer integration tests, a handful of E2E tests, all on a base of static analysis. Up the pyramid, tests get slower, more realistic and more expensive."

See Q12 for the full answer and diagram. The repo shows the shape: PHPCS + ESLint + tsc, 32 Jest, 136 PHPUnit, 11 Playwright, run in that order in CI so the cheap checks fail first.

APIs, debugging and caching

M30.How do you handle REST API work and integrations?

"For our own endpoints: schema-validated arguments, a real permission check and consistent errors. For third-party APIs: never call them during a reader's request; sync in the background, cache, and degrade gracefully."

Full answers in Q14 (building endpoints) and Q25 (integrating an external API).

Your storyYour OAuth, Salesforce and Dynamics 365 integrations, and the Ziff Davis GraphQL catalogue (from the explainer's Q14 and Q25 notes).

M31.How do you debug server-side issues?

"Turn on WP_DEBUG and WP_DEBUG_LOG (without displaying errors), use Query Monitor to see queries, hooks, HTTP calls and errors for the request, and step through with Xdebug when I need to see state."

  • WP_DEBUG, WP_DEBUG_LOG, WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY false, SCRIPT_DEBUG for unminified scripts.
  • Query Monitor: slow or duplicate queries, which template and blocks rendered, HTTP API calls, PHP errors, object cache stats.
  • Xdebug: breakpoints in the IDE; wp shell or wp eval to try code quickly. All of this is local: on VIP production, errors go to the Runtime Logs in the VIP Dashboard, and wp shell isn't available through VIP-CLI.
  • On VIP: New Relic and the logs available through the VIP dashboard and CLI.
In this repoThe local wp-config.php sets the debug constants, and the VIP mu-plugins bundle Query Monitor (it shows in the admin bar locally).

M32.How do you troubleshoot API and backend functionality?

"Reproduce it in isolation, look at the actual request and response, narrow it down, then fix with a test."

  • Reproduce with curl, Postman or rest_do_request(); read status, headers and body.
  • Check logs and Query Monitor's HTTP panel; compare with a known-good environment.
  • Narrow down: one variable at a time (user, cache, plugin, data).
Example from this repoPlaywright couldn't log in. curl showed no auth cookie and the error "You have exceeded the login limit", which led to the real cause: VIP blocks the username admin, so no user had been created. Then the admin cookie path didn't cover /wp-admin. Two root causes found by looking at the actual responses.

M33.Which browser and monitoring tools do you use for front-end debugging?

"Chrome DevTools for network, performance, Lighthouse and accessibility; React DevTools for components; Query Monitor for the server side of a page; New Relic for production."

  • DevTools: Network (status, caching headers), Performance (long tasks, layout shift), Lighthouse (Core Web Vitals), Coverage (unused JS/CSS), the accessibility tree.
  • New Relic: APM for slow transactions and external calls, plus browser monitoring for real users.
Example from this repoA REST read looked stale in the browser. DevTools showed Cache-Control: max-age=60 from VIP, so the browser had reused its cached response (Q35).

M34.How do you debug React and JavaScript applications?

"React DevTools for props, state and re-renders; breakpoints with source maps; and in the block editor, the wp.data stores straight from the console."

  • React DevTools Profiler to find unnecessary re-renders (e.g. a useSelect returning new objects).
  • SCRIPT_DEBUG for unminified WordPress scripts and readable stack traces.
  • Console: wp.data.select( 'core/block-editor' ).getBlocks(), getBlockEditingMode( clientId ).
Example from this repoA Playwright click kept timing out. In the console, getBlockEditingMode() returned "disabled" and the element was inert: template blocks are read-only in the post editor. That changed the test design (Q4).

M35.Explain edge caching.

"The CDN keeps a copy of the whole HTML page at locations near readers, so most requests never reach WordPress. Freshness is controlled by Cache-Control and by purging URLs when content changes."

  • TTL: Cache-Control: max-age; stale-while-revalidate serves the old copy while fetching a new one; stale-if-error keeps the site up if the origin struggles.
  • Purge: when a post is updated, VIP purges its URL plus the home page, the post's public category and tag archives, the posts page and the feeds. Other pages that show the post (a custom landing page) need wpvip_purge_edge_cache_for_url() or the wpcom_vip_cache_purge_{$post_type}_post_urls filter.
  • Bypass: logged-in users and personalised pages aren't cached; anything per-user is done client-side or with a cache variant.
  • News angle: a short TTL on live blogs (30–60s) absorbs a traffic spike and stays fresh enough.
In this repoQ27 sets max-age=60, stale-while-revalidate=30, stale-if-error=600 on live blogs.

M36.What is the difference between object caching and transients?

"The object cache is a key-value cache for anything, and it only persists between requests with a drop-in such as Memcached. Transients are a cache API with an expiry that falls back to the database when there's no persistent object cache."

  • With Memcached (VIP), transients are stored in the object cache, so in practice they're the same thing with an expiry.
  • Either can disappear early, so always handle a miss.
  • Prefer wp_cache_* with a group on VIP; use transients when the code must also work on hosts without an object cache.

Full answers in Q15 and Q16; the Q16 test shows both storage paths.

M37.Where does the object cache store its data?

"By default, in a PHP array for the current request only. With a persistent drop-in (object-cache.php) it's stored in Memcached, as on VIP, or Redis, so it survives between requests and is shared by all servers."

  • The drop-in lives at wp-content/object-cache.php; wp_using_ext_object_cache() says whether one is active.
  • Keys are grouped and can be salted (WP_CACHE_KEY_SALT) so environments sharing a server don't collide.
In this repoThe Docker stack runs a Memcached container used through VIP's drop-in. When tests started sharing it, they needed their own WP_CACHE_KEY_SALT or they overwrote the site's cache (Q10).

Local development and platform

M38.Which local development tool do you use?

"For VIP projects, an environment that mirrors the platform: VIP's dev-env, or a Docker setup with the VIP mu-plugins and Memcached like the one in this repo. For quick plugin work, wp-env."

In this repodocker-compose.yml: WordPress (PHP 8.4), MySQL and Memcached, with the VIP mu-plugins loaded and the repo mounted as wp-content, as on VIP.
Your storyName what you actually use day to day, and why it matches production.

M39.What is the difference between wp-env and LocalWP?

"wp-env is a command-line, Docker-based environment configured by a JSON file, built for plugin and theme development and CI, with a separate test site. LocalWP is a desktop app for running full sites with a GUI."

wp-envLocalWP
InterfaceCLI (npx wp-env start), .wp-env.json in the repoDesktop app
Runs onDockerIts own bundled services (no Docker)
Best forPlugins/themes, reproducible setups, CI; includes a tests siteWhole sites, client work, quick demos; SSL, mail capture, sharing links
In version controlYes, config travels with the codeNo, set up per machine

M40.How do you run unit tests in LocalWP?

"Open the site shell from LocalWP, install the WordPress test library pointed at LocalWP's database, then run PHPUnit from the plugin folder."

  1. Site shell ("Open site shell") so php, mysql and wp use the site's versions.
  2. Install the test suite: bin/install-wp-tests.sh wordpress_test root root localhost:/path/to/mysqld.sock latest (from wp scaffold plugin-tests), or use wp-phpunit/wp-phpunit from Composer.
  3. composer install, then vendor/bin/phpunit in the plugin folder.

The usual snag is the database host: LocalWP uses a socket, shown on the site's Database tab.

M41.How do you run unit tests in wp-env?

"wp-env starts a separate tests environment, so you run PHPUnit inside its tests-cli container, pointed at the plugin folder."

npx wp-env start
npx wp-env run tests-cli --env-cwd=wp-content/plugins/my-plugin composer install
npx wp-env run tests-cli --env-cwd=wp-content/plugins/my-plugin vendor/bin/phpunit

The tests site runs on its own port (8889 by default) with its own database, so tests never touch the development site. That's also the default target for the Playwright config in @wordpress/scripts.

In this repoThe Docker equivalent: docker compose exec -w /var/www/html/wp-content wordpress phpunit.

M42.What are the reasons for migrating to the cloud?

"To scale with traffic instead of buying for the peak, to hand infrastructure, patching and failover to a provider, and to ship faster. For a publisher, staying up during a breaking-news spike is the big one."

  • Elastic scaling and global CDN; high availability across zones.
  • Managed security patching, backups, monitoring.
  • Pay for use; faster environments and deploys; teams focus on product, not servers.
  • For WordPress, a managed platform like VIP adds code review, edge caching and enterprise support.
Your storyCHOICE Australia's migration to VIP and the Ziff Davis move of CNET, ZDNET and PCMag to a VIP multisite are your examples.

M43.Have you used any cloud services or third-party APIs?

"Yes: WordPress VIP as the platform, and integrations such as OAuth providers, Salesforce, Dynamics 365 and a GraphQL product catalogue."

Your storyPick one and give the shape: what it did, how failures were handled (timeouts, retries, cached fallback), how it was tested. The explainer's Q14 and Q25 notes list your integrations; only name cloud services you've used.

About your code sample

M44.Why have the blocks been integrated with the theme and not in a plugin?

"They shouldn't be: blocks belong in a plugin, so content keeps working if the theme changes. In this repo they're in the news-uk-practice plugin; the theme only has templates, styles and patterns."

  • The theme owns presentation (theme.json, templates, parts, patterns). The plugin owns functionality and data (blocks, meta, REST, integrations).
  • If a block ever sat in a theme, say why (prototype, theme-only styling) and how you'd move it: same block name, so saved content still works.

M45.I can see some ACF references in the code. Why have you used it?

"Only as a side-by-side comparison for the Block Bindings question. The production approach in the repo is registered post meta with Block Bindings, and the ADR explains why."

  • Q4 builds the same standfirst three ways: two with ACF, one native. The native one needs no plugin and puts the data in REST.
  • ACF is still a fair choice for complex field UIs (repeaters, galleries) or a site already built on it.
  • Lessons from building it: "ACF Blocks" are PRO only, and ACF's binding source reads the global post rather than block context.

M46.How and where do you use particular tooling?

"Each tool sits where its feedback is cheapest: linters in the editor and on commit, unit tests on every save, integration and E2E in CI, Query Monitor while developing, New Relic in production."

WhereTools

AI

M47.What is MCP, and which MCP servers have you used?

"MCP, the Model Context Protocol, is an open standard for connecting AI assistants to tools and data. An MCP server exposes actions (like 'click this element' or 'search issues'), and any MCP-capable assistant can use them."

  • It replaces one-off integrations per assistant with one protocol: write a server once, use it from different clients.
  • Guardrails matter: servers act with your credentials, so scope access and review what the assistant does, especially write actions.
In this repoThe Chrome DevTools MCP server was used to check the blocks in a real Chrome: reading the editor's state, inspecting the rendered markup and taking screenshots.
Your storyName the servers you've actually used and what for (e.g. browser automation, issue trackers, docs). Keep it within the client's policy (Q35).

M48.What are the differences between Sonnet models and other AI models?

"Sonnet is the middle tier of Anthropic's Claude family: Opus and Fable are more capable and cost more, Haiku is faster and cheaper. Sonnet is the usual balance of speed, cost and quality for everyday coding and agent work."

  • Within Claude: pick by task. The most capable model for hard reasoning and long agentic work; Sonnet for most coding; Haiku for high-volume, simple tasks.
  • Against other vendors: models differ in quality per task, context window, tool use, price and data policies. Compare on your own tasks, not benchmarks alone.
  • For a client like News UK: the approved tool comes first (GitHub Copilot), whatever model is "best".

M49.How often have you used AI?

"Regularly, as an assistant: scaffolding, test stubs, explaining unfamiliar code and drafting docs. Its output goes through the same review, standards and tests as my own code."

Your storyBe specific and honest about frequency and tasks. Tie it to the Q35 guardrails (approved tools only, untrusted output, human review) and to your AI hackathon awards and Publisher AI Readiness Audit at XWP.

Collaboration, leadership and opinion

M50.Tell us about your experience with code sharing and collaboration.

"Shared code lives in versioned packages or a shared plugin with clear owners. Changes go through small PRs with reviews, CI and written decisions, so several teams can build on it safely."

  • Git with short-lived branches and small PRs; code review with Conventional Comments (Q33).
  • Shared libraries via Composer/npm or a monorepo; semantic versioning; changelogs.
  • ADRs and docs in the repo (Q26); pairing for knowledge transfer.
Your storyThe Ziff Davis catalogue shared across CNET, ZDNET and PCMag, with per-brand overrides (Q28): one codebase, several teams.

M51.How do you approach code review as a lead engineer?

"Review fast, automate the nitpicks, focus on correctness, security, performance, tests and accessibility, separate facts from preferences, and use reviews to teach."

  • Tooling handles style (PHPCS, ESLint), so humans review design and risk.
  • Conventional Comments with (blocking) / (non-blocking), and questions rather than orders.
  • Small PRs, a PR template with a testing section, a same-day turnaround target.
  • Two rounds of comments, then a call (Q33).

M52.How do you mentor engineers and raise the team's quality bar, especially on testing?

"Make good testing the easy path, show it in real code, and give specific feedback. Raise the bar through habits and tooling, not lectures."

  • Working examples and templates (a test file per block, factories, data providers).
  • Pairing on the first tests of a feature; reviewing tests as carefully as code.
  • CI gates so untested or unlinted code can't merge; regression test per bug as a team rule.
  • Short sessions on real failures, like the setup gotchas in Q10.
Your storyLeading a team of seven on the Woo Marketplace: one concrete change you made to how that team tested or reviewed.

M53.What is your opinion of the classic editor? Is it difficult to use?

"It's simple and familiar for plain text, but it stores one blob of HTML. Blocks give structured, reusable content, patterns and safer layouts, which matters for a publisher. I'd move editors over with training and patterns that match how they already work."

  • Classic: quick for text; layouts rely on shortcodes and pasted HTML, which break easily.
  • Blocks: structure, reuse, design consistency, Site Editor; a learning curve for editors.
  • The Classic block and the Classic Editor plugin smooth the transition; be diplomatic, since some editors love it.

Follow-ups to Q5: Interactivity API stores (practice questions, not from the bank)

Likely follow-ups once you've answered Q5. All of them use one block, q05-interactivity/save-for-later/: a "Save for later" button, a saved-articles count and a "Show summary" button. Its code is in the Q5 box.

M54.How does data-wp-on--click="actions.toggle" find its code? What if the name is wrong?

"The value is a path into a store. data-wp-interactive on a parent picks the store by namespace, and actions.toggle is read from that store's object. Nothing checks it at build time, so a typo only fails in the browser."

  • data-wp-interactive="news-uk/saved" sets the namespace for everything inside it; store( 'news-uk/saved', { actions: { toggle } } ) defines it.
  • Another store can be named in the value: data-wp-text="other/ns::state.count".
  • A wrong name: the server HTML still renders, the click does nothing, and the console shows an error. That's why an E2E test matters for interactive blocks.
In this repoThe Playwright test in e2e/save-for-later.spec.js clicks every directive in the block, so a typo in render.php fails CI.

M55.What are the standard parts of a store?

"state for shared data, actions for events, callbacks for side effects. Context, getElement() and getConfig() sit next to the store."

  • state: shared by every block in the namespace on the page; PHP sets the starting values with wp_interactivity_state(); getters give derived values. Read by data-wp-bind--*, data-wp-text, data-wp-class--*, data-wp-style--*.
  • actions: run by data-wp-on--* (click, submit, keydown). Async ones are generators (M58).
  • callbacks: run by data-wp-init, data-wp-watch, data-wp-run (M59).
  • store() returns the merged store, and calling it again with the same namespace merges more in. That's how PHP state and the JS definition become one object.
  • The names are conventions (the runtime follows whatever path you write), except state, which is where server state is merged. Stick to the three names anyway.

M56.State or context: how do you decide where a value lives?

"Shared by every instance on the page: state. Belongs to one instance: context. Never changes: config."

  • Context (data-wp-context, or wp_interactivity_data_wp_context() in PHP) is per element and inherited by its children; read it with getContext(). The Q5 accordion keeps isOpen here so each accordion opens on its own.
  • State is one object per namespace. In "Save for later", savedIds is state, so saving in one block updates the count in every block.
  • A getter can combine both: isSaved reads the shared savedIds and this block's postId, so the same getter gives a different answer per block.
In this repoThe table at the top of save-for-later/render.php lists every value and where it lives. The Playwright test puts two blocks in one post and checks a click in one updates both.

M57.What is derived state, and how does the server render it?

"A value computed from other state, written as a getter in JS so it can never get out of sync. Since WordPress 6.7 you can give PHP a matching closure, so the first HTML already has the right value."

  • JS: get count() { return state.savedIds.length; }. Directives read it like a normal value, and it updates when savedIds changes.
  • PHP: 'count' => static fn () => count( wp_interactivity_state()['savedIds'] ). Inside the closure, wp_interactivity_state() and wp_interactivity_get_context() with no arguments read the current namespace and block.
  • Without the closure, the server leaves those attributes empty and the page changes when JS runs. 6.9 also tells the client which values were closures, so it uses its own getters.
  • The server can only render what it knows. Saved items in localStorage appear after JS runs; for a logged-in reader, load the list from user meta in PHP and the first HTML is right.
In this repoPHPUnit renders the block with an empty list ("Save for later", count 0, aria-pressed="false") and with the post saved ("Saved", count 2, aria-pressed="true").

M58.How do you write an async action, and why a generator instead of async/await?

"Write *loadSummary() { const res = yield fetch( url ); }. The runtime resumes the generator with the block's scope restored, so getContext() after the wait still means the block that was clicked. With await that scope is lost."

  • yield where you'd write await; the runtime waits for the promise and passes the result back.
  • Set a loading flag in context, use try/catch/finally so a failed request still clears it, and show a fallback.
  • Code that runs later outside an action (a setTimeout, a third-party callback) can be wrapped with withScope() to keep the scope.
In this repoactions.loadSummary fetches the excerpt from wp/v2/posts. Jest drives the generator step by step, as the runtime would, for both success and a 404.

M59.What are callbacks for? Explain data-wp-init, data-wp-watch and data-wp-run.

"Side effects. init runs once when the element starts, watch runs at start and again whenever a value it read changes, and run runs on every render, so it can use hooks."

  • init: one-off setup: read localStorage, start an observer. "Save for later" restores the reader's list here.
  • watch: keep something outside the DOM in sync. persist writes savedIds to storage on every change.
  • Gotcha: a watch only re-runs for values it actually read. If it returns early before reading state.savedIds, it never subscribes and never runs again. So persist reads first, then checks it may write.
  • Gotcha: a watch also runs at start, possibly before init. Without a guard, persist would overwrite saved data with the server's empty list.
  • Several of the same directive on one element need a suffix: data-wp-watch--persist, data-wp-watch--focus.
In this repoA Jest test runs persist before restore and checks storage isn't wiped; Playwright reloads the page and checks the saved state comes back.

M60.What are getElement(), getConfig() and private stores for?

"getElement() gives the DOM element a directive is on, getConfig() gives read-only settings from PHP, and a private store stops other scripts reading or extending yours."

  • getElement().ref: for what directives can't do, like moving focus. focusSummary focuses the summary when it loads, so screen readers announce it.
  • wp_interactivity_config( 'news-uk/saved', [ 'restUrl' => rest_url( ... ) ] ) in PHP, getConfig().restUrl in JS. For values that never change in the page: URLs, storage keys, feature flags. Unlike state, it isn't reactive.
  • store( 'ns', { ... }, { lock: true } ) makes the store private. Use it for a plugin's internals that other code shouldn't rely on; cross-store paths like ns::state.x then can't reach it.

M61.How do you test an Interactivity API block?

"Three layers: PHPUnit for the server HTML, Jest for the store's logic with the runtime mocked, and Playwright for the directives working in a browser."

  • PHPUnit: render the block and check the processed HTML with WP_HTML_Tag_Processor: derived state, hidden panels, context, and that wp_interactivity_state() / wp_interactivity_config() hold what the client needs.
  • Jest: mock @wordpress/interactivity with a tiny fake store / getContext / getConfig / getElement, then call getters, actions and callbacks directly. Drive generator actions with .next().
  • Playwright: the only layer that proves the directive paths match the store, that shared state updates every block, and that init and watch really run.
In this reposave-for-later/test/view.test.js (8 Jest tests), tests/class-save-for-later-test.php (6 PHPUnit tests), e2e/save-for-later.spec.js (1 Playwright test).

9. Mock interview (17 Nov 2026): every question, corrected

Every question the panel asked in the mock interview, in the order they came, plus the topics they said the real interview will push on. The answers here were written from the WordPress 6.9 source and docs, not from what was said in the call. Where the answer given in the call was wrong or incomplete, a red "In the mock" box says what to fix. Feedback from the panel
  • Setup: have power and internet backup, a fresh WordPress install that's ready to use, and your best code open in tabs. Expect to share your screen and code live (autocomplete is usually allowed, AI agents are not). npm run setup in this repo gives you all of that.
  • Focus areas: every kind of testing and why you'd pick each; TypeScript (their JS is written in TS); FSE; apiVersion 2 vs 3 (they migrate blocks); caching layers on VIP; AI usage.
  • Depth: they're hiring at senior/staff level. For every tool you mention (Elasticsearch, WP-Cron, an index), have the pros, the cons, and when you wouldn't use it.
  • When unsure: say so in one sentence, then say how you'd find out or reason it out. Don't guess at length.

Blocks and the editor

I1.Walk us through how you'd create a custom Gutenberg block.

"First I decide whether core blocks, a pattern or a variation would do. If a custom block is justified, I decide static or dynamic, scaffold it with create-block, describe it in block.json, register it from PHP, build the editor UI in React, render it (save or render.php), and write tests."

  1. Scaffold: npx @wordpress/create-block@latest my-block (static) or --variant dynamic (adds render.php). Inside an existing plugin, add --no-plugin.
  2. Describe: block.json is the single source of truth: name, apiVersion: 3, attributes, supports, context, and asset files.
  3. Register: register_block_type( __DIR__ . '/build/my-block' ) on init. WordPress then loads the assets only on pages that use the block.
  4. Edit: spread useBlockProps() on the wrapper; use supports for colour, spacing and typography before writing custom controls; settings go in InspectorControls.
  5. Render: static save(), or render.php with get_block_wrapper_attributes() and escaping.
  6. Test: PHPUnit for render.php, Jest for save and edit, Playwright for the editor journey.
In the mockRight overall. The flag is --variant dynamic, not --dynamic. Don't offer to "go through all the files" when they stop you: give the six steps in one breath, then stop.
In this repoQ1 (Article Teaser) follows exactly these steps. The README's "Bootstrap a new plugin or theme" section has the commands.
Remember it asCore first, then Scaffold, Describe, Register, Edit, Render, Test.

I2.What are the trade-offs between static and dynamic blocks?

"Static blocks save their final HTML, so they cost nothing to render and survive without the plugin, but any markup change needs a deprecation. Dynamic blocks render in PHP on every request, so they're always current and their markup can change freely, but they cost server time and render nothing if the plugin is gone."

Static (save())Dynamic (render.php)
Render costNone: HTML is in post_contentPHP on every uncached request (edge cache hides most of it)
Changing markupNeeds a deprecation, or old posts show "unexpected content"Change render.php and every post updates
Live data (prices, titles, dates)Frozen at save timeAlways current
Plugin deactivatedSaved HTML still showsBlock renders nothing
Portability (RSS, exports, headless)HTML is already thereNeeds do_blocks(); raw content has only the comment
Editor previewSame React codeRebuild the preview in React, or ServerSideRender

Hybrid: a dynamic block can still save its inner blocks (save: () => <InnerBlocks.Content />) and render.php wraps them. Editors' words are saved; the wrapper stays flexible.

In the mockYou covered render cost and deprecations. Also say live data, what happens when the plugin is deactivated, and portability. These are the points a principal engineer weighs.
In this repoQ2: one static, one hybrid. The FAQ Item in this section is also a hybrid.

I3.What is apiVersion in block.json, why do we need it, and which version is current?

"apiVersion is the version of the Block API the block is written for. It tells the editor how to treat the block: who renders the wrapper, and whether the block is safe in the iframed editor. It isn't a version of block.json's format. The current one is 3, since WordPress 6.3."

  • 1: the editor wrapped each block in its own extra element. The block didn't control its wrapper.
  • 2 (WP 5.6): the block renders its own wrapper with useBlockProps(). The editor DOM matches the front end.
  • 3 (WP 6.3): same markup as 2, plus a promise that the block works inside the iframed editor: its styles and scripts load in the iframe, and its JS doesn't assume the editor's document.
  • It's an integer, not semver. The block.json format is described by $schema, not by apiVersion.
  • WordPress 6.9 logs a deprecation for any block registered with apiVersion 2 or lower: "all editors are planned to work as iframes in the future".
In the mockYou described it as a version number for block.json's own keys. The panel said that wasn't correct. It's about how the editor treats the block (wrapper, then iframe). Lead with "iframe".
Remember it as"Editor wraps, block wraps, block works in the iframe."

I4.A block was built with apiVersion 2. How do you migrate it to 3? What do you check?

"Change the number, then make the block work inside an iframe: styles must load in the iframe, and JavaScript must use the block's own document and window, not the global ones. The saved markup doesn't change, so no deprecation is needed."

  1. Set "apiVersion": 3 in block.json (and in any registerBlockType settings that repeat it).
  2. Styles: content styles must come from block.json (style, editorStyle) or the enqueue_block_assets hook, which load inside the iframe. enqueue_block_editor_assets is for the editor UI around the canvas.
  3. Scripts: code that does document.querySelector, window.addEventListener or measures window.innerWidth now looks at the wrong document. Use the element's ownerDocument and defaultView, e.g. through a ref (useRefEffect).
  4. Third-party libraries (sliders, maps, jQuery plugins) that attach to the global document need the iframe's document passed in.
  5. Test where the iframe is used: the Site Editor, template and pattern editing, and device previews are always iframed. The post editor iframes only when every registered block type is v3, so one v2 block from any plugin keeps it un-iframed and can hide your bugs.
  6. Markup: v2 and v3 save the same HTML, so no deprecation. Coming from v1 is different: adding useBlockProps.save() can change saved markup, which needs a deprecation (Q3).
In the mockYou said you'd read the changelog. That's fine as a habit, but they migrate blocks often, so have this checklist ready.
In this repoEvery block here is v3. In the Jest run, a test block registered without apiVersion printed the 6.9 deprecation (Q3).
Remember it as"Bump, Styles in, Document from the element, Libraries, Test in the iframe."

I5.How would you optimise a block's performance, in the editor and on the front end?

"Measure first. In the editor, the usual causes are too many re-renders and too many requests. On the front end, it's queries per block, assets loaded everywhere, and anything that stops the page being cached."

Editor (React DevTools Profiler, the Network tab):

  • useSelect with a dependency array, returning small values (an ID or a string), not new objects or arrays on every call, which re-render the block on every store change.
  • Fetch through the data store, so ten copies of a block make one request (I11). Debounce search inputs (Q20).
  • No heavy work in render: useMemo for expensive derived values; move side effects out of render.
  • Avoid ServerSideRender for things that change on every keystroke: it makes a request per change.

Front end (Query Monitor, New Relic, Lighthouse):

  • No query per block instance (N+1): cache render output or query results in the object cache, with lean WP_Query args (Q18).
  • Stay edge-cacheable: no per-user output or cookies in the HTML; personalise with a small script or the Interactivity API.
  • Assets only where used: block.json registration does this. viewScriptModule loads deferred. Ship no front-end JS if CSS or <details> can do it.
  • Images: width and height, loading="lazy" below the fold, fetchpriority="high" for the hero.
In the mockYour points were right but generic ("what slows down any React app"). Name WordPress tools and WordPress causes: useSelect returning new objects, ServerSideRender, N+1 queries in render.php, cache-busting cookies.

I6.How do you build a block that holds other blocks, like an accordion or FAQ?

"With InnerBlocks. The parent uses useInnerBlocksProps to become a drop zone, limits it with allowedBlocks and a template, and saves the children with useInnerBlocksProps.save(). The child declares parent in block.json so it can only be inserted inside."

  • Edit: useInnerBlocksProps( useBlockProps(), { allowedBlocks, template, templateLock } ) puts the wrapper and the drop zone on one element. templateLock: "all" (nothing added, removed or moved), "insert" (blocks can only be moved, not added or removed) or "contentOnly" (only the text and media can change).
  • Save: <div { ...useInnerBlocksProps.save( useBlockProps.save() ) } />, or <InnerBlocks.Content />. A dynamic parent saves only <InnerBlocks.Content /> and render.php gets the children as $content.
  • Child: "parent": [ "news-uk/faq" ] (direct child only) or "ancestor" (anywhere inside).
  • Sharing settings: block context (I8).
In the mockYou said save returns "innerBlocks.children". It's <InnerBlocks.Content /> or useInnerBlocksProps.save(). You also didn't mention allowedBlocks, template or parent; they're the point of the question.
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/faq/, plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/faq-item/

An FAQ block that holds FAQ Items. The parent is static and uses useInnerBlocksProps with allowedBlocks and a template; each item declares "parent" so it can only go inside. The parent shares its heading level through block context: providesContext on the parent, usesContext on the item. Change the level once and every question follows, in the editor and on the front end, without the items being re-saved.

{
	"$schema": "https://schemas.wp.org/trunk/block.json",
	"apiVersion": 3,
	"name": "news-uk/faq",
	"title": "FAQ",
	"category": "text",
	"description": "A list of questions and answers. Holds FAQ Item blocks and tells them which heading level to use.",
	"attributes": {
		"headingLevel": { "type": "integer", "default": 3 }
	},
	"providesContext": {
		"news-uk/faqHeadingLevel": "headingLevel"
	},
	"supports": {
		"html": false,
		"color": { "background": true, "text": true },
		"spacing": { "padding": true }
	},
	"textdomain": "news-uk",
	"editorScript": "file:./index.js"
}
