	"apiVersion": 3,
	"name": "news-uk/faq-item",
	"title": "FAQ Item",
	"category": "text",
	"description": "One question and its answer. Only allowed inside an FAQ block.",
	"parent": [ "news-uk/faq" ],
	"attributes": {
		"question": { "type": "string", "default": "" }
	},
	"usesContext": [ "news-uk/faqHeadingLevel" ],
	"supports": { "html": false, "reusable": false },
	"textdomain": "news-uk",
	"editorScript": "file:./index.js",
	"render": "file:./render.php"
}
faq/index.js: InnerBlocks with allowedBlocks and a template plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/faq/index.js
/**
 * Mock interview: the parent of a nested block (FAQ holds FAQ Items).
 *
 * Two questions from the mock interview are answered here:
 *
 * 1. "How do you nest blocks (an accordion or FAQ)?"
 *    useInnerBlocksProps() turns the block's own element into a drop zone.
 *    allowedBlocks limits it to FAQ Items, and template gives a new FAQ two
 *    empty items. save() stores the children with useInnerBlocksProps.save().
 *
 * 2. "How does a parent share data with its children?"
 *    block.json "providesContext" maps a context name to one of this block's
 *    attributes: { "news-uk/faqHeadingLevel": "headingLevel" }. Any block
 *    inside it that lists the name in "usesContext" receives the value, in
 *    the editor (props.context) and in PHP ($block->context).
 *    The context itself is never saved anywhere: it is read from this block's
 *    headingLevel attribute, which is saved in this block's comment delimiter.
 */
import { registerBlockType } from '@wordpress/blocks';
import {
	InspectorControls,
	useBlockProps,
	useInnerBlocksProps,
} from '@wordpress/block-editor';
import { PanelBody, SelectControl } from '@wordpress/components';
import { __ } from '@wordpress/i18n';
import metadata from './block.json';

const ALLOWED_BLOCKS = [ 'news-uk/faq-item' ];
const TEMPLATE = [ [ 'news-uk/faq-item' ], [ 'news-uk/faq-item' ] ];

function Edit( { attributes, setAttributes } ) {
	// One element is both the block wrapper and the place children go.
	const innerBlocksProps = useInnerBlocksProps(
		useBlockProps( { className: 'news-uk-faq' } ),
		{ allowedBlocks: ALLOWED_BLOCKS, template: TEMPLATE }
	);

	return (
		<>
			<InspectorControls>
				<PanelBody title={ __( 'Questions', 'news-uk' ) }>
					<SelectControl
						__next40pxDefaultSize
						__nextHasNoMarginBottom
						label={ __( 'Heading level', 'news-uk' ) }
						help={ __(
							'Every question in this FAQ uses this level.',
							'news-uk'
						) }
						value={ String( attributes.headingLevel ) }
						options={ [ 2, 3, 4 ].map( ( level ) => ( {
							label: `H${ level }`,
							value: String( level ),
						} ) ) }
						onChange={ ( value ) =>
							setAttributes( { headingLevel: Number( value ) } )
						}
					/>
				</PanelBody>
			</InspectorControls>
			<div { ...innerBlocksProps } />
		</>
	);
}

registerBlockType( metadata.name, {
	edit: Edit,
	// Static parent: the wrapper and the children's saved HTML go into
	// post_content. Each child is dynamic, so its heading is built at render
	// time from context.
	save: () => (
		<div
			{ ...useInnerBlocksProps.save(
				useBlockProps.save( { className: 'news-uk-faq' } )
			) }
		/>
	),
} );
faq-item/index.js: props.context in the editor plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/faq-item/index.js
/**
 * Mock interview: the child of a nested block, reading its parent's context.
 *
 * - block.json "parent": [ "news-uk/faq" ] means the inserter only offers it
 *   inside an FAQ. ("ancestor" would allow it anywhere deeper in the tree.)
 * - block.json "usesContext" asks for the FAQ's heading level. The editor
 *   passes it in as props.context; render.php gets it as $block->context.
 * - The question is an attribute (JSON in the comment delimiter). The answer
 *   is inner blocks, saved as HTML between the delimiters.
 */
import { registerBlockType } from '@wordpress/blocks';
import {
	InnerBlocks,
	RichText,
	useBlockProps,
	useInnerBlocksProps,
} from '@wordpress/block-editor';
import { __ } from '@wordpress/i18n';
import metadata from './block.json';

const ANSWER_TEMPLATE = [
	[ 'core/paragraph', { placeholder: __( 'Answer…', 'news-uk' ) } ],
];
const ANSWER_BLOCKS = [ 'core/paragraph', 'core/list', 'core/image' ];

function Edit( { attributes, setAttributes, context } ) {
	// Fallback for when the item is rendered outside an FAQ (it can't be
	// inserted there, but content can be pasted or moved in code).
	const level = context[ 'news-uk/faqHeadingLevel' ] ?? 3;

	const blockProps = useBlockProps( { className: 'news-uk-faq-item' } );
	const answerProps = useInnerBlocksProps(
		{ className: 'news-uk-faq-item__answer' },
		{ allowedBlocks: ANSWER_BLOCKS, template: ANSWER_TEMPLATE }
	);

	return (
		<div { ...blockProps }>
			<RichText
				tagName={ `h${ level }` }
				className="news-uk-faq-item__question"
				placeholder={ __( 'Question…', 'news-uk' ) }
				allowedFormats={ [] }
				value={ attributes.question }
				onChange={ ( question ) => setAttributes( { question } ) }
			/>
			<div { ...answerProps } />
		</div>
	);
}

registerBlockType( metadata.name, {
	edit: Edit,
	// Dynamic block with children: save only the answer's blocks. render.php
	// receives them as $content and adds the heading from context.
	save: () => <InnerBlocks.Content />,
} );
faq-item/render.php: $block->context on the server plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/faq-item/render.php
<?php
/**
 * Server render for news-uk/faq-item.
 *
 * $block->context holds what the parent FAQ provides. WordPress builds it
 * while rendering: when it creates this block's WP_Block object, it copies
 * the parent's "providesContext" attributes into the child's context. Nothing
 * about context is stored in post_content.
 *
 * @var array    $attributes Block attributes.
 * @var string   $content    The answer: inner blocks, already rendered.
 * @var WP_Block $block      Block instance.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

// Context is missing when the item is used outside an FAQ, so fall back to
// h3. Clamp it so a bad value can't print <h99>.
$news_uk_level = (int) ( $block->context['news-uk/faqHeadingLevel'] ?? 3 );
$news_uk_level = min( 6, max( 2, $news_uk_level ) );

// RichText with no formats can still hold entities such as &amp;, so strip
// tags instead of esc_html(), which would double-encode them.
$news_uk_question = wp_kses( $attributes['question'] ?? '', [] );

// A question with no text is unfinished: render nothing.
if ( '' === trim( $news_uk_question ) ) {
	return;
}
?>
<div <?php echo get_block_wrapper_attributes( [ 'class' => 'news-uk-faq-item' ] ); // phpcs:ignore WordPress.Security.EscapeOutput.OutputNotEscaped -- Escaped by core. ?>>
	<h<?php echo (int) $news_uk_level; ?> class="news-uk-faq-item__question"><?php echo $news_uk_question; // phpcs:ignore WordPress.Security.EscapeOutput.OutputNotEscaped -- Passed through wp_kses() above. ?></h<?php echo (int) $news_uk_level; ?>>
	<div class="news-uk-faq-item__answer"><?php echo $content; // phpcs:ignore WordPress.Security.EscapeOutput.OutputNotEscaped -- Already-rendered inner blocks. ?></div>
</div>
PHPUnit: context flows down and is not saved plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/tests/class-faq-context-test.php
<?php
/**
 * Mock interview: block context flows from the FAQ to its items, and is
 * worked out at render time rather than stored.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

namespace News_UK_Practice\Tests;

use WP_UnitTestCase;

/**
 * FAQ block context tests.
 */
class Faq_Context_Test extends WP_UnitTestCase {

	/**
	 * Saved markup for an FAQ with one item. $attrs is the FAQ's JSON.
	 *
	 * @param string $attrs    FAQ attributes as JSON, or '' for none.
	 * @param string $question The question.
	 * @return string
	 */
	private function faq( $attrs, $question = 'Can I cancel any time?' ) {
		$open = '' === $attrs ? '<!-- wp:news-uk/faq -->' : '<!-- wp:news-uk/faq ' . $attrs . ' -->';
		return $open . '<div class="wp-block-news-uk-faq news-uk-faq">'
			. '<!-- wp:news-uk/faq-item ' . wp_json_encode( [ 'question' => $question ] ) . ' -->'
			. '<!-- wp:paragraph --><p>Yes, from your account page.</p><!-- /wp:paragraph -->'
			. '<!-- /wp:news-uk/faq-item -->'
			. '</div><!-- /wp:news-uk/faq -->';
	}

	/**
	 * The item uses the heading level the FAQ provides.
	 */
	public function test_item_reads_heading_level_from_parent_context() {
		$html = do_blocks( $this->faq( '{"headingLevel":2}' ) );

		$this->assertStringContainsString( '<h2 class="news-uk-faq-item__question">Can I cancel any time?</h2>', $html );
		$this->assertStringContainsString( '<p>Yes, from your account page.</p>', $html );
	}

	/**
	 * With no attribute saved, the parent's default (3) is what flows down.
	 */
	public function test_parent_default_flows_down() {
		$this->assertStringContainsString( '<h3 class="news-uk-faq-item__question">', do_blocks( $this->faq( '' ) ) );
	}

	/**
	 * Context is not stored: the item's saved markup only holds its question.
	 * Changing the FAQ's level changes every item without re-saving them.
	 */
	public function test_context_is_not_saved_in_the_child() {
		$blocks = parse_blocks( $this->faq( '{"headingLevel":4}' ) );
		$item   = $blocks[0]['innerBlocks'][0];

		$this->assertSame( 'news-uk/faq-item', $item['blockName'] );
		$this->assertSame( [ 'question' => 'Can I cancel any time?' ], $item['attrs'] );
		$this->assertStringContainsString( '<h4', do_blocks( $this->faq( '{"headingLevel":4}' ) ) );
	}

	/**
	 * Outside an FAQ there is no context, so the item falls back to h3.
	 */
	public function test_item_without_parent_falls_back() {
		$html = do_blocks( '<!-- wp:news-uk/faq-item {"question":"Lonely?"} --><!-- /wp:news-uk/faq-item -->' );

		$this->assertStringContainsString( '<h3 class="news-uk-faq-item__question">Lonely?</h3>', $html );
	}

	/**
	 * A bad level can't produce an invalid tag, and markup in the question is stripped.
	 */
	public function test_level_is_clamped_and_question_is_cleaned() {
		$html = do_blocks( $this->faq( '{"headingLevel":99}', 'Is <script>alert(1)</script>this safe?' ) );

		$this->assertStringContainsString( '<h6 class="news-uk-faq-item__question">Is alert(1)this safe?</h6>', $html );
		$this->assertStringNotContainsString( '<script>', $html );
	}

	/**
	 * Both blocks are registered from block.json with the context keys.
	 */
	public function test_registration_declares_context() {
		$registry = \WP_Block_Type_Registry::get_instance();

		$this->assertSame( [ 'news-uk/faqHeadingLevel' => 'headingLevel' ], $registry->get_registered( 'news-uk/faq' )->provides_context );
		$this->assertSame( [ 'news-uk/faqHeadingLevel' ], $registry->get_registered( 'news-uk/faq-item' )->uses_context );
		$this->assertSame( [ 'news-uk/faq' ], $registry->get_registered( 'news-uk/faq-item' )->parent );
	}
}
Playwright: change the parent, every child follows plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/e2e/faq.spec.js
/**
 * Mock interview: nested blocks and block context, in the real editor.
 *
 * Changing the FAQ's heading level re-renders every FAQ Item inside it, in
 * the editor and on the front end, although the items themselves don't change.
 */

/**
 * WordPress dependencies
 */
const { test, expect } = require( '@wordpress/e2e-test-utils-playwright' );

test.describe( 'FAQ (block context)', () => {
	test.afterEach( async ( { requestUtils } ) => {
		await requestUtils.deleteAllPosts();
	} );

	test( 'the parent heading level flows to every item', async ( {
		admin,
		editor,
		page,
	} ) => {
		await admin.createNewPost( { title: 'FAQ test' } );
		await editor.insertBlock( { name: 'news-uk/faq' } );

		// The template gives a new FAQ two items, each starting as an h3.
		const questions = editor.canvas.getByRole( 'textbox', {
			name: 'Question…',
		} );
		await expect( questions ).toHaveCount( 2 );
		await questions.nth( 0 ).fill( 'Can I cancel any time?' );
		await questions.nth( 1 ).fill( 'Do you deliver on Sundays?' );
		await expect(
			editor.canvas.locator( 'h3.news-uk-faq-item__question' )
		).toHaveCount( 2 );

		// Change the level once, on the parent.
		await editor.selectBlocks(
			editor.canvas.getByRole( 'document', {
				name: 'Block: FAQ',
				exact: true,
			} )
		);
		await editor.openDocumentSettingsSidebar();
		await page
			.getByRole( 'combobox', { name: 'Heading level' } )
			.selectOption( 'H2' );

		// Both children follow, but only the parent's attribute changed.
		await expect(
			editor.canvas.locator( 'h2.news-uk-faq-item__question' )
		).toHaveCount( 2 );
		await expect.poll( editor.getBlocks ).toMatchObject( [
			{
				name: 'news-uk/faq',
				attributes: { headingLevel: 2 },
				innerBlocks: [
					{ attributes: { question: 'Can I cancel any time?' } },
					{ attributes: { question: 'Do you deliver on Sundays?' } },
				],
			},
		] );

		const postId = await editor.publishPost();

		// render.php reads the same context on the server.
		await page.goto( `/?p=${ postId }` );
		await expect(
			page.getByRole( 'heading', {
				level: 2,
				name: 'Do you deliver on Sundays?',
			} )
		).toBeVisible();
	} );
} );

How it's tested: PHPUnit: 6 tests (the level flows down, the default flows down, context isn't in the child's saved JSON, the fallback outside an FAQ, clamping and cleaning, registration). Playwright: inserts an FAQ, changes the level on the parent, checks both questions become h2 in the editor and on the front end.

✓ Builds with wp-scripts✓ PHPUnit passes✓ Playwright passes (Chrome)

I7.Where are block attributes stored? Is the comment delimiter the only place?

"By default, as JSON in the block's comment delimiter in post_content. But an attribute with a source is read from the saved HTML instead, and data that must be queried or reused belongs in post meta, connected with Block Bindings."

  • Comment delimiter: <!-- wp:news-uk/faq {"headingLevel":2} -->. Values equal to the default aren't written.
  • Sourced from HTML: "source": "attribute" (with selector and attribute), "html", "rich-text", "text" or "query". The value is parsed out of the saved markup, so it isn't duplicated in the JSON. Core Image's url is read from the <img src>.
  • Post meta: through Block Bindings (6.5+, Q4). The old "source": "meta" attribute is deprecated.
  • Not stored: context (I9) and anything computed in render.php.
In the mockYou got the comment delimiter, then answered "programmatically at render time" for other places. The answer they wanted was sourced attributes read from the HTML.
In this repoM3 covers the pitfalls. The FAQ test checks the item's saved JSON holds only question.

I8.How do you share data between a parent block and its children?

"Block context. The parent maps a context name to one of its attributes with providesContext; any block inside it that lists that name in usesContext receives the value, in the editor and in PHP."

// Parent block.json
"providesContext": { "news-uk/faqHeadingLevel": "headingLevel" }
// Child block.json
"usesContext": [ "news-uk/faqHeadingLevel" ]
// Child edit:      props.context[ 'news-uk/faqHeadingLevel' ]
// Child render.php: $block->context['news-uk/faqHeadingLevel']
  • It works at any depth, not only for direct children. Core uses it for postId and postType in the Query Loop.
  • It only flows down the tree. For siblings or unrelated blocks, use a data store (I13).
  • Namespace the context name (news-uk/…) to avoid clashes.
In the mockThe key is providesContext (you said "share context"). The consumer side, usesContext, was right.
In this repoThe FAQ block (code under I6). PHPUnit and Playwright both check the level flows down.

I9.Where is that context data stored?

"It isn't stored. Context is worked out while the tree is rendered, from the parent's attributes, and those attributes are saved in the parent's comment delimiter. Change the parent and every child follows without being re-saved."

  • Editor: the block list wraps each block's children in a React context provider (BlockContextProvider) holding the values from its providesContext. Children read it as props.context.
  • Server: when WP_Block creates the child block objects, it passes the parent's provided values down as $block->context.
  • A child rendered on its own (outside the parent) gets no context, so always have a fallback.
In the mockYou guessed "JavaScript memory, not persistent", which is half right, but missed the server side and that it's derived from the parent's saved attributes. The panel was steering you there.
In this repotest_context_is_not_saved_in_the_child in tests/class-faq-context-test.php.
Remember it as"Saved on the parent, computed for the child."

I10.Inside edit.js, how do you fetch posts of a particular post type?

"With useSelect and the core data store: getEntityRecords( 'postType', 'book', query ). The post type needs show_in_rest. The data store fetches it through the REST API, caches it, and re-renders the block when it arrives."

import { useSelect } from '@wordpress/data';
import { store as coreStore } from '@wordpress/core-data';

const { books, isLoading } = useSelect( ( select ) => {
	const query = { per_page: 10, status: 'publish', _fields: 'id,title' };
	return {
		books: select( coreStore ).getEntityRecords( 'postType', 'book', query ),
		isLoading: ! select( coreStore ).hasFinishedResolution( 'getEntityRecords', [ 'postType', 'book', query ] ),
	};
}, [] );
  • One post: getEntityRecord( 'postType', 'book', id ). Shorter hook: useEntityRecords( 'postType', 'book', query ) returns { records, isResolving, hasResolved }.
  • It returns null until loaded, so render a Spinner first.
In the mockRight idea. The selector is getEntityRecords / getEntityRecord (you said "get entity type"), and the store is imported as store as coreStore from @wordpress/core-data.

I11.Why use the data store rather than calling the REST API directly?

"The store does the plumbing: it caches and de-duplicates requests, re-renders every component that reads the data when it changes, tracks loading state, and shows unsaved edits. With raw apiFetch I'd rebuild all of that, and my block wouldn't see edits made elsewhere in the editor."

  • Cache and de-duplication: ten blocks asking for post 5 make one request; the resolver runs once per set of arguments.
  • Reactivity: components re-render when the data they selected changes.
  • Loading and errors: isResolving, hasFinishedResolution, getResolutionError.
  • Edits: editEntityRecord then saveEditedEntityRecord, with undo/redo, and the post's Save button includes them.
  • Auth: the request goes through apiFetch, which adds the REST nonce (I36).
In this repoThe store test (code under I13) proves "two readers, one request".

State, React and TypeScript

I12.What is a Redux store?

"A single object that holds the application's state. The state is read through selectors and changed only by dispatching actions, which a pure reducer turns into the next state. That one-way flow makes changes predictable and easy to test."

  • Action: a plain object describing what happened: { type: 'RECEIVE_STANDFIRST', postId, standfirst }.
  • Reducer: ( state, action ) => newState, pure and immutable: no fetches, no mutation.
  • Selector: reads a piece of state. Subscribers re-render when what they read changes.
  • Side effects (fetching) live in middleware: thunks, or in WordPress, resolvers.
In the mockYou described it as a way to avoid prop drilling and mixed it up with React context. Lead with action, reducer, selector and one-way data flow.
Remember it as"Dispatch an action, reduce to new state, select what you need."

I13.Have you used @wordpress/data? How would you manage shared state across several blocks that depend on the same data?

"@wordpress/data is Redux plus a registry of named stores (core, core/editor, core/block-editor, core/notices), hooks to use them, and resolvers that fetch data when a selector first asks for it. For shared state across unrelated blocks I'd use an existing store if core already has the data, or register a small custom store with createReduxStore."

  • Read: useSelect( ( select ) => select( store ).getX( id ), [ id ] ). Write: const { setX } = useDispatch( store ).
  • Custom store: createReduxStore( name, { reducer, actions, selectors, resolvers } ) then register( store ). Actions can be thunks: ( args ) => async ( { dispatch, select } ) => { … }.
  • Which mechanism: parent to children: block context (I8). Unrelated blocks in the editor: a store. Data that must survive a reload: post meta or an entity, saved with the post. Shared state on the front end: Interactivity API state (Q5).
In the mockYou said "context API and the Redux store". Name the actual APIs: createReduxStore, register, useSelect, useDispatch, resolvers. And say what happens on reload.
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/data-store/

A small custom store, news-uk/standfirsts, over the Q14 REST route: a reducer, plain and thunk actions, a selector and a resolver. The test renders two unrelated components that read the same standfirst and proves they share one request and both update after one dispatch. That's "shared state across blocks" and "why a store instead of REST" in one file.

store.js: createReduxStore with reducer, actions, selectors, resolvers plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/data-store/store.js
/**
 * Mock interview: a custom @wordpress/data store.
 *
 * Questions it answers:
 * - "What is a Redux store?" One object holds the state. The only way to
 *   change it is to dispatch an action, which a pure reducer turns into the
 *   next state. Components read it through selectors and re-render when the
 *   part they read changes.
 * - "What is @wordpress/data?" Redux underneath, plus a registry of many
 *   named stores (core, core/editor, core/block-editor, ...), the
 *   useSelect / useDispatch hooks, and resolvers that fetch data the first
 *   time a selector asks for it.
 * - "How do two unrelated blocks share data?" Both read the same store.
 *   Context only flows from a parent to the blocks inside it; a store is
 *   global to the editor.
 * - "Why a store instead of calling the REST API?" The resolver runs once per
 *   argument, so ten blocks asking for post 5's standfirst make one request;
 *   every reader updates when the data changes; loading state comes free
 *   (isResolving, hasFinishedResolution).
 *
 * The data is the Q14 standfirst endpoint. For real post data you'd use the
 * core store instead (getEntityRecord), which already does all of this.
 * A custom store is for data core doesn't know about.
 *
 * Editor-only state is lost on reload. Anything that must persist goes back
 * to the server (saveStandfirst below), or into post meta.
 */
import { createReduxStore, register } from '@wordpress/data';
import apiFetch from '@wordpress/api-fetch';

export const STORE_NAME = 'news-uk/standfirsts';

const DEFAULT_STATE = { byPostId: {} };

// Reducer: (state, action) => new state. Pure: no fetches, no mutation.
export function reducer( state = DEFAULT_STATE, action ) {
	switch ( action.type ) {
		case 'RECEIVE_STANDFIRST':
			return {
				...state,
				byPostId: {
					...state.byPostId,
					[ action.postId ]: action.standfirst,
				},
			};
		default:
			return state;
	}
}

export const actions = {
	// A plain action: an object describing what happened.
	receiveStandfirst( postId, standfirst ) {
		return { type: 'RECEIVE_STANDFIRST', postId, standfirst };
	},

	// A thunk: an async action that can dispatch others. Saves to the server,
	// then stores what the server returned (sanitised there).
	saveStandfirst:
		( postId, standfirst ) =>
		async ( { dispatch } ) => {
			const response = await apiFetch( {
				path: `/news-uk/v1/standfirst/${ postId }`,
				method: 'POST',
				data: { standfirst },
			} );
			dispatch.receiveStandfirst( postId, response.standfirst );
		},
};

export const selectors = {
	getStandfirst( state, postId ) {
		return state.byPostId[ postId ];
	},
};

// Resolver: same name as the selector. Runs the first time
// getStandfirst( postId ) is selected for that postId, never again.
export const resolvers = {
	getStandfirst:
		( postId ) =>
		async ( { dispatch } ) => {
			const response = await apiFetch( {
				path: `/news-uk/v1/standfirst/${ postId }`,
			} );
			dispatch.receiveStandfirst( postId, response.standfirst );
		},
};

export const store = createReduxStore( STORE_NAME, {
	reducer,
	actions,
	selectors,
	resolvers,
} );

/**
 * Register on the default registry. A block's index.js would call this once;
 * tests use their own registry instead.
 */
export function registerStore() {
	register( store );
}
Jest: a fresh registry per test, apiFetch mocked plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/data-store/test/store.test.js
/**
 * Mock interview: testing a @wordpress/data store.
 *
 * - The reducer is a pure function: test it directly.
 * - Everything else runs through a fresh registry per test (createRegistry),
 *   so tests don't share state.
 * - apiFetch is mocked with jest.mock: no network, and we can count calls.
 *
 * The @wordpress packages are real npm packages, so Jest imports them
 * normally. Only the network (apiFetch) is mocked.
 */
import { createRegistry, RegistryProvider, useSelect } from '@wordpress/data';
import apiFetch from '@wordpress/api-fetch';
import { act, render, screen } from '@testing-library/react';
import { reducer, store } from '../store';

jest.mock( '@wordpress/api-fetch', () => jest.fn() );

function makeRegistry() {
	const registry = createRegistry();
	registry.register( store );
	return registry;
}

// Two "blocks" that know nothing about each other but read the same data.
function Teaser( { postId } ) {
	const standfirst = useSelect(
		( select ) => select( store ).getStandfirst( postId ),
		[ postId ]
	);
	return <p>Teaser: { standfirst ?? 'loading' }</p>;
}
function Sidebar( { postId } ) {
	const standfirst = useSelect(
		( select ) => select( store ).getStandfirst( postId ),
		[ postId ]
	);
	return <p>Sidebar: { standfirst ?? 'loading' }</p>;
}

beforeEach( () => {
	apiFetch.mockReset();
} );

test( 'the reducer returns new state and never mutates the old one', () => {
	const before = { byPostId: { 1: 'Old' } };
	const after = reducer( before, {
		type: 'RECEIVE_STANDFIRST',
		postId: 2,
		standfirst: 'New',
	} );

	expect( after ).toEqual( { byPostId: { 1: 'Old', 2: 'New' } } );
	expect( before ).toEqual( { byPostId: { 1: 'Old' } } );
} );

test( 'the resolver fetches once, however many times it is selected', async () => {
	apiFetch.mockResolvedValue( { id: 5, standfirst: 'Polls close at 10pm.' } );
	const registry = makeRegistry();

	await registry.resolveSelect( store ).getStandfirst( 5 );
	await registry.resolveSelect( store ).getStandfirst( 5 );

	expect( registry.select( store ).getStandfirst( 5 ) ).toBe(
		'Polls close at 10pm.'
	);
	expect( apiFetch ).toHaveBeenCalledTimes( 1 );
	expect( apiFetch ).toHaveBeenCalledWith( {
		path: '/news-uk/v1/standfirst/5',
	} );
} );

test( 'two unrelated components share the data and both update', async () => {
	apiFetch.mockResolvedValue( { id: 5, standfirst: 'First version' } );
	const registry = makeRegistry();

	render(
		<RegistryProvider value={ registry }>
			<Teaser postId={ 5 } />
			<Sidebar postId={ 5 } />
		</RegistryProvider>
	);

	expect( await screen.findByText( 'Teaser: First version' ) ).toBeVisible();
	expect( screen.getByText( 'Sidebar: First version' ) ).toBeVisible();
	expect( apiFetch ).toHaveBeenCalledTimes( 1 ); // Two readers, one request.

	// One dispatch, and every reader re-renders.
	act( () => {
		registry.dispatch( store ).receiveStandfirst( 5, 'Updated' );
	} );
	expect( screen.getByText( 'Teaser: Updated' ) ).toBeVisible();
	expect( screen.getByText( 'Sidebar: Updated' ) ).toBeVisible();
} );

test( 'saveStandfirst posts to the server and stores what it returns', async () => {
	apiFetch.mockResolvedValue( { id: 5, standfirst: 'Trimmed by server' } );
	const registry = makeRegistry();

	await registry
		.dispatch( store )
		.saveStandfirst( 5, '  Trimmed by server  ' );

	expect( apiFetch ).toHaveBeenCalledWith( {
		path: '/news-uk/v1/standfirst/5',
		method: 'POST',
		data: { standfirst: '  Trimmed by server  ' },
	} );
	expect( registry.select( store ).getStandfirst( 5 ) ).toBe(
		'Trimmed by server'
	);
} );

How it's tested: 4 Jest tests: the reducer is pure, the resolver fetches once, two readers share one request and both re-render, and the thunk saves then stores the server's value.

✓ Jest passes✓ ESLint clean

I14.Which React hooks do you use most, and what for?

"useState for local state, useEffect to sync with something outside React, useMemo and useCallback to keep expensive values and callbacks stable, useRef for DOM nodes and values that shouldn't re-render. In blocks, mostly useSelect, useDispatch and useBlockProps."

  • useState / useReducer: component state; useReducer when updates depend on each other.
  • useEffect: synchronise with an external system (a subscription, a timer, a non-React widget), with a cleanup. Not for reacting to events or computing derived values.
  • useMemo / useCallback: I15. useRef: DOM access, and mutable values like a timer ID. useContext: read context. useLayoutEffect: measure the DOM before paint.
  • Custom hooks to share logic, e.g. useDebouncedValue in Q20.
In the mockYour example, "an event happens, so set state inside a useEffect", is a known anti-pattern. Update state in the event handler; useEffect is for syncing with things outside React. Also don't call hooks "for side effects" in general: most hooks aren't.

I15.What's the difference between useMemo and useCallback?

"useMemo caches the result of a function; useCallback caches the function itself. useCallback( fn, deps ) is the same as useMemo( () => fn, deps ). Both only recompute when a dependency changes."

  • useMemo: an expensive calculation, or an object/array you pass to a memoised child or a dependency list.
  • useCallback: a handler passed to a React.memo child, or used in another hook's dependencies.
  • Not everywhere: memoising has its own cost. Profile first. (The React Compiler can add it automatically.)
Remember it as"Memo keeps the answer, callback keeps the function."

I16.In TypeScript, what's the difference between a type and an interface?

"For object shapes they're interchangeable. An interface can be reopened and merged, which is how you extend a library's types. A type alias can name anything, not just objects: unions, tuples, primitives, and computed types like mapped and conditional types."

  • Both: describe objects; can be extended (interface B extends A, type B = A & { … }); classes can implement both.
  • Only interface: declaration merging. Declare it twice and the fields combine (module augmentation).
  • Only type: 'draft' | 'publish', [ number, string ], { [ K in keyof T ]?: T[ K ] }, T extends U ? X : Y.
  • Habit: interface for object shapes others extend (props, public APIs), type for everything else.
In the mockYou guessed that interfaces allow computation and types are strict. It's the other way round: types do the computing (mapped, conditional); interfaces are the ones that can be reopened.
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/typescript/

Each difference as code. Lines marked @ts-expect-error must fail to compile; if one ever compiled, tsc would report an unused directive and fail.

type-vs-interface.ts plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/typescript/type-vs-interface.ts
/**
 * Mock interview: "What is the difference between a type and an interface?"
 *
 * Short answer: both describe object shapes and are interchangeable for that.
 * An interface can be reopened and merged (useful for extending a library's
 * types). A type alias can name any type, not just objects: unions, tuples,
 * and computed (mapped and conditional) types.
 *
 * Every @ts-expect-error below marks a line tsc must reject. If one stopped
 * being an error, `npm run typecheck` would fail.
 */

// 1. Both describe the same object shape.
interface ArticleShape {
	id: number;
	title: string;
}
type ArticleAlias = {
	id: number;
	title: string;
};
const fromInterface: ArticleShape = { id: 1, title: 'Budget day' };
const fromAlias: ArticleAlias = fromInterface; // Same shape, so assignable.

// 2. Both can be extended: interfaces with `extends`, types with `&`.
interface LiveArticle extends ArticleShape {
	updatedAt: string;
}
type LiveArticleAlias = ArticleAlias & { updatedAt: string };
const live: LiveArticle = { ...fromAlias, updatedAt: '2026-11-17T10:15' };
const liveToo: LiveArticleAlias = live;

// 3. Only interfaces merge. Declaring the same name twice adds the fields.
//    This is how you add a field to a library's interface (augmentation).
interface BlockSettings {
	title: string;
}
interface BlockSettings {
	icon: string;
}
const settings: BlockSettings = { title: 'FAQ', icon: 'editor-help' };

// @ts-expect-error A type alias can't be declared twice.
type Sizes = { small: number }; // eslint-disable-line @typescript-eslint/no-unused-vars
// @ts-expect-error Duplicate identifier.
type Sizes = { large: number };

// 4. Only type aliases can name unions, tuples and primitives.
type PostStatus = 'draft' | 'publish' | 'private';
type IdAndTitle = [ number, string ];
const status: PostStatus = 'publish';
// @ts-expect-error 'published' is not one of the allowed values.
const typo: PostStatus = 'published';
const pair: IdAndTitle = [ 1, 'Budget day' ];

// 5. Only type aliases can compute new types from old ones.
// Mapped type: every field optional and read-only.
type Draft< T > = { readonly [ K in keyof T ]?: T[ K ] };
// Conditional type with infer: pull out the type of `id`.
type IdOf< T > = T extends { id: infer I } ? I : never;

const draft: Draft< ArticleShape > = { title: 'Untitled' };
// @ts-expect-error Draft fields are read-only.
draft.title = 'Changed';
const id: IdOf< ArticleShape > = 42;
// @ts-expect-error The id of an ArticleShape is a number.
const badId: IdOf< ArticleShape > = '42';

// Rule of thumb: interface for object shapes you or others extend (props,
// public APIs); type for unions, tuples and anything computed.

export { liveToo, settings, status, typo, pair, draft, id, badId };

How it's tested: npm run typecheck passes.

✓ tsc --noEmit passes✓ ESLint clean

I17.Do you have experience building blocks with TypeScript?

"Yes. wp-scripts compiles .ts and .tsx with no extra config, and I run tsc --noEmit in CI for the type check. I type the attributes once and use them in BlockEditProps, so a typo in an attribute name fails the build."

  • The @wordpress packages ship their own types (some still need @types/wordpress__*).
  • Babel strips types without checking them, so the separate tsc --noEmit step is what catches errors.
  • Type the edges: block attributes, REST responses, store state and selectors.
In this repoQ21 (Reading Time block in TSX, with @ts-expect-error checks) and the type-vs-interface file above. npm run typecheck.

I18.Out of 10, how would you rate yourself in React and in TypeScript?

Give the number, then one piece of evidence for each, and stop. For example: "React 8: I've written it in every role, including a team of seven on the Woo Marketplace. TypeScript 7: I use it to type props, attributes and API data; I'm less practised at advanced generics, and I'm working on that."

  • Have one concrete thing to show for each (this repo: Q20 for React, Q21 and I16 for TypeScript).

Themes and Full Site Editing

I19.What's the difference between FSE and a block theme?

"A block theme is a kind of theme: HTML templates made of blocks, template parts and theme.json. Full Site Editing is the set of features that lets people edit the whole site with blocks, mainly the Site Editor. A block theme is what turns those features on."

  • Block theme: templates/*.html, parts/*.html, theme.json, patterns/. wp_is_block_theme() is true when templates/index.html exists.
  • FSE: the Site Editor, global styles, template and template-part editing, the navigation block.
In the mockYou said a block theme "has block.json". It's theme.json. block.json belongs to blocks. This slip came up again in the next answer, so practise saying "theme.json" for themes.
In this repoQ6, and themes/news-uk-theme/.

I20.Can a classic theme use theme.json?

"Yes, since WordPress 5.8. A classic theme can add theme.json to define colour palettes, font sizes, spacing and layout widths for the block editor, and to turn editor controls on or off, while keeping PHP templates."

  • It's a common middle step when moving a classic theme to blocks: design tokens first, templates later.
  • Classic themes can also opt in to block template parts (add_theme_support( 'block-template-parts' ), 6.1).
  • What they don't get without block templates: the full Site Editor for templates.
In the mockThe question said "block.json"; it meant theme.json (it followed your slip). Your answer was right in spirit. If a question sounds off, check: "Do you mean theme.json?"

I21.What are the most useful settings in theme.json?

"settings defines what editors can choose (palettes, font sizes, spacing, layout widths) and which controls exist; styles applies the defaults to the site, elements and blocks. Each preset becomes a CSS custom property, so the whole site uses the same tokens."

  • settings: color.palette, typography.fontSizes and fontFamilies, spacing.spacingSizes, layout.contentSize / wideSize, appearanceTools.
  • Locking down for editors: color.custom: false, color.defaultPalette: false, typography.customFontSize: false, and per-block settings under settings.blocks.
  • styles: site-wide, styles.elements (link, button, h1–h6), styles.blocks, and style variations.
  • Also: customTemplates, templateParts (with area), version, $schema.
In the mockYou named styles, tokens and palettes. Add locking controls (custom colours off, per-block settings): for a publisher with a strict brand, that's usually the most important part.

I22.What's the difference between theme.json version 2 and version 3?

"Version 3 arrived in WordPress 6.6. The main change is to default presets: two new settings, defaultFontSizes and defaultSpacingSizes, which default to true. In v3, core's default font sizes and spacing sizes show alongside the theme's and win when the slugs clash, unless you turn them off."

  • In v2, a theme that defined its own fontSizes with core's slugs (small, medium, large…) overrode core's values. In v3 the defaults are protected unless defaultFontSizes / defaultSpacingSizes is false.
  • In v3, spacingSizes is merged with the sizes generated by spacingScale instead of replacing them.
  • Migrating: set "version": 3. If the theme defines font sizes or spacing sizes, add "defaultFontSizes": false and "defaultSpacingSizes": false to keep the v2 look. That's what core's own v2-to-v3 migration does for old files.
  • Check in the editor that the size pickers show only what you expect, and compare the generated CSS variables before and after.
In the mockYou didn't know it. This one is likely to come up again: they said they migrate themes and blocks.
Remember it as"v3 keeps core's sizes unless you say no."

I23.You changed single.html in a block theme and deployed it, but production still shows the old template. Why, and how do you debug it?

"Most likely someone customised that template in the Site Editor. The customised copy is saved in the database as a wp_template post, and it always beats the theme file. After that I'd check caches and which template WordPress actually picked."

  1. Database override: Appearance → Editor → Templates shows "Customized" templates. Check with wp post list --post_type=wp_template --fields=ID,post_name,post_modified. Fix: "Reset" in the Site Editor (deletes the DB copy), or copy the editor's change into the theme file first if it should be kept.
  2. A more specific template wins: single-post.html, single-post-{slug}.html, or a custom template assigned to that post beats single.html. Query Monitor's "Template" panel shows which file or post was used.
  3. Child theme: a child theme's single.html beats the parent's.
  4. Caches: the edge/page cache (purge the URL), the object cache, a CDN serving old CSS.
  5. Deploy: did it reach this environment and the active theme?
In the mockYou went straight to caching. The answer they wanted is the database copy from the Site Editor, and their next question (I25) confirmed it. Say that first.
Remember it as"Database first, then specificity, then caches."

I24.What's the difference between a block pattern and a synced pattern?

"A normal pattern is a starting point: inserting it copies its blocks into the post, and later changes to the pattern don't affect copies already used. A synced pattern is one shared piece of content: it's stored once as a wp_block post, posts only reference it, and editing it updates it everywhere."

  • Normal: registered in code (register_block_pattern() or the theme's patterns/ folder) or created unsynced in the editor. After insertion it's ordinary blocks in that post.
  • Synced (formerly "reusable blocks"): stored in wp_block; the post holds <!-- wp:block {"ref":123} /-->.
  • Pattern overrides (6.6+): parts of a synced pattern can be marked overridable, so each copy keeps the shared layout but has its own text or image.
In the mockRight, apart from one detail: an unsynced pattern's copy is saved in the post's content, not as its own database record.
In this repoM6, and the "Key points" pattern in Q7.

I25.How does FSE resolve the template hierarchy when a user customises templates in the Site Editor?

"Two steps. First the normal hierarchy picks the most specific slug that exists, for example single-post-hello, then single-post, then single, then singular, then index. Then, for that slug, a user-customised copy in the database beats the child theme's file, which beats the parent theme's file, which beats a template registered by a plugin."

  • Specificity comes first: a theme file single-post.html still beats a customised single.html in the database.
  • Customised templates are wp_template posts linked to the theme by the wp_theme taxonomy, so switching themes hides them.
  • Template parts work the same way (wp_template_part). Plugins register templates with register_block_template() (6.7+).
In the mockThe order (database, child, parent) was right. Add the first step: slug specificity is decided before the source.

Testing

I26.What's your approach to covering blocks and plugins with tests?

"I test logic where it lives: PHPUnit for PHP (render.php, REST routes, hooks), Jest for JavaScript (save, edit, stores), and a few Playwright tests for the journeys that matter, from the editor inserting the block to a reader seeing it. Static analysis runs before all of them, and CI runs everything on every pull request."

In this repoQ11 (PHPUnit for a dynamic block), Q13 (where the logic lives), Q22 (Jest + RTL), Q23 (Playwright).
In the mockA good answer. In the real interview, follow it with one concrete example on screen, e.g. npm run test:php -- --filter Article_Teaser_Test.

I27.What's the difference between a unit test and an integration test? How do you load WordPress in tests?

"A unit test checks one piece of code on its own, with its dependencies replaced, so it's fast and pinpoints failures. An integration test checks that pieces work together for real. In WordPress, WP_UnitTestCase loads WordPress and a test database, so despite the name, those are integration tests."

  • Loading WordPress: the core test library (the wp-phpunit/wp-phpunit Composer package, or wp scaffold plugin-tests), a wp-tests-config.php pointing at a separate database, and a bootstrap that loads your plugin on muplugins_loaded before WordPress boots. Each test runs in a transaction that's rolled back.
  • Without WordPress: a plain PHPUnit TestCase for pure logic, with WordPress functions stubbed by Brain Monkey or WP_Mock.
  • Rule of thumb: pure logic gets unit tests; code whose correctness depends on WordPress (queries, hooks, REST) gets integration tests.
In this repoQ10 (setup), Q12 (a pure unit test), and class-paywall-unit-test.php below (unit test with mocks, no WordPress).

I28.Explain the testing pyramid.

"Many fast unit tests at the base, fewer integration tests in the middle, and a few slow end-to-end tests at the top. Moving up, each test costs more to write, run and maintain, but gives more confidence that the whole thing works."

  • Static analysis (PHPCS, ESLint, tsc) sits underneath as the cheapest check of all. Kent C. Dodds' "testing trophy" draws it as the base.
  • The anti-pattern is the "ice cream cone": mostly manual and E2E tests, few unit tests. Slow, flaky, and failures don't say where the bug is.
  • CI runs them cheapest first, so a lint error fails in seconds.
In the mockYou described it upside down: static checks at the top and E2E at the bottom. The base is the many cheap unit tests; the top is the few E2E tests. This one matters: they focus on testing.
In this repoQ12. npm test runs lint, Jest, PHPUnit, then Playwright, in that order.
Remember it as"Wide and cheap at the bottom, narrow and slow at the top."

I29.What are setUp and tearDown used for?

"setUp runs before every test method and tearDown after every one, so each test starts from the same state and doesn't leak into the next. For fixtures that are expensive and shared, there are once-per-class versions."

  • WordPress spelling: set_up() / tear_down() (snake case, from the PHPUnit polyfills). Always call parent::set_up().
  • Once per class: wpSetUpBeforeClass( $factory ) creates shared posts and users once; WordPress deletes them after the class.
  • What WP_UnitTestCase already undoes: the database (each test is a rolled-back transaction) and hooks. So you don't need to delete posts in tear_down.
  • What you must undo: superglobals ($_SERVER, $_GET), other globals, registered blocks or routes, files, static properties.
In the mockYou said the post "persists while the other test functions run and is deleted when testing is done". That describes wpSetUpBeforeClass, not setUp. setUp runs before each test. And WordPress rolls back the database for you.
In this repoclass-rest-auth-test.php: wpSetUpBeforeClass makes one editor for all tests, set_up() installs a spy REST server, and tear_down() resets the $_SERVER nonce header and globals the rollback doesn't touch.

I30.What are mocks for? Give an example, and which packages have you used?

"A mock replaces a dependency of the code under test with a controlled fake, so the test is fast, deterministic and only fails for bugs in my code. A stub just returns canned answers; a mock also checks how it was called."

  • Example: a Paywall depends on a Subscription_Service that calls a remote API. Stub it to return true, then false, to test both branches; use a mock with expects( $this->never() ) to prove free articles don't call the API at all.
  • The vocabulary (test doubles): dummy, stub, spy, mock, fake.
  • PHP: PHPUnit's createStub() / createMock(), Mockery, Brain Monkey and WP_Mock (stub WordPress functions and hooks). In integration tests, WordPress's own seams: pre_http_request for HTTP (Q25, Q35).
  • JavaScript: jest.fn(), jest.mock(), jest.spyOn(), Sinon, MSW for network calls.
  • Don't over-mock: mocking what you don't own (WordPress internals) can test the mock, not the code. Prefer integration tests there.
In the mockThe explanation was good. The PHP packages you couldn't remember: Mockery, Brain Monkey, WP_Mock. And the JS one is Sinon (you said "signon").
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/

The "class B depends on class A" example from the interview, as real code. Paywall receives a Subscription_Service in its constructor, so a test can hand it a fake. The test is a plain PHPUnit TestCase: no WordPress, no database.

class-paywall.php: the dependency is injected plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/class-paywall.php
class Paywall {

	/**
	 * Subscriptions.
	 *
	 * @var Subscription_Service
	 */
	private $subscriptions;

	/**
	 * Constructor.
	 *
	 * @param Subscription_Service $subscriptions Subscriptions.
	 */
	public function __construct( Subscription_Service $subscriptions ) {
		$this->subscriptions = $subscriptions;
	}

	/**
	 * Whether a user can read an article.
	 *
	 * Free articles are open to everyone, and don't call the service at all
	 * (it's a remote API, so that matters). Premium articles need a logged-in
	 * subscriber.
	 *
	 * @param bool $is_premium Whether the article is premium.
	 * @param int  $user_id    User ID, 0 when logged out.
	 * @return bool
	 */
	public function can_read( bool $is_premium, int $user_id ): bool {
		if ( ! $is_premium ) {
			return true;
		}
		if ( 0 === $user_id ) {
			return false;
		}
		return $this->subscriptions->is_subscriber( $user_id );
	}
}
PHPUnit: stubs for answers, mocks for calls plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/tests/class-paywall-unit-test.php
<?php
/**
 * Mock interview: mocking a dependency ("class B depends on class A").
 *
 * A plain PHPUnit TestCase: no WordPress, no database, runs in milliseconds.
 * The Paywall is real; the Subscription_Service it depends on is a test
 * double made by PHPUnit.
 *
 * - A STUB only returns canned answers: createStub()->method()->willReturn().
 * - A MOCK also checks how it was called: expects( $this->once() ), never().
 *
 * For code that calls WordPress functions directly (get_option() and so on)
 * without loading WordPress, Brain Monkey or WP_Mock stub those functions.
 * In WP_UnitTestCase tests, use WordPress's own seams instead, such as the
 * pre_http_request filter for HTTP calls (Q25, Q35).
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

namespace News_UK_Practice\Tests;

use News_UK_Practice\Mock_Interview\Paywall;
use News_UK_Practice\Mock_Interview\Subscription_Service;
use PHPUnit\Framework\TestCase;

/**
 * Paywall unit tests.
 */
class Paywall_Unit_Test extends TestCase {

	/**
	 * Premium article, subscriber: the stubbed service says yes.
	 */
	public function test_subscriber_can_read_premium() {
		$service = $this->createStub( Subscription_Service::class );
		$service->method( 'is_subscriber' )->willReturn( true );

		$this->assertTrue( ( new Paywall( $service ) )->can_read( true, 42 ) );
	}

	/**
	 * Premium article, not a subscriber: the stubbed service says no.
	 */
	public function test_non_subscriber_cannot_read_premium() {
		$service = $this->createStub( Subscription_Service::class );
		$service->method( 'is_subscriber' )->willReturn( false );

		$this->assertFalse( ( new Paywall( $service ) )->can_read( true, 42 ) );
	}

	/**
	 * The mock checks the service is asked once, about the right user.
	 */
	public function test_asks_the_service_about_the_right_user() {
		$service = $this->createMock( Subscription_Service::class );
		$service->expects( $this->once() )
			->method( 'is_subscriber' )
			->with( 42 )
			->willReturn( true );

		( new Paywall( $service ) )->can_read( true, 42 );
	}

	/**
	 * Free articles, and logged-out readers, never cost a call to the service.
	 */
	public function test_does_not_call_the_service_when_it_does_not_need_to() {
		$service = $this->createMock( Subscription_Service::class );
		$service->expects( $this->never() )->method( 'is_subscriber' );

		$paywall = new Paywall( $service );
		$this->assertTrue( $paywall->can_read( false, 42 ) );
		$this->assertFalse( $paywall->can_read( true, 0 ) );
	}
}

How it's tested: 4 tests, under a millisecond each. expects( $this->never() ) proves free articles never call the remote service.

✓ PHPUnit passes✓ PHPCS (WordPress-Extra + VIP-Go) clean

I31.How do you test the JavaScript side of a block?

"Jest, which comes with wp-scripts (npm run test:unit), with React Testing Library and user-event for components. I test save() output and deprecations with the @wordpress/blocks helpers, and the edit component by rendering it and acting like a user."

  • Queries by role and label (getByRole( 'button', { name } )), not class names.
  • createBlock, getSaveContent, parse, serialize for save and deprecations (Q3, Q13).
  • Front-end scripts: Jest on the DOM (jsdom), or Playwright for real behaviour.
In this repoQ22 (edit component), Q3 and Q13 (save and deprecations), the store test under I13.

I32.In Jest, the WordPress packages aren't available like they are in the editor. How do you test a component that uses them?

"They are available: the @wordpress packages are normal npm packages, so I install them as dependencies and Jest imports the real code. What doesn't exist in Jest is the editor around them: the global wp object, registered stores, the REST API. So I mock just those edges."

  • Why the confusion: in the browser, webpack turns @wordpress/* imports into the global wp.* (they're externals). In Jest there's no global, but the import resolves to node_modules.
  • Mock the edges: jest.mock( '@wordpress/data' ) to control useSelect (Q22); jest.mock( '@wordpress/api-fetch' ) for network; ServerSideRender.
  • Or use the real thing: a fresh createRegistry() per test with your store registered, wrapped in RegistryProvider.
  • Components like SelectControl render for real in jsdom, so test them through their labels. jsdom gaps (ResizeObserver, TextEncoder) go in a setup file.
In the mockYou accepted the premise and went to mocking everything. Push back politely: the packages are installable; only the runtime is missing.
In this repotests/js/setup-globals.js (jsdom gaps), Q22 (mocked useSelect), the store test (real registry, mocked apiFetch).

I33.Which tools have you used for end-to-end tests?

"Playwright, with @wordpress/e2e-test-utils-playwright: that's what Gutenberg itself uses, and it handles login, creating posts over REST and the editor's iframe. I've also used Cypress and Puppeteer, and Testim for codeless tests."

  • Others worth knowing: Codeception with wp-browser (PHP, WordPress-aware), WebdriverIO, Selenium, Behat.
  • Set up data by API, act through the UI, check as a reader (Q23).
In the mockYou couldn't name the third tool. Have a list ready, and lead with Playwright plus the WordPress utils.

I34.Have you used visual regression testing (VRT) tools?

"VRT takes screenshots of pages or components and compares them with approved baselines, failing when pixels change beyond a threshold. Playwright has it built in with toHaveScreenshot(); BackstopJS, Percy, Chromatic and Applitools are the common dedicated tools."

  • Making it stable: fixed viewport and fonts, disable animations, mask dynamic content (dates, ads), run in the same Docker image as the baselines (rendering differs across operating systems).
  • Workflow: a failing diff is reviewed; if the change is intended, update the baseline in the same pull request.
  • Good for CSS and theme.json changes that unit tests can't see.
In this repoM25 has more detail.

I35.How would you run the tests on every commit and every pull request?

"On commit, a fast pre-commit hook with husky and lint-staged that lints only the staged files. On every pull request, CI (GitHub Actions) runs everything: lint, Jest, PHPUnit against a real database, and Playwright, with branch protection so a failing check blocks the merge."

  • Keep the commit hook to seconds (lint, maybe jest --findRelatedTests), or people skip it with --no-verify. Heavier checks can go in pre-push.
  • CI: services for MySQL (and Memcached), dependency caching, a PHP version matrix if you support several, Playwright traces and reports uploaded as artifacts on failure.
  • Required status checks in branch protection make it a real gate.
In this repo.husky/pre-commit, bin/phpcs-staged.sh, .github/workflows/ci.yml (Q9).

REST API, authentication and security

I36.How are REST API requests from the block editor authenticated?

"With the login cookie plus a nonce. The browser sends the logged-in cookie automatically, and apiFetch adds an X-WP-Nonce header with a nonce for the wp_rest action. WordPress only trusts the cookie if the nonce is valid; then each route's permission_callback checks what the user may do."

  • Authentication (who are you): cookie + nonce here; application passwords for other systems (I39).
  • Authorisation (are you allowed): permission_callback with current_user_can( 'edit_post', $id ). Return 401 when not logged in, 403 when not allowed (Q14).
  • Core sets up the nonce for apiFetch in the editor (createNonceMiddleware) and refreshes it through the Heartbeat API.
In the mockYou were right about authentication vs authorisation. Lead with "cookie plus nonce, sent by apiFetch", which is what was asked.

I37.What is a nonce, and how do you pass it to the REST API?

"A token that proves a request came from our own page, to stop cross-site request forgery. For REST, create it with wp_create_nonce( 'wp_rest' ) and send it as the X-WP-Nonce header (or a _wpnonce parameter). Without it, WordPress treats a cookie request as logged out; with a bad one, it returns 403."

  • Not really "once": a WordPress nonce is valid for 12 to 24 hours and is tied to the user, the action and the login session.
  • From your own script: pass it to the page (wp_add_inline_script or wp_localize_script), then fetch( url, { headers: { 'X-WP-Nonce': nonce } } ). With @wordpress/api-fetch on the front end, add apiFetch.createNonceMiddleware( nonce ).
  • Nonces protect logged-in actions. They aren't authentication: for logged-out readers they protect nothing.
  • Custom admin-ajax or form handlers check with check_ajax_referer() / wp_verify_nonce(); for REST, core checks wp_rest for you.
In the mockClose. The action is wp_rest and the header is X-WP-Nonce. Name both.
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/tests/

No new feature here: the tests pin down how core behaves, so every claim in I36–I42 is checked against WordPress 6.9 rather than memory.

class-rest-auth-test.php: nonces, permission_callback, application passwords, password hashing plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/tests/class-rest-auth-test.php
<?php
/**
 * Mock interview: how REST authentication really works in core.
 *
 * - A route with no permission_callback is public, and WordPress logs a
 *   _doing_it_wrong notice (since 5.5).
 * - Cookie auth needs a "wp_rest" nonce. Without one, core treats the request
 *   as logged out; with a bad one, it returns 403.
 * - Application passwords are stored hashed in user meta and only work on
 *   API requests.
 * - User passwords are hashed with bcrypt since 6.8 (phpass before).
 *
 * Also shows set_up() / tear_down(): the test changes globals that the
 * database rollback doesn't undo, so tear_down() puts them back.
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

namespace News_UK_Practice\Tests;

use WP_Application_Passwords;
use WP_REST_Request;
use WP_UnitTestCase;

/**
 * REST authentication tests.
 */
class Rest_Auth_Test extends WP_UnitTestCase {

	/**
	 * Editor user ID, created once for the whole class.
	 *
	 * @var int
	 */
	private static $editor_id;

	/**
	 * Runs once before the first test. Fixtures made here are shared by every
	 * test and deleted automatically after the last one.
	 *
	 * @param \WP_UnitTest_Factory $factory Factory.
	 */
	public static function wpSetUpBeforeClass( $factory ) {
		self::$editor_id = $factory->user->create(
			[
				'role'       => 'editor',
				'user_login' => 'nonce_editor',
			]
		);
	}

	/**
	 * Runs before every test.
	 */
	public function set_up() {
		parent::set_up();
		// A spy server records headers instead of calling header().
		$GLOBALS['wp_rest_server'] = new \Spy_REST_Server();
	}

	/**
	 * Runs after every test. The DB transaction is rolled back and hooks are
	 * restored by the parent, but superglobals and other globals are ours to
	 * reset.
	 */
	public function tear_down() {
		unset( $_SERVER['HTTP_X_WP_NONCE'], $_REQUEST['_wpnonce'], $GLOBALS['wp_rest_auth_cookie'], $GLOBALS['wp_rest_server'] );
		parent::tear_down();
	}

	/**
	 * No permission_callback: the route still works for anyone, with a notice.
	 */
	public function test_route_without_permission_callback_is_public_with_a_notice() {
		$this->setExpectedIncorrectUsage( 'register_rest_route' );

		add_action(
			'rest_api_init',
			function () {
				// Deliberately missing permission_callback.
				register_rest_route(
					'news-uk-test/v1',
					'/open',
					[
						'methods'  => 'GET',
						'callback' => fn() => [ 'ok' => true ],
					]
				);
			}
		);
		do_action( 'rest_api_init', rest_get_server() );

		wp_set_current_user( 0 );
		$response = rest_do_request( new WP_REST_Request( 'GET', '/news-uk-test/v1/open' ) );

		$this->assertSame( 200, $response->get_status() );
	}

	/**
	 * Logged in by cookie but no nonce: core acts as if logged out (CSRF guard).
	 */
	public function test_cookie_without_nonce_is_treated_as_logged_out() {
		wp_set_current_user( self::$editor_id );
		$GLOBALS['wp_rest_auth_cookie'] = true; // What a valid auth cookie sets.

		$this->assertTrue( rest_cookie_check_errors( null ) );
		$this->assertSame( 0, get_current_user_id() );
	}

	/**
	 * A valid wp_rest nonce in X-WP-Nonce keeps the user, and core sends a fresh one.
	 */
	public function test_cookie_with_valid_nonce_keeps_the_user() {
		wp_set_current_user( self::$editor_id );
		$GLOBALS['wp_rest_auth_cookie'] = true;
		$_SERVER['HTTP_X_WP_NONCE']     = wp_create_nonce( 'wp_rest' );

		$this->assertTrue( rest_cookie_check_errors( null ) );
		$this->assertSame( self::$editor_id, get_current_user_id() );
		$this->assertArrayHasKey( 'X-WP-Nonce', rest_get_server()->sent_headers );
	}

	/**
	 * A wrong or expired nonce is a 403, not a silent logout.
	 */
	public function test_cookie_with_bad_nonce_is_403() {
		wp_set_current_user( self::$editor_id );
		$GLOBALS['wp_rest_auth_cookie'] = true;
		$_SERVER['HTTP_X_WP_NONCE']     = 'not-a-nonce';

		$result = rest_cookie_check_errors( null );

		$this->assertWPError( $result );
		$this->assertSame( 'rest_cookie_invalid_nonce', $result->get_error_code() );
		$this->assertSame( 403, $result->get_error_data()['status'] );
	}

	/**
	 * Application passwords: shown once, stored only as a hash in user meta.
	 */
	public function test_application_password_is_stored_hashed_in_user_meta() {
		[ $password, $item ] = WP_Application_Passwords::create_new_application_password( self::$editor_id, [ 'name' => 'CI bot' ] );

		$stored = get_user_meta( self::$editor_id, '_application_passwords', true );

		$this->assertSame( 24, strlen( $password ) );
		$this->assertSame( $item['uuid'], $stored[0]['uuid'] );
		$this->assertStringStartsWith( '$generic$', $stored[0]['password'] ); // BLAKE2b via wp_fast_hash() (6.8+).
		$this->assertStringNotContainsString( $password, maybe_serialize( $stored ) );
	}

	/**
	 * Application passwords authenticate API requests only.
	 */
	public function test_application_password_only_works_for_api_requests() {
		[ $password ] = WP_Application_Passwords::create_new_application_password( self::$editor_id, [ 'name' => 'CI bot' ] );
		add_filter( 'wp_is_application_passwords_available', '__return_true' ); // Normally needs HTTPS.

		// A normal (non-API) login ignores it.
		$this->assertNull( wp_authenticate_application_password( null, 'nonce_editor', $password ) );

		// A REST request (Authorization: Basic base64(user:password)) accepts it.
		add_filter( 'application_password_is_api_request', '__return_true' );
		$user = wp_authenticate_application_password( null, 'nonce_editor', $password );
		$this->assertInstanceOf( \WP_User::class, $user );
		$this->assertSame( self::$editor_id, $user->ID );

		$wrong = wp_authenticate_application_password( null, 'nonce_editor', 'abcd efgh ijkl mnop qrst uvwx' );
		$this->assertWPError( $wrong );
		$this->assertSame( 'incorrect_password', $wrong->get_error_code() );
	}

	/**
	 * User passwords: bcrypt since 6.8, and old phpass hashes still work.
	 */
	public function test_user_passwords_use_bcrypt_and_old_hashes_still_verify() {
		$hash = wp_hash_password( 'correct horse battery staple' );

		// "$wp" marks WordPress's SHA-384 pre-hash; "$2y$" is bcrypt.
		$this->assertStringStartsWith( '$wp$2y$', $hash );
		$this->assertTrue( wp_check_password( 'correct horse battery staple', $hash ) );
		$this->assertFalse( wp_password_needs_rehash( $hash ) );

		// A pre-6.8 phpass ("portable", MD5-based) hash still logs in and is
		// flagged for an upgrade, which happens on the user's next login.
		require_once ABSPATH . WPINC . '/class-phpass.php';
		$legacy = ( new \PasswordHash( 8, true ) )->HashPassword( 'old password' );

		$this->assertStringStartsWith( '$P$', $legacy );
		$this->assertTrue( wp_check_password( 'old password', $legacy ) );
		$this->assertTrue( wp_password_needs_rehash( $legacy ) );
	}
}

How it's tested: 7 tests: a route without permission_callback is public and raises _doing_it_wrong; cookie without nonce is logged out, valid nonce keeps the user, bad nonce is 403; application passwords are stored as $generic$ hashes in _application_passwords and only work for API requests; user passwords hash as $wp$2y$ and old $P$ hashes still verify.

✓ PHPUnit passes✓ PHPCS (WordPress-Extra + VIP-Go) clean

I38.What happens if you register a REST route without a permission_callback?

"The route still works and is public: anyone can call it. Since WordPress 5.5 it also triggers a _doing_it_wrong notice telling you to add one. For a route that's meant to be public, pass '__return_true' so the intent is explicit."

In the mockYou said "public" as a 50/50 guess. It's correct; add the 5.5 notice and __return_true. The test below proves both.

I39.What authentication options does the WordPress REST API have?

"Built in: cookie plus nonce for code running in the browser of a logged-in user, and application passwords (5.6+) for other systems, sent as HTTP Basic auth. Through plugins: OAuth 1.0a, JWT. Custom schemes hook into determine_current_user."

  • Cookie + nonce: the editor, your own front-end scripts.
  • Application passwords: CI jobs, other servers, mobile apps. Over HTTPS only; each can be revoked.
  • OAuth / JWT: third-party apps acting for users without sharing a password.
  • Public data needs none: 'permission_callback' => '__return_true'.

I40.How do application passwords work behind the scenes?

"The user creates one per app in their profile. WordPress shows the 24-character password once and stores only a hash. The app sends username and password as HTTP Basic auth; on API requests, a determine_current_user filter checks the password against the stored hashes and logs the user in for that request only."

  1. Create: Users → Profile, or the authorize-application.php flow an app can redirect to. The password is shown in groups of four; spaces are ignored.
  2. Send: Authorization: Basic base64( username:password ).
  3. Check: wp_validate_application_password() runs on determine_current_user (priority 20). It only works for API requests (REST and XML-RPC), so it can't be used to log in to wp-admin.
  4. Record: on success it updates "last used" and the IP, so you can see and revoke stale ones.
  5. Requirements: HTTPS (or a "local" environment type). Some servers strip the Authorization header and need a config fix.
In the mockYou had the Basic-auth part. Add: shown once, stored hashed, API requests only, revocable per app.

I41.Where are application passwords stored?

"In user meta: the _application_passwords key in wp_usermeta, as an array with one entry per app (UUID, app name, the hashed password, created, last used and last IP). Since 6.8 the hash is BLAKE2b, made with wp_fast_hash()."

  • Fast hashing is fine here because the password is 24 random characters, so it can't be guessed. User passwords are short and human-chosen, which is why they get the slow bcrypt (I42).
  • API: WP_Application_Passwords::get_user_application_passwords( $user_id ).
In the mockYou didn't know where. The answer: user meta, _application_passwords.

I42.Which algorithm does WordPress use to hash user passwords?

"Since WordPress 6.8, bcrypt, through PHP's password_hash(). Before that, phpass portable hashes, which are salted MD5 repeated thousands of times. Old hashes still work and are upgraded to bcrypt the next time the user logs in."

  • Format: $wp$2y$10$…. $wp means WordPress first runs the password through HMAC-SHA384, because bcrypt ignores anything after 72 bytes; $2y$ is bcrypt. Old hashes start with $P$.
  • Configurable: the wp_hash_password_algorithm filter can switch to Argon2 if the server supports it.
  • Why slow hashing: it makes guessing leaked hashes expensive. A fast hash such as SHA-256 is the wrong tool for passwords.
In the mockYou said SHA-256. That's wrong, and a fast hash is exactly what you shouldn't use for passwords. Answer: bcrypt since 6.8, phpass before.
Remember it as"Users get bcrypt (slow), app passwords get BLAKE2b (fast, because they're random)."

I43.Your REST endpoint needs to return 10,000 posts with their meta and categories. How do you make it fast?

"Never in one response. Page through them with a cursor, not page numbers; load meta and terms in bulk so the query count doesn't grow with the page size; return only the fields needed; and cache the responses. If a client really needs everything, generate the export in the background and give them a file."

  1. Pagination, capped: core caps per_page at 100. A custom route caps it in the schema.
  2. Keyset (cursor) instead of offset: WHERE ID > last_id ORDER BY ID LIMIT 100 uses the primary key, so the last page is as fast as the first. OFFSET 9900 makes MySQL read and discard 9,900 rows, and pages shift when posts are added mid-export.
  3. No N+1: WP_Query loads all meta and terms for the page in one query each (update_post_meta_cache, update_post_term_cache). Never query inside the loop.
  4. No COUNT: no_found_rows if the client follows a "next" cursor instead of needing a total.
  5. Less data: _fields on core routes, or a slim custom response.
  6. Cache: object cache for the query, edge cache for public GETs (Cache-Control).
  7. Background export: for "everything", a cron or Action Scheduler job in batches, writing a file to storage.
  8. Elasticsearch only if they need search or complex filters across the set (I47).
In the mockYou said pagination, caching and Elasticsearch, which is fine. Add keyset vs offset and N+1: those show you know why it's slow.
Real code in this repo

📁 plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/rest-export.php

GET /news-uk/v1/export?after=0&per_page=100. Keyset pagination with a posts_where filter (WP_Query has no "ID greater than" argument), one extra row instead of a COUNT query, a slim response, and a Link: rel="next" header.

rest-export.php plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/rest-export.php
const EXPORT_MAX_PER_PAGE = 500;

/**
 * Register the route.
 *
 * @return void
 */
function register_export_route() {
	register_rest_route(
		'news-uk/v1',
		'/export',
		[
			'methods'             => \WP_REST_Server::READABLE,
			'callback'            => __NAMESPACE__ . '\export_posts',
			// Public on purpose: published posts only, and the page size is
			// capped. Missing this key would still leave the route public,
			// but WordPress logs a _doing_it_wrong notice (see the tests).
			'permission_callback' => '__return_true',
			'args'                => [
				'after'    => [
					'description' => 'Return posts with an ID greater than this. Use next_after from the previous page.',
					'type'        => 'integer',
					'minimum'     => 0,
					'default'     => 0,
				],
				'per_page' => [
					'type'    => 'integer',
					'minimum' => 1,
					'maximum' => EXPORT_MAX_PER_PAGE,
					'default' => 100,
				],
			],
		]
	);
}
add_action( 'rest_api_init', __NAMESPACE__ . '\register_export_route' );

/**
 * Add "AND ID > after" to queries that ask for it.
 *
 * WP_Query has no "ID greater than" argument, so a custom query var plus
 * posts_where adds it. Only queries that set news_uk_after_id are changed.
 *
 * @param string    $where The WHERE clause.
 * @param \WP_Query $query The query.
 * @return string
 */
function where_after_id( $where, $query ) {
	$after = (int) $query->get( 'news_uk_after_id' );
	if ( $after > 0 ) {
		global $wpdb;
		$where .= $wpdb->prepare( " AND {$wpdb->posts}.ID > %d", $after );
	}
	return $where;
}
add_filter( 'posts_where', __NAMESPACE__ . '\where_after_id', 10, 2 );

/**
 * One page of posts.
 *
 * @param \WP_REST_Request $request Request.
 * @return \WP_REST_Response
 */
function export_posts( $request ) {
	$per_page = (int) $request['per_page'];

	$query = new \WP_Query(
		[
			'post_type'           => 'post',
			'post_status'         => 'publish',
			'news_uk_after_id'    => (int) $request['after'],
			'orderby'             => 'ID',
			'order'               => 'ASC',
			// One extra row tells us whether there is a next page, without a
			// COUNT query.
			'posts_per_page'      => $per_page + 1,
			'no_found_rows'       => true,
			'ignore_sticky_posts' => true,
		]
	);

	$posts    = $query->posts;
	$has_more = count( $posts ) > $per_page;
	$posts    = array_slice( $posts, 0, $per_page );

	$items = [];
	foreach ( $posts as $post ) {
		$terms   = get_the_terms( $post, 'category' );
		$items[] = [
			'id'         => $post->ID,
			'title'      => get_the_title( $post ),
			'date'       => get_post_time( 'c', true, $post ),
			'link'       => get_permalink( $post ),
			'standfirst' => (string) get_post_meta( $post->ID, 'news_uk_standfirst', true ),
			'categories' => is_array( $terms ) ? wp_list_pluck( $terms, 'slug' ) : [],
		];
	}

	$next_after = $has_more ? end( $posts )->ID : null;
	$response   = rest_ensure_response(
		[
			'items'      => $items,
			'next_after' => $next_after,
		]
	);

	if ( null !== $next_after ) {
		$response->link_header(
			'next',
			add_query_arg(
				[
					'after'    => $next_after,
					'per_page' => $per_page,
				],
				rest_url( 'news-uk/v1/export' )
			)
		);
	}

	return $response;
}
PHPUnit: walk the cursor, cap, and count queries plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/tests/class-rest-export-test.php
<?php
/**
 * Mock interview: the 10,000-post export endpoint pages correctly, and its
 * query count doesn't grow with the page size (no N+1).
 *
 * @package News_UK_Practice
 */

namespace News_UK_Practice\Tests;

use WP_REST_Request;
use WP_UnitTestCase;

/**
 * Export endpoint tests.
 */
class Rest_Export_Test extends WP_UnitTestCase {

	/**
	 * Published post IDs, oldest first.
	 *
	 * @var int[]
	 */
	private static $ids = [];

	/**
	 * 30 published posts with meta and a category, plus a draft.
	 *
	 * @param \WP_UnitTest_Factory $factory Factory.
	 */
	public static function wpSetUpBeforeClass( $factory ) {
		$cat = $factory->category->create( [ 'slug' => 'politics' ] );
		for ( $i = 1; $i <= 30; $i++ ) {
			$id = $factory->post->create( [ 'post_title' => "Story $i" ] );
			update_post_meta( $id, 'news_uk_standfirst', "Standfirst $i" );
			wp_set_post_categories( $id, [ $cat ] );
			self::$ids[] = $id;
		}
		$factory->post->create( [ 'post_status' => 'draft' ] );
	}

	/**
	 * Call the endpoint.
	 *
	 * @param array $params Query params.
	 * @return \WP_REST_Response
	 */
	private function get( $params ) {
		$request = new WP_REST_Request( 'GET', '/news-uk/v1/export' );
		$request->set_query_params( $params );
		return rest_do_request( $request );
	}

	/**
	 * Following next_after returns every published post exactly once, in order.
	 */
	public function test_cursor_walks_every_post_once() {
		$seen  = [];
		$after = 0;
		$pages = 0;
		do {
			$data  = $this->get(
				[
					'after'    => $after,
					'per_page' => 7,
				]
			)->get_data();
			$seen  = array_merge( $seen, wp_list_pluck( $data['items'], 'id' ) );
			$after = $data['next_after'];
			++$pages;
		} while ( null !== $after && $pages < 20 );

		$this->assertSame( self::$ids, $seen );
		$this->assertSame( 5, $pages ); // 7 + 7 + 7 + 7 + 2.
	}

	/**
	 * Each item carries its meta and categories, and a Link header points to the next page.
	 */
	public function test_item_shape_and_link_header() {
		$response = $this->get( [ 'per_page' => 2 ] );
		$item     = $response->get_data()['items'][0];

		$this->assertSame( 'Story 1', $item['title'] );
		$this->assertSame( 'Standfirst 1', $item['standfirst'] );
		$this->assertSame( [ 'politics' ], $item['categories'] );
		$link = $response->get_headers()['Link'];
		$this->assertStringContainsString( 'rel="next"', $link );
		$this->assertStringContainsString( 'after=' . self::$ids[1], $link );
	}

	/**
	 * The page size is capped by the schema.
	 */
	public function test_per_page_is_capped() {
		$this->assertSame( 400, $this->get( [ 'per_page' => 10000 ] )->get_status() );
	}

	/**
	 * Same number of queries for 5 posts or 25: meta and terms are loaded in bulk.
	 */
	public function test_query_count_does_not_grow_with_page_size() {
		global $wpdb;

		$count = function ( $per_page, $after ) use ( $wpdb ) {
			wp_cache_flush(); // Cold cache, so we count real queries.
			$before = $wpdb->num_queries;
			$this->get(
				[
					'after'    => $after,
					'per_page' => $per_page,
				]
			);
			return $wpdb->num_queries - $before;
		};

		$small = $count( 5, 0 );
		$large = $count( 25, self::$ids[4] );

		$this->assertSame( $small, $large, "5 posts took $small queries, 25 posts took $large." );
	}
}

How it's tested: 4 tests. Following next_after returns all 30 posts exactly once; per_page=10000 is a 400; and, on a cold cache, 25 posts take exactly as many queries as 5. That last one is the N+1 check.

✓ PHPUnit passes✓ PHPCS (WordPress-Extra + VIP-Go) clean

Platform and operations

I44.What are the limitations of WP-Cron?

"WP-Cron isn't a real scheduler: it only runs when someone loads a page. Low traffic means late jobs; full-page caching means even fewer requests reach PHP. It runs inside a web request with time limits, gives no retries or monitoring, and keeps every event in one autoloaded option."

  • Timing: on each request, WordPress checks for due events and fires a background loopback request to wp-cron.php. No requests, no cron.
  • Long jobs: PHP and web-server time limits; the lock lasts 60 seconds (WP_CRON_LOCK_TIMEOUT), so a slow job can run twice.
  • Storage: all events in the cron option. Many events make it big, and it's loaded on every request.
  • Fix: DISABLE_WP_CRON plus a real system cron calling wp cron event run --due-now. VIP does this for you with Cron Control: events in a custom table, run by a dedicated runner on schedule, regardless of traffic.
  • Even then: keep jobs short, idempotent and batched; for big queues, Action Scheduler.
In the mockYou had the main point (traffic-driven). Add caching makes it worse, time limits, no retries, and how VIP solves it.

I45.You deploy, and suddenly the production site is slow. How do you debug it?

"First limit the damage: if the slowdown started with my deploy and it's serious, revert it, then investigate. Then I find which requests got slow in APM (New Relic on VIP), trace them to slow queries, uncached remote calls or a lower cache hit rate, fix it, and add a check so it can't come back."

  1. Confirm and scope: which pages, logged-in or not, since exactly when? Does it line up with the deploy?
  2. Mitigate: revert the merge and redeploy. Communicate in the incident channel.
  3. Find the cause: New Relic transaction traces; the slow query log and Query Monitor; edge cache hit rate (did a new cookie, header or query string bust the cache?); object cache misses; new wp_remote_get calls without timeouts; cron storms; a growing autoloaded option.
