  4. Fix and prove: reproduce locally with production-like data; compare before and after.
  5. Prevent: a regression test (e.g. a query-count assertion), a performance budget in CI, a short post-mortem.
In this repoQ27 (traffic spike), Q31 (incident story), M31 (debugging server-side).

Flagged in the feedback for the real interview

I46.What caching layers are there on WordPress VIP?

"From the reader inwards: the browser cache, VIP's edge cache (full-page cache for logged-out requests), the CDN for media and static files, the object cache (Memcached) for queries and computed values, Enterprise Search to take heavy queries off MySQL, and the database itself."

  • Edge cache: serves whole pages without touching PHP. Logged-in users and requests with certain cookies bypass it. Purged automatically when posts change (vip-cache-manager), or by URL with wpvip_purge_edge_cache_for_url() (the old wpcom_vip_ name is deprecated). Controlled with Cache-Control.
  • Object cache: core caches queries, posts, meta, options; transients live here. Your code uses wp_cache_get / wp_cache_set with good keys (Q15, Q16).
  • Media: VIP File Service plus CDN, with image resizing by query string.
  • What breaks caching: per-user output in the HTML, Set-Cookie on public pages, random query strings, nocache_headers().
In this repoQ15, Q16, Q18, Q27, M35 (edge caching), M36 and M37.

I47.Elasticsearch: advantages, disadvantages, and why not index everything?

"It makes full-text search, complex filters and aggregations fast, and takes that load off MySQL. The costs: results lag a little behind saves, it's a second copy of the data to keep in sync, and every indexed field makes indexing slower and the index bigger. So you index what you search or filter by, nothing else."

  • Pros: relevance-ranked search, fast meta and taxonomy filters, facets, scales reads.
  • Cons: eventual consistency (a just-saved post may not appear yet); sync failures; reindexing a large site takes time; more to debug; you need a MySQL fallback.
  • Why not everything: each field adds to the mapping (too many fields slows the cluster and can hit field limits); bigger index, slower writes; private meta could leak into search results. That's why VIP uses an allow list for meta (vip_search_post_meta_allow_list, Q17).
  • Same idea for MySQL indexes: an index speeds reads but slows every write and uses memory. Index the columns your queries filter and sort on. (meta_value is LONGTEXT, which is why meta queries are slow in the first place.)
In this repoQ17: the allow list, and a query that opts in with 'es' => true and falls back to MySQL.

I48.How do you use AI day to day? How would you use it on a big task?

"As a pair programmer I review closely. For a big task, I write the plan and acceptance criteria myself, split it into small steps that each end in passing tests, and let the agent do one step at a time while I review every diff. I pick the model by the job, and I keep the context small and specific."

  • Splitting work: plan first (in a plan mode, or a written plan), then one step per change, tests first where possible, run lint and tests after each step.
  • Models: the most capable model for planning, architecture and review; a faster, cheaper one for mechanical edits and summaries.
  • Tokens: point it at the right files instead of the whole repo; keep project rules in a context file (CLAUDE.md or similar); start fresh sessions for new tasks; use sub-agents for wide searches; prompt caching in API work.
  • MCP: an open protocol that lets the assistant use tools and data (GitHub, Jira, a browser, a database) through one standard interface. Treat what tools return as data, not instructions.
  • Guardrails: no client secrets or personal data in prompts; review everything; AI output gets the same tests and code review as human code.
In this repoM47–M49 (MCP, models, how often), Q35 (AI inside the product, with guardrails).
Your storyOne real task where you split the work this way, what the AI got wrong, and how your tests or review caught it.

I49.Show us your best code. What's the most challenging thing you've built?

Have it open before the call. Pick one piece, say in one sentence what problem it solved, then walk through: the constraint that made it hard, the decision you made, and how it's tested.

  • Make sure the file passes the coding standards (npm run lint:php with the VIP rules), has doc comments, and has a test you can run on screen.
  • From this repo, good candidates: Q25 (external API sync that survives outages), Q27 (stampede guard), the FAQ context example with its three layers of tests.

10. VIP courses: more questions, fact-checked

Questions taken from the seven WordPress VIP Learn courses that sections 1–9 don't already cover. The course material had many errors, so every answer here was checked against the WordPress 6.9 source and the VIP mu-plugins. Where the course is wrong, a red "Course check" box says what's actually true, so you don't repeat the mistake in the interview. The same check also fixed a few answers in earlier sections.

WordPress VIP Architecture and Tooling

V1.What does "code up, content down" mean, and what environments does a VIP application have?

"Code moves up from develop to preprod to production through Git; content only moves down, by copying the production database to lower environments. Production is the single source of truth for content, so nothing is ever pushed up over it."

  • Environments: production plus non-production ones, usually develop and preprod. VIP avoids "staging" because it suggests staging content, which is not what they're for.
  • Each environment has a branch: merging to that branch deploys it. Same code path everywhere, so preprod is a real rehearsal for production.
  • Content down: Dashboard data sync or vip sync copies production to a non-production environment and rewrites the domains. Anything editors created on preprod is overwritten, so don't build real content there.
  • Config differs, code doesn't: branch on VIP_GO_APP_ENVIRONMENT or wp_get_environment_type() (VIP maps develop to development, other non-production names to staging), not on hostnames.
  • Why it matters at News UK: newsroom content changes every minute; a database push from preprod would lose live articles.
In this repovip-config/vip-config.php reads VIP_GO_APP_ENVIRONMENT to decide the environment type locally.
Remember it as"Code climbs, content falls."

V2.What is read-only on VIP, and where can your code write files?

"All application code, core, plugins and themes, is read-only, so nothing can write to or update it at runtime. Uploads go to the VIP File System through wp_upload_dir(), temporary files go to get_temp_dir(), and /tmp belongs to one container only, so it can't be shared between requests."

  • Why read-only: a hacked admin or plugin can't drop a PHP backdoor, and every container runs the same, known code, which is what makes horizontal scaling safe.
  • Uploads: on VIP wp_upload_dir() points at a vip:// stream, so normal file functions write to the File System API, not local disk. It's slower than disk, and uploads can't execute PHP.
  • /tmp: fine for building a file within one request (wp_tempnam()), then move it to uploads. Not fine for a CSV built across several AJAX requests or cron jobs, because the next request may hit another container.
  • Config files: read-only data that shouldn't be public goes in private/, read with WPCOM_VIP_PRIVATE_DIR.
  • Plugin fit: plugins that write their own cache files, logs or .htaccess, or update themselves, won't work. Check before choosing a plugin.
Remember it as"Code is locked, uploads go to vip://, /tmp is one container's scratch pad."

V3.How is a VIP codebase laid out, and how do you load plugins?

"The repo is wp-content from the VIP skeleton: themes/, plugins/, client-mu-plugins/, vip-config/, private/, images/ and languages/. Core and VIP's own mu-plugins are not in it. Plugins are loaded in code with wpcom_vip_load_plugin() from a client mu-plugin, so they can't be switched off in wp-admin."

  • client-mu-plugins/: always on, loaded after VIP's mu-plugins. Only top-level .php files load, so a plugin in a subfolder needs a small loader file.
  • wpcom_vip_load_plugin( 'slug' ): activation in code, versioned and reviewed, identical on every environment.
  • vip-config/vip-config.php: what you'd put in wp-config.php; runs very early, so plain PHP only. sunrise.php for multisite domain mapping also lives here.
  • private/: deployed but not web-accessible. docs/: not deployed.
  • mu-plugins are VIP's and not in your repo; you only configure them with constants and filters.
In this repoclient-mu-plugins/plugin-loader.php calls wpcom_vip_load_plugin( 'news-uk-practice' ); vip-config/vip-config.php holds early config.
Remember it as"Activate in code, configure in vip-config, keep secrets out of the repo."

V4.What do the VIP mu-plugins give you, and can you change them?

"They connect WordPress to the platform: the Memcached object cache, HyperDB for the primary and replicas, cache purging, the VIP File System, Cron Control, plus Query Monitor, two-factor, Jetpack and helpers like wpcom_vip_*(). VIP versions and deploys them; you can't edit them, only configure them with constants and filters."

  • Drop-ins: object-cache.php (Memcached) and db.php (HyperDB, which sends reads to replicas and writes to the primary).
  • On by default: Cron Control, Query Monitor, two-factor, Jetpack, Akismet, Rewrite Rules Inspector, Lightweight Term Count Update, HTTP Concat, the importer, Advanced Post Cache.
  • Opt in: Enterprise Search (VIP_ENABLE_VIP_SEARCH and VIP_ENABLE_VIP_SEARCH_QUERY_INTEGRATION), Parse.ly, Block Data API, Block Governance.
  • Query Monitor: loaded only for users with the view_query_monitor capability (or a valid QM cookie), so readers never pay for it. Grant the capability to give developers access.
  • Locally: load the same mu-plugins so code behaves as on VIP and the helpers exist.
In this repoThe real mu-plugins are in plugins/vendor/automattic/vip-go-mu-plugins/, loaded by the Docker stack (Q19, M38).
Remember it as"VIP owns the mu-plugins; you hold the filters."
Course checkThe course says Advanced Post Cache "will ultimately be deprecated" because 6.1 added query caching to core. In the mu-plugins used here it's still loaded on every request (advanced-post-cache.php, outside core test runs). Say "VIP still runs it today", not "it's deprecated".

V5.Which users must use two-factor authentication on VIP, and how would you change that?

"On VIP environments, users with manage_options, so administrators, are forced to set up two-factor before they can use wp-admin. The capability can be changed with the wpcom_vip_two_factor_enforcement_cap filter, and sites with their own SSO can tell VIP so the SSO provider handles the second factor."

  • Raise the bar: for a newsroom, enforce it for editors too, for example with 'edit_others_posts', since an editor account can deface the front page.
  • SSO: when the identity provider already does MFA, VIP skips its own enforcement (wpcom_vip_use_custom_sso()).
  • Not forced locally: only on VIP environments, unless you opt in for testing.
  • API logins: VIP also stops users with two-factor from logging in to APIs with their normal password (two_factor_user_api_login_enable is false).
add_filter( 'wpcom_vip_two_factor_enforcement_cap', fn() => 'edit_others_posts' );
Remember it as"Admins by default, editors by choice, SSO by agreement."
Course checkThe course is right about manage_options (two-factor.php line 140), but leaves out that it's skipped outside VIP environments and for custom SSO or Jetpack SSO with two-step (lines 23–60).

V6.How does code get from a pull request to production on VIP, and how do you roll back?

"A pull request against the environment's branch, reviewed by the team and scanned by VIP's Code Analysis Bot, is merged and deployed automatically to that environment. We test on develop or preprod, then merge to the production branch. To roll back, we revert the commit and let it deploy, or redeploy an earlier deploy from the Dashboard."

  • Build step: the repo should not hold node_modules; CI builds assets and pushes to a "built" branch that the environment deploys from.
  • Small, frequent deploys: easier to review and to revert. Avoid deploying right before a big news event.
  • After deploy: watch the Dashboard's runtime logs and performance for errors and slow requests; purge edge cache only for what changed.
  • Database changes don't roll back with code: make them backward-compatible (add before you remove) so a code revert is safe.
  • Access: GitHub access for the VIP repo is managed by the customer; VIP staff get temporary, logged access only for support.
Remember it as"PR, bot, merge, deploy; revert is just another PR."

V7.What is VIP-CLI, how does it differ from WP-CLI, and what do you use it for?

"VIP-CLI is VIP's own command-line tool for the platform: environments, logs, backups, cache, imports and the local dev-env. It also runs WP-CLI on a remote environment, like vip @app.production -- wp user list, with some risky WP-CLI commands blocked."

TaskCommand
Run WP-CLI remotelyvip @app.develop -- wp post list
Runtime or batch logsvip @app.production logs --type=batch
Slow queriesvip @app.production slowlogs
Purge one URLvip @app.production cache purge-url <url>
Backup / download DBvip @app.production backup db, export sql
Copy prod data downvip sync
  • Blocked commands: those that would write code or bypass the platform, for example installing or updating plugins (the file system is read-only anyway). VIP documents the list.
  • Long jobs: write a custom WP-CLI command that works in batches, is idempotent, and has a dry-run flag, rather than one huge command.
  • Production: VIP-CLI asks for confirmation; --yes skips it, so use that only in scripts you trust.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q31-incident-cli/ is a batched, dry-run-first WP-CLI repair command of the kind you'd run with vip @app.production -- wp ....
Remember it as"vip talks to the platform, wp talks to WordPress, -- joins them."
Course checkThe course writes the syntax as vip @app.env <command> for WP-CLI. VIP-CLI 3.21 needs the double dash: vip @app.env -- wp <command> (its own help says the -- "must separate" the two). The course's link for "available VIP-CLI commands" actually points to the disallowed WP-CLI commands page.

V8.How do you keep a non-production environment private?

"At the edge, from the Dashboard's Access & Routing panel: Basic Authentication for a shared username and password, or an IP allow list for office or VPN ranges. Both are checked before the request reaches WordPress, so blocked traffic costs nothing at origin."

  • Basic Auth: quick for reviewers. It also uses the Authorization header, so it can clash with API clients that send application passwords (also Basic auth).
  • IP allow list: stronger for internal tools, but breaks for remote staff off VPN and for third-party services that call webhooks.
  • Also: keep non-production out of search (no public links, noindex), and remember it holds a copy of production data, including user emails, after a sync.
  • HSTS is in the same panel.
Remember it as"Lock non-prod at the edge, and remember it holds real data."

V9.Which parts of the VIP Dashboard do you use as a developer?

"Logs and Performance to debug errors and slow requests, Code to see deploys and plugin updates or known vulnerabilities, Database for backups and data sync, Access & Routing for Basic Auth, IP allow lists and HSTS, and Domains & TLS when launching."

  • Logs: runtime (PHP errors) and batch (cron and WP-CLI) logs; the same data as vip logs.
  • Performance: response times, cache hit rate, errors; the first stop after a deploy.
  • Code: deploy history, open PRs, plugin versions with update PRs (wordpress.org plugins only) and vulnerability warnings.
  • Database: download backups, ship backups to your own S3 (higher support tiers), data sync to lower environments.
  • Media: export the whole media library.
  • Roles: what you see depends on your org and app role; only org admins grant access.
Remember it as"Logs, Performance, Code, Database, Access." The five panels a developer opens.

V10.How are VIP sites backed up, and how would you recover from a bad bulk change to content?

"VIP backs up the database automatically, hourly for production and daily for non-production, with copies kept off-site, and media is stored separately in the VIP File System. For a bad bulk change I'd avoid a full restore, which would lose newer articles, and instead restore only the affected rows or table from a backup, or repair them with a script."

  • Get a backup: Dashboard Database panel, vip @app.production backup db for a fresh one, vip @app.production export sql to download.
  • Partial restore: load the backup into a non-production or local environment, extract the rows you need, write them back with a tested WP-CLI command. VIP support can help with table-level restores.
  • Before risky jobs: take a backup, run with --dry-run, log what you change so it can be undone.
  • Plan it: write down and test the restore steps; a backup you've never restored is a guess.
Remember it as"Restore the rows, not the world."
Course checkThe retention periods in the course (hourly for 72 hours, daily for 30 days) and the support tiers for backup shipping are commercial details that change; check the current VIP docs before quoting them in an interview.

V11.A bot is hammering an uncached endpoint. What can you do on VIP?

"VIP's edge already absorbs DDoS-scale traffic. For a specific abusive client I can block it at origin early in vip-config.php with VIP_Request_Block::ip(), ::ua() or ::header(), and then fix the real problem: make the endpoint cacheable or cheaper, and ask VIP support for an edge rule if it continues."

  • Where it runs: before WordPress loads, so a blocked request costs almost nothing, but it is at origin, not the edge.
  • Take care with IPs: make sure it's the client IP, not a proxy or a partner's NAT, or you block real readers.
  • Root cause: unique query strings that bust the cache, logged-out requests to admin-ajax, an expensive REST route. Cache the response, add rate limiting, or require authentication.
  • XML-RPC: a common target; block unused methods (see the security questions).
// vip-config/vip-config.php: plain PHP, WordPress isn't loaded yet.
VIP_Request_Block::ua( 'BadBot/1.0' );
Remember it as"Block early, then make it cheap."
Course checkThe security course says VIP_Request_Block can "block or limit" requests by IP, user agent or request method. It only blocks, and by IP, user agent (exact or partial) or header; there is no rate limiting and no request-method rule (lib/class-vip-request-block.php, methods at lines 35–141, "block unwanted clients at origin (not edges)" at line 3).

V12.How do you handle secrets and per-environment settings on VIP?

"With VIP environment variables: set them per environment with vip @app.env config envvar set NAME, read them in PHP with vip_get_env_var( 'NAME', $default ). That keeps API keys out of Git and lets each environment use its own value."

  • Under the hood: each variable becomes a constant named VIP_ENV_VAR_NAME, and vip_get_env_var() reads that constant.
  • Timing: VIP's docs say a new or changed value takes effect on the next deploy, so plan a deploy (or redeploy) after setting it.
  • Locally: vip dev-env envvar set --slug=site NAME, or define the constant yourself.
  • Never: commit keys, store them in options editable in wp-admin, or print them in errors and logs.
Remember it as"Set with envvar, read with vip_get_env_var(), never in Git."
Course checkThe course says environment variables can't be used in the local dev-env and you must define constants. VIP-CLI 3.21 has vip dev-env envvar (get, set, list, delete) for local environments. The mu-plugin reads the VIP_ENV_VAR_ constant (lib/environment/class-environment.php lines 10 and 29–32), so defining that constant by hand also still works.

Enterprise WordPress Performance

V13.Does WP_Query cache its own results? What invalidates that cache?

"Yes, since WordPress 6.1. With a persistent object cache, WP_Query stores the post IDs for each distinct query and reuses them. But the cache is salted with the posts 'last_changed' value, so any post save or post meta write anywhere on the site invalidates every cached query at once."

  • How it works: cache_results defaults to true. The key is a hash of the query args and the SQL; results go in the post-queries group, salted with wp_cache_get_last_changed( 'posts' ), plus 'terms' when there is a tax query.
  • Not cached: orderby => 'rand', or a query whose selected fields were changed by a posts_fields/posts_clauses filter.
  • Why it matters at News UK scale: added_post_meta, updated_post_meta and deleted_post_meta all bump the posts last_changed. A plugin that writes meta on every page view (a view counter, a "last seen" stamp) silently empties the whole query cache on every request.
  • Hit rate: every distinct set of args is a separate key. post__not_in => [ get_the_ID() ] makes a new key per article, so the "related posts" query is never shared. Fetch one extra post and drop the current one in PHP instead.
  • So you still cache yourself: the core cache helps between edits. On a busy newsroom, where posts change every minute, a hand-built cache with its own TTL (or a fragment cache of the rendered HTML) is steadier for hot lists.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q27-traffic-spike/load.php: views are counted in Memcached and flushed to post meta by cron every five minutes. That keeps writes off GET requests, but note each flush still bumps last_changed for posts, so the query cache is reset every five minutes.
Course checkThe NOT IN lesson says such a query "will not be cached". In 6.9 it is cached, but under a key unique to that page, so it is rarely reused. The advice (filter in PHP) is right; the reason is hit rate, not "not cached".
Remember it as"Core caches queries, but one meta write wipes them all."

V14.Memcached can't delete keys by prefix or group. How do you invalidate a set of related cache keys?

"Put a version number in the key. Store a 'last changed' value per group, include it in every key, and bump it when the data changes. Old keys are never read again and Memcached evicts them."

  • On VIP: wp_cache_supports( 'flush_group' ) returns false and wp_cache_flush_group() does nothing. Never call wp_cache_flush(): it clears the whole site's cache.
  • Core helpers: wp_cache_get_last_changed( $group ) reads (or creates) the version; wp_cache_set_last_changed( $group ) bumps it (6.3+). Core uses this exact pattern for the query caches.
  • Pattern: key = 'latest_' . $cat . ':' . wp_cache_get_last_changed( 'news_uk_lists' ); on save_post, call wp_cache_set_last_changed( 'news_uk_lists' ).
  • Trade-off: one bump invalidates everything in the group. Keep groups narrow (per section, per feature) so one edit doesn't cold-start unrelated caches.
  • Expiry rule on VIP: an expiry above 30 days is treated as "no expiry". Orphaned versioned keys rely on eviction, so always set a sensible TTL.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q18-performance/load.php: latest_ids_in_category() puts wp_cache_get_last_changed( 'posts' ) in the key, so a new post moves the key on its own.
Remember it as"Don't delete the drawer, change the label."

V15.What are autoloaded options, and what goes wrong when alloptions gets big?

"Autoloaded options are loaded in one query on every request and cached as a single 'alloptions' object. If that object grows, every request pays for it, and on VIP it can exceed Memcached's 1 MB item limit, at which point VIP returns a 503 to protect the site."

  • What changed in 6.6: add_option() and update_option() default $autoload to null, not 'yes'. WordPress then stores 'auto' (still autoloaded), or 'auto-off' if the value is over 150 KB (wp_max_autoloaded_option_size). Explicit values are now 'on'/'off'.
  • What to do: pass false for anything large, rarely read, or written often. Writes to an autoloaded option rewrite the whole alloptions cache entry.
  • VIP safeguard: alloptions-limit.php warns above 2.5 MB uncompressed, and above 4 MB checks the compressed size; at 1,000,000 bytes compressed it sends a 503.
  • Common culprits: transients without an expiry on non-object-cache hosts (they are autoloaded), plugin logs, big serialised settings arrays.
  • Fixing existing data: wp_set_option_autoload_values( [ 'name' => false ] ), or wp option set-autoload in WP-CLI.
Course checkThe course says "the default for the autoload parameter is yes". Since WordPress 6.6 the default is null; it still autoloads unless the value is over 150 KB, so the practical advice (pass false) is unchanged.
Remember it as"Autoload is a suitcase you carry on every trip. Pack light."

V16.How do you read an EXPLAIN plan for a slow WP_Query, and which indexes does wp_posts actually have?

"Take the SQL from Query Monitor or $query->request, run EXPLAIN, and look at type, key, rows and Extra. 'ALL' with no key, a large rows estimate, or 'Using temporary; Using filesort' means it isn't using an index. Then check the query against the indexes that really exist."

  • Columns that matter: type (ALL = full scan; ref/range/const are good), key (index chosen), rows (estimate examined), Extra (filesort/temporary are the costly ones).
  • wp_posts indexes: primary ID, post_name(191), type_status_date (post_type, post_status, post_date, ID), post_parent, post_author, type_status_author. There is no index on post_title and no index on post_date alone.
  • Leftmost prefix: type_status_date only helps if the query filters on post_type (and ideally post_status) first. A typical WP_Query does, which is why "latest posts of type X" is fast.
  • wp_postmeta: post_id and meta_key(191) only. meta_value is LONGTEXT and not indexed.
  • Why not add more indexes: each index slows every insert and update, uses disk and buffer pool memory, and on VIP schema changes go through VIP support. Index what your hot queries filter and sort on, nothing else.
Course checkTwo course examples don't match core's schema. The LIKE lesson says post_title LIKE 'WordPress%' "can take advantage of an index on the post_title column": there is no such index, so it is still a full scan. The EXPLAIN example shows a key named post_date: core has none, and WHERE post_date > ... with no post_type cannot use type_status_date.
Remember it as"ALL is bad, key is good, rows is the bill."

V17.Why are meta_value queries slow, and how would you model the data to avoid them?

"wp_postmeta is the biggest table, and only meta_key is indexed. A meta_value filter has to read every row with that key. So I avoid filtering on values: use a taxonomy for anything you filter by, use the presence of a key as the flag, or send it to Enterprise Search."

  • Size: postmeta has many rows per post. At News UK scale it is tens of millions of rows.
  • Taxonomy instead: "premium", "section", "content type" are filters. Terms are indexed through wp_term_relationships, are cached, and the edge cache already purges term archives.
  • Key presence as the flag: store hide_on_home only when true and query 'compare' => 'EXISTS' / 'NOT EXISTS'. That uses the meta_key index instead of scanning values.
  • Sorting by meta (orderby => 'meta_value_num') needs a join and a filesort. "Most read" lists are better pre-computed by cron into a cached list of IDs.
  • Enterprise Search: add the key to vip_search_post_meta_allow_list and query with 'es' => true. VIP may also add a meta_key + meta_value index on request; that's a platform change, not something code does.
Remember it as"Filter by terms, flag by key, sort in advance."

V18.A query has LIMIT 10 but is still slow. Why, and how do you bound a WP_Query properly?

"LIMIT caps the rows returned, not the rows MySQL examines. If it has to sort a million matches to find the top ten, or skip thousands with OFFSET, it's still slow. Bound the scope: a date range, a specific post type and status, and keyset instead of deep pagination."

  • Date range: a "latest in Sport" module rarely needs posts older than a few months. 'date_query' => [ [ 'after' => '3 months ago' ] ] lets the type_status_date index cut the range.
  • Deep pages: paged => 500 becomes LIMIT 4990, 10; MySQL reads and throws away 4,990 rows. Cap page numbers on archives, or use keyset (ID < last_id).
  • The falsy ID trap: 'p' => (int) $maybe_false is 0, which WP_Query ignores, so you get the latest posts instead of one. Combined with posts_per_page => -1 it loads every post. Check the ID before querying.
  • Defaults: WP_Query without posts_per_page uses the site's "posts per page" option (10 by default), not "all". -1 is the unbounded one.
  • Don't over-fetch: 'no_found_rows' => true unless paginating; 'fields' => 'ids' when you only need IDs.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/rest-export.php: keyset pagination through a posts_where filter, so the last page is as fast as the first.
Course checkThe WP_Query lesson says "by default, WP_Query retrieves all matching posts". It doesn't: an empty posts_per_page falls back to get_option( 'posts_per_page' ) (class-wp-query.php line 2007). The same lesson recommends "specific taxonomies instead of tax_query": cat and tag are converted into a tax_query internally, so there is no performance difference.
Remember it as"LIMIT trims the answer, not the work."

V19.How do you personalise pages on VIP without losing the edge cache?

"Keep the HTML the same for everyone and personalise in the browser, or split the cache into a few segments with VIP's Vary_Cache. Never vary the HTML per user on a cached URL."

  • Client-side first: serve one cached page; fetch the small personal part (login state, "saved for later", paywall meter) with a cacheable or per-user request after load.
  • Segments with Vary_Cache: \Automattic\VIP\Cache\Vary_Cache::register_group( 'edition' ), then set_group_for_user( 'edition', 'uk' ). It sets a vip-go-seg cookie and the edge keeps one copy per segment. Few segments (UK/IE, subscriber/not) keep the hit rate high.
  • Timing: set_group_for_user() returns a WP_Error after send_headers, so set it early (e.g. on init) or on the response that handles the user's action.
  • Sensitive segments: the plain segment cookie can be edited by the reader. For paywall tiers, enable encryption (Vary_Cache::enable_encryption(), needs VIP-provided constants) and still check entitlement on the server for the real content.
  • What kills the hit rate: a Set-Cookie on public pages, PHP sessions, nocache_headers(), unique query strings, POST for reads.
Remember it as"Same page for all; a few segments at most; the rest in the browser."

V20.When an editor updates a story, what does VIP purge from the edge, and how do you purge pages it doesn't know about?

"VIP's cache manager purges the post's URL, the home page, the first two pages of each public term archive the post is in, and the feeds. Anything else, like a section front built from a custom query or a custom REST route, I add with the purge URL filter or purge directly."

  • Trigger: the cache manager hooks clean_post_cache and clean_term_cache. A permalink change also purges the old URL (post_updated). Purges are queued and sent at shutdown.
  • Only viewable, published posts: drafts and non-viewable post types are skipped.
  • Adding URLs: add_filter( 'wpcom_vip_cache_purge_post_post_urls', fn( $urls, $post_id ) => ..., 10, 2 ) (the middle part is the post type). Term archive depth is wpcom_vip_cache_purge_urls_max_pages (default 2).
  • Manual purge: wpvip_purge_edge_cache_for_url( $url ) and wpvip_purge_edge_cache_for_post( $post ). The older wpcom_vip_* names are deprecated.
  • Be careful: purging is cheap to call but expensive in effect. Purging the home page on every meta save during a live event turns the edge off for your busiest URL. Prefer short TTLs for fast-moving pages, and purge only on real content changes.
Remember it as"Post, home, two pages of terms, feeds. The rest is on you."

V21.If you must call a third-party API during a page request on VIP, how do you stop a slow API from taking the site down?

"Short timeout, cache the result, keep a last-good copy, and stop calling a host that keeps failing. VIP's vip_safe_wp_remote_get() gives the timeout and the circuit breaker; wpcom_vip_file_get_contents() adds caching with a backup copy."

  • Why it matters: each waiting request holds a PHP worker. A 10-second API stall under load uses up the workers and the whole site queues, not just the page with the widget.
  • vip_safe_wp_remote_get( $url, $fallback, $threshold = 3, $timeout = 1, $retry = 20 ): after $threshold slow calls to the same host, it stops calling for $retry seconds and returns the fallback. Timeouts above 5 s are capped on the front end. It does not cache.
  • wpcom_vip_file_get_contents( $url, $timeout = 3, $cache_time = 900 ): caches the body, and keeps a backup copy to return if a later fetch fails. Timeouts over 3 s trigger a _doing_it_wrong.
  • Better still: move the call out of the request (cron or webhook), store the data locally, and render from that (Q25).
Remember it as"Timeout, cache, backup, breaker."

V22.Static variable, non-persistent group, or object cache: which do you use for caching inside one request?

"The VIP object cache already keeps an in-request copy, so a repeated wp_cache_get() for the same key is free after the first. A static variable is for computed values you don't want in Memcached. A non-persistent group is for data that must never leave the request. And to cut network round trips, fetch many keys at once."

  • Runtime copy: the VIP drop-in stores every get and set in a local array for the request; only the first lookup goes to Memcached.
  • static $cache = []: good for pure, cheap-to-store results (parsed config, a lookup map). Gone at the end of the request; hard to reset in tests.
  • wp_cache_add_non_persistent_groups( [ 'news_uk_request' ] ): the cache API without Memcached. Useful for per-user or large data you must not share.
  • Round trips: 50 teasers each calling wp_cache_get() is 50 network calls. wp_cache_get_multiple( $keys, $group ) is one. Core already does this when priming posts, meta and terms for a WP_Query.
Remember it as"Memcached remembers within the request too; batch what you fetch."

V23.How do you keep XML sitemaps fast on a news site with millions of posts?

"Core sitemaps build each page on request with 2,000 URLs per page and page-number pagination, so the deep, old pages are slow and crawlers hit them all. For a large archive I'd pre-generate sitemaps in the background, for example with msm-sitemap, and serve stored output."

  • Core behaviour: wp_sitemaps_get_max_urls() returns 2000 (wp_sitemaps_max_urls). Each page is a WP_Query with paged, so page 1,000 is a large OFFSET.
  • Crawler pattern: bots walk every page, often in parallel, and old pages are rarely in the edge cache. That is a lot of uncached origin work at the same time.
  • Pre-generation: msm-sitemap builds per-day sitemaps by cron and stores them, so a request is a lookup. Old days never change, so they cache well.
  • News sitemap: Google News only wants the last 48 hours. Keep that one small and short-TTL.
  • Cheap wins: drop post types and taxonomies that shouldn't be indexed (wp_sitemaps_post_types, wp_sitemaps_taxonomies).
Remember it as"Old news doesn't change: build it once."

V24.Election night is in two weeks. How do you prepare the site?

"Find what will miss the cache and what is already slow, fix it, then prove it with a load test against a production-like environment. Agree the plan for the night with VIP and the newsroom."

  • Cache hit rate: which public URLs miss the edge, and why: cookies, query strings, nocache_headers(), POST reads.
  • Slowest endpoints: P95 response times from New Relic. A slow endpoint that gets traffic holds PHP workers and DB connections.
  • Writes on GET: Query Monitor on a normal uncached page should show no INSERT or UPDATE. A write on every view means DB CPU and binlog growth, and it resets the WP_Query cache.
  • Memory and options: Memcached hit rate, alloptions size, any large autoloaded option.
  • Clean logs: no fatals or warnings, so real problems are visible on the night.
  • Load test: k6 or similar against a non-production environment, with realistic URL mix: hot results page, live blog, its polling endpoint. VIP asks to be told about load tests in advance.
  • On the night: code freeze, short TTLs on live pages instead of repeated purges, a pre-warmed results page, someone watching dashboards, a rollback plan.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q27-traffic-spike/load.php: live blog cache headers, a stampede-safe remember(), and view counts kept off GET requests.
Remember it as"Hit rate, slow pages, writes, memory, logs, then load test."

Enterprise Block Editor

V25.How do you control which blocks editors can insert, by post type, role or site?

"On the server I filter allowed_block_types_all, which gets the editor context, so I can decide per post type, user or site. For finer rules on VIP I use the Block Governance plugin. I don't hide blocks by registering them conditionally, because unregistered blocks stop rendering for readers."

  • The main filter: allowed_block_types_all( $allowed, $block_editor_context ). Return true (all), false (none) or an array of block names. $block_editor_context->post gives the post type; it can be null in the Site Editor, so check it.
  • Per role: check current_user_can() inside the filter. Per site on multisite: check get_current_blog_id() or a network option.
  • In JavaScript: unregisterBlockType() or unregisterBlockVariation() inside domReady(), so the core blocks are registered first. Use this for one variation (for example a social link) that the PHP list can't target.
  • What it does and doesn't do: it controls the inserter. It doesn't delete blocks already in content, and it isn't security: someone with REST access can still save any markup. kses and capabilities protect the content (see the security question).
  • Why not register blocks conditionally: register_block_type() inside if ( current_user_can( … ) ) means the block isn't registered for logged-out readers. A dynamic block then renders nothing on the front end, and other editors see "unsupported block".
  • Structure, not just a list: template_lock, the per-block lock attribute and contentOnly stop editors moving or deleting the parts that matter.
Course checkThe multisite lesson filters $settings['allowedBlockTypes'] in block_editor_settings_all. That value is true by default (wp-includes/block-editor.php lines 216 and 493), so is_array() is false and the code does nothing. Use allowed_block_types_all instead. The security and multisite lessons also register blocks only for some users, which breaks front-end rendering as described above.
Remember it asHide it in the inserter. Keep it registered.

V26.What is the VIP Block Governance plugin, and how does it decide which rules apply?

"It's a VIP plugin that reads a governance-rules.json file and limits which blocks, block settings and editor features each role or post type gets. One post-type rule and one role rule can apply, and the default rule is merged in under them."

  • Turning it on: on VIP it ships in the mu-plugins. Call \Automattic\VIP\Integrations\activate( 'vip-governance' ); from client-mu-plugins. It needs WordPress 6.8+ and PHP 8.2+ (version 1.0).
  • Where the rules live: governance-rules.json in the VIP private directory (WPCOM_VIP_PRIVATE_DIR). The vip_governance__governance_file_path filter can choose a different file, for example per site in a network.
  • Rule keys: type (default, role, postType), then roles or postTypes, allowedBlocks, allowedFeatures (only codeEditor and lockBlocks) and blockSettings (theme.json-style settings for each block, such as limited colour palettes). Unknown keys are dropped with a warning.
  • How rules combine: it checks postType, then role, then default. From each non-default type, only the first matching rule counts. If both match, the post-type rule's values replace the role rule's values. The default rule is then added to the result (its lists are appended). Only one default rule is allowed.
  • How it enforces: in the editor only. It uses the JS filters blockEditor.__unstableCanInsertBlockType, blockEditor.useSetting.before and editor.BlockEdit, plus block_editor_settings_all for canLockBlocks and codeEditingEnabled. There is no check when a post is saved.
  • Admin page: "VIP Block Governance" (needs manage_options) validates the JSON and shows the combined rules for a chosen role and post type. Use it before deploying a rules change.
Course checkThe lesson lists an allowedStyles property. The parser doesn't accept it (allowed keys are type, allowedBlocks, allowedFeatures, blockSettings plus roles/postTypes, vip-governance-1.0/governance/rules-parser.php line 195), so it's removed with a warning. Style limits go in blockSettings. The lesson's role and post-type example also doesn't say which rule wins: the post-type rule does.
Remember it asPost type beats role, default fills in, and it's editor-only.

V27.How do you add your own setting to a core block, for example an "uppercase" toggle on Heading, without forking it?

"Three steps: add the attribute with blocks.registerBlockType, add the control with editor.BlockEdit, and apply it on the front end with a render_block_core/heading filter. If you only style the editor, readers never see the change."

  • 1. Attribute (JS): addFilter( 'blocks.registerBlockType', 'ns/x', ( settings, name ) => … ) adds isUppercase to settings.attributes. The script must load before blocks register, so enqueue it on enqueue_block_editor_assets with the generated index.asset.php dependencies.
  • 2. Control: addFilter( 'editor.BlockEdit', … ) with createHigherOrderComponent. Return <BlockEdit { ...props } /> plus <BlockControls> or <InspectorControls> from @wordpress/block-editor. Exit early for other block names: this wraps every block.
  • 3. Front end: the attribute is saved in the block comment, so PHP can read it. add_filter( 'render_block_core/heading', fn( $html, $block ) => … , 10, 2 ), then add a class with WP_HTML_Tag_Processor. This runs at render time, so no stored markup changes.
  • Why not blocks.getSaveContent.extraProps: it changes the saved HTML of a static block. Every existing heading then fails validation if the filter is ever removed or changed. A render filter avoids that.
  • Server registration: register_block_type_args can add the attribute to the PHP block type too. That matters when the block is rendered through REST (ServerSideRender): unknown attributes fail validation there.
  • Editor preview: use editor.BlockListBlock to add a class to the wrapper in the canvas, so editors see what readers will see.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q07-fse-customisation/load.php uses render_block_core/button and WP_HTML_Tag_Processor to add an attribute to every core Button at render time. That's step 3.
Course checkThe Custom Block Controls lesson only applies the uppercase style through editor.BlockListBlock, which runs only in the editor, so the front end never changes. It also imports BlockControls from @wordpress/editor. That import has been deprecated since 5.3 (wp-includes/js/dist/editor.js line 21619); use @wordpress/block-editor. In the Editor Filters lesson, the RangeControl sets core/image width to a number, but the attribute is a string such as "300px" (wp-includes/blocks/image/block.json).
Remember it asAttribute, control, render. The editor and the front end each need their own code.

V28.How do you give a custom post type a fixed starting layout in the editor, and how is that different from its front-end template?

"The editor layout is the template argument of register_post_type(), optionally with template_lock. The front-end layout is a block template: a theme file such as single-book.html, or register_block_template() from a plugin since 6.7."

  • Editor template: a nested array, [ 'core/heading', [ 'placeholder' => 'Headline' ], [ …inner blocks… ] ]. It's applied when a new post is created, not to existing posts.
  • Existing post types: add a template to post through the register_post_type_args filter.
  • template_lock: 'all' stops inserting, moving and removing. 'insert' stops inserting and removing but allows moving. 'contentOnly' leaves only the content (text, media) editable. A block's own lock attribute overrides the template lock.
  • Changing the template later: with a lock set, posts that don't match it show "The content of your post doesn't match the template assigned to your post type." Plan migrations for old posts.
  • Front end: the theme's single-{post_type}.html follows the template hierarchy. register_block_template( 'plugin-slug//name', [ 'title', 'content', 'post_types' ] ) registers a plugin template. A slug like single-book joins the hierarchy; any other slug becomes a custom template that editors pick per post, and post_types limits where it's offered.
Course checkThe Complex InnerBlocks lesson says 'insert' "allows moving and deleting blocks". Deleting isn't allowed: any template lock blocks removal (canRemoveBlock, wp-includes/js/dist/block-editor.js lines 11646–11648), and core's own docs say so (wp-includes/post.php line 1804). The Block Templates lesson gets this right, so the course contradicts itself. Neither lesson mentions contentOnly.
Remember it asThe post type's template shapes the editor; the block template shapes the page.

V29.How would you convert legacy shortcodes and pasted HTML into your blocks?

"I use block transforms. A shortcode transform turns [gallery] into a block, a raw transform catches pasted HTML, and block transforms let editors switch between block types. They only run in the editor, so for a big archive I also run a server-side migration."

  • Types: block, shortcode, raw (paste and classic conversion), prefix (typing ?? ), enter, files (drag and drop). Each goes under transforms.from or transforms.to.
  • Shortcode transform: transform( attrs, match ) gets { named, numeric }, so read attrs.named.ids. isMatch( attrs ) skips shortcodes you can't map.
  • Block transform: transform( attributes, innerBlocks ) returns createBlock() results. isMatch( attributes, block ) makes it conditional. isMultiBlock: true handles several selected blocks at once.
  • Limits: transforms change content only when an editor opens the post and saves it. Thousands of archive articles need a WP-CLI command that rewrites post_content in batches, with a dry run, logging and a backup.
  • Testing: switchToBlockType() and rawHandler() in Jest (M14).
Course checkTwo examples in the Transforms lesson don't work. The gallery example reads atts.ids, but the transform receives { named, numeric } (wp-includes/js/dist/blocks.js line 11601, shortcode.js lines 245–256), so it should be atts.named.ids. Core does exactly that (block-library.js line 20765). The list example splits values, but since 6.1 list items are core/list-item inner blocks (wp-includes/blocks/list/block.json), so read innerBlocks.
Remember it asTransforms convert content when an editor opens a post. WP-CLI converts the whole archive.

V30.How would you stop an article being published until it meets editorial rules, such as having a standfirst and a featured image?

"In the editor I register a plugin with registerPlugin(), show a checklist in PluginPrePublishPanel, and call lockPostSaving() until the rules pass. Because editor checks can be bypassed, I enforce the same rule on the server with rest_pre_insert_post."

  • Register: registerPlugin( 'news-uk-checks', { render: Checks } ). Import the SlotFills (PluginPrePublishPanel, PluginDocumentSettingPanel, PluginSidebar, PluginPostStatusInfo) from @wordpress/editor.
  • Read state: useSelect( ( select ) => select( editorStore ).getEditedPostAttribute( 'meta' )?.standfirst ). Return small values (I5).
  • Lock: dispatch( editorStore ).lockPostSaving( 'news-uk-checks' ) disables Save and Publish. Call unlockPostSaving( 'news-uk-checks' ) when the rules pass. Each lock has a name, so several plugins can lock independently.
  • Server: rest_pre_insert_post receives the prepared post and the request. Return a WP_Error with status 400 if the status is publish or future and the rule fails. This also covers the REST API, apps and imports.
  • Why both: the editor check is a helpful prompt; the server check is the rule.
Course checkThe Slot & Fill lesson imports PluginSidebar from @wordpress/edit-post. Since 6.6 that's a deprecated wrapper around the @wordpress/editor version (wp-includes/js/dist/edit-post.js lines 2522–2527). Its "Adding Controls to the Toolbar" heading actually describes PluginBlockSettingsMenuItem, which adds an item to the block's "Options" (⋮) menu.
Remember it asA prompt in the editor, the rule on the server.

V31.How do you secure a custom block? What does WordPress already do for you?

"WordPress filters block attributes and HTML through kses when the author lacks unfiltered_html, and React escapes text in save(). My job is to escape late in render.php, check capabilities in REST permission callbacks, and treat any capability check in the editor UI as convenience only."

  • On save: for users without unfiltered_html, wp_filter_post_kses runs on content_save_pre, and the pre_kses filter wp_pre_kses_block_attributes also cleans the JSON attributes in block comments (filter_block_kses()). On multisite only super admins have unfiltered_html.
  • Static blocks: JSX text children are already escaped. Don't call escapeHTML() on them (you'll double-escape). For HTML attributes use RichText.Content, not dangerouslySetInnerHTML.
  • Dynamic blocks: output goes through get_block_wrapper_attributes(), esc_html(), esc_attr(), esc_url(), or wp_kses_post() for RichText HTML. Escape even "trusted" data: imports and direct database writes skip kses.
  • Capabilities: canUser() in the editor only hides UI. The real check is in permission_callback on REST routes and in auth_callback on registered meta.
  • VIP: no activation hooks run, so add custom capabilities with wpcom_vip_add_role_caps( $role, $caps ) behind a version check, not register_activation_hook().
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q01-article-teaser/render.php escapes late with get_block_wrapper_attributes(), esc_url() and esc_html(), and renders nothing for unpublished posts.
Course checkSeveral mistakes in the Security lesson:
  • Its save() wraps JSX text in escapeHTML(), which double-escapes, and it doesn't receive attributes.
  • It says "only Administrators can … edit Pages, while Editors can only edit Posts". Editors do have edit_pages (wp-admin/includes/schema.php), and the Site Editor needs edit_theme_options.
  • It treats canUser( 'create', 'posts' ) as "publish_posts". It actually maps to create_posts, which defaults to edit_posts (wp-includes/post.php lines 2029–2030), so contributors pass.
Remember it askses on save, escaping on output, permissions in REST. Editor checks only change the UI.

V32.How do you make a custom block accessible, in the editor and on the front end?

"I start with semantic HTML and core components, which handle labels and keyboard for me. Then I keep ARIA state in sync, announce dynamic changes, give images real alt text, and test with keyboard, a screen reader and axe."

  • Native elements first: a <button> already handles Enter, Space, focus and its role. Use ARIA only when HTML can't express it, for example aria-expanded and aria-controls on an accordion toggle, or aria-pressed on a toggle button.
  • Editor UI: @wordpress/components controls (TextControl, ToggleControl, BaseControl) link labels and help text. Always pass a label.
  • Alt text vs caption: alt text belongs in an inspector field (TextareaControl) and is read in place of the image. A caption is visible text for everyone. Don't use one field for both.
  • Dynamic content: wrap live updates (scores, "saved" messages) in an aria-live="polite" region that is already in the DOM before the text changes. Move focus deliberately after opening a dialog.
  • Colour: use theme.json palettes chosen to meet 4.5:1 (normal text) and 3:1 (large text) under WCAG AA, rather than any hex colour.
  • Testing: keyboard only, then VoiceOver or NVDA, then @axe-core/playwright in CI (M19).
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q05-interactivity/render.php renders a real <button> with aria-controls, and binds aria-expanded to state so it matches the panel before and after JS loads.
Course checkThe Accessibility lesson's CustomButton adds an onKeyDown handler for Enter and Space to a native <button>. The browser already fires click for those keys, so onClick runs twice. Its image example edits alt text through a visible figcaption, which mixes up alt text and caption. <main role="main"> is redundant.
Remember it asNative first, ARIA for state, announce changes, test with a real screen reader.

V33.What is Remote Data Blocks on VIP, and when would you use it instead of writing your own block?

"It's a VIP plugin that registers blocks whose inner core blocks are bound to data from an external API through the Block Bindings API. It handles fetching and caching. I'd use it for read-only display of third-party data, and write a custom integration when I need writes, complex logic or full control of failure handling."

  • Model: a data source (endpoint, auth headers), a query (input schema, output schema with JSONPath, cache_ttl), and register_remote_data_block(), which creates an inserter block plus patterns. HTTP and GraphQL queries are built in (HttpQuery, GraphqlQuery).
  • Rendering: inner core blocks (Heading, Paragraph, Image) are bound to the source remote-data/binding, so values are filled on the server at render time.
  • Caching: responses go into the object cache for the query's TTL, keyed by the inputs. On VIP that's Memcached, so it's shared across requests.
  • On VIP: activate with \Automattic\VIP\Integrations\activate( 'remote-data-blocks' );. It needs WordPress 6.7+ and PHP 8.1+ (version 1.8).
  • Limits: it's still a remote call at render time on a cache miss. For a slow or flaky API, Q25's approach (refresh in the background, serve stale data, fall back) still applies. Check how it behaves when the API is down before using it on a high-traffic template.
Course checkThe lesson says you bind to a source "like remote-data-blocks/http-query". The registered source is remote-data/binding (vip-integrations/remote-data-blocks-1.8/inc/Editor/DataBinding/BlockBindings.php line 24). It also calls the plugin "Beta". VIP now bundles it as a platform integration (vip-integrations.php line 89).
Remember it asSource, query, block, binding, all cached.

V34.How do you load a block's front-end JavaScript only when it's needed?

"I register the view script as a script module with viewScriptModule, so it only loads on pages with the block and loads deferred. Heavy code goes behind a dynamic import(), declared as a dynamic dependency, so WordPress adds it to the import map but doesn't preload it."

  • block.json: "viewScriptModule": "file:./view.js" (6.5+). WordPress enqueues it only when the block renders.
  • Dependencies: wp_register_script_module( $id, $src, [ '@ns/core', [ 'id' => '@ns/chart', 'import' => 'dynamic' ] ] ). Static dependencies get <link rel="modulepreload">; dynamic ones are only in the import map and download when import() runs.
  • Build: wp-scripts build --experimental-modules writes these into view.asset.php. Through npm you must pass the flag after --, as in npm run build -- --experimental-modules.
  • Trigger: load on interaction (click, IntersectionObserver) for things like charts or video players below the fold.
  • Why it matters at News UK: article pages are cached at the edge, so payload size and main-thread time drive Core Web Vitals, not server time.
Course checkThe lesson says to run npm run build --experimental-modules. Without --, npm consumes the flag and doesn't pass it to the script. Its "code splitting" webpack config points both entries at the same file and destructures { defaultConfig, plugins }, which @wordpress/scripts/config/webpack.config doesn't export (it exports the config, or an array of two configs with modules). Its examples still use "apiVersion": 2.
Remember it asStatic gets preloaded, dynamic loads on demand.

V35.How do strings in a block's JavaScript get translated?

"Wrap strings in __() from @wordpress/i18n with the text domain. Set textdomain in block.json, and WordPress calls wp_set_script_translations() for the block's scripts, which loads the JSON translation files for that script."

  • Functions: __(), _x() (with context), _n() (plurals), and sprintf() from @wordpress/i18n for placeholders. Never build sentences by joining strings.
  • Wiring: register_block_script_handle() calls wp_set_script_translations( $handle, $metadata['textdomain'] ) (wp-includes/blocks.php line 280). For scripts you enqueue yourself, call it by hand.
  • Files: wp i18n make-pot extracts JS and PHP strings; wp i18n make-json builds one JSON file per script, named with an md5 hash of the script path.
  • block.json fields: title, description and keywords are translated automatically using textdomain.
Remember it asText domain in block.json, __() in code, JSON per script.

V36.From a block, how do you change post data? When do you use editPost, editEntityRecord and saveEntityRecord?

"For the post being edited I use editPost() or useEntityProp(): they change the unsaved edits, and the change is saved with the post. saveEntityRecord() sends a REST request immediately, so I only use it for other records, such as creating a term."

  • editPost( { meta: { standfirst } } ) (core/editor): marks the post as changed and enables Undo; the change is saved when the user saves.
  • editEntityRecord( 'postType', type, id, edits ) (core): the same, for any entity, such as a related post or site settings.
  • saveEntityRecord( kind, name, record ): makes a REST request now and returns a promise. Using it on the open post saves the post behind the user's back.
  • Nonces: in the editor apiFetch already has the nonce middleware (wp-includes/script-loader.php lines 361–364), so store actions and apiFetch calls are authenticated. Don't add your own middleware there.
  • Several changes: wrap them in registry.batch() so selectors run, and components re-render, only once.
Course checkThe Modifying Data lesson calls apiFetch.use( apiFetch.createNonceMiddleware( wpApiSettings.nonce ) ). In the editor that adds a second nonce middleware, and wpApiSettings only exists if the wp-api script is loaded. The Custom Stores lesson calls registry.unregisterStore(), which the registry doesn't have (wp-includes/js/dist/data.js lines 2440–2451).
Remember it as"Edit" changes the draft, "save" sends a request.

V37.A dynamic block shows ten headlines with get_posts( [ 'fields' => 'ids' ] ), then get_the_title() and get_permalink() for each ID. Is that efficient?

"No. With 'fields' => 'ids' WP_Query doesn't prime the post cache, so each get_the_title() loads its post separately: one query per post when the object cache is cold. If you need post data, fetch full posts (one query, primed cache) or prime the IDs with _prime_post_caches()."

  • Why: the ids branch returns IDs from $wpdb->get_col() and skips update_post_caches() (class-wp-query.php lines 3311–3313).
  • When ids is right: when you only need IDs, for counting, post__in for another query, or the edge cache purge list.
  • get_posts() defaults: numberposts 5 and suppress_filters true (wp-includes/post.php lines 2588 and 2597). It's never unbounded, and it skips query filters such as ElasticPress integration unless you set suppress_filters to false.
  • Lean args for a list: no_found_rows => true, update_post_meta_cache => false and update_post_term_cache => false if you don't use meta or terms, plus ignore_sticky_posts. Then cache the rendered HTML with a key that includes wp_cache_get_last_changed( 'posts' ) (Q15).
Course checkManaging Editor Performance calls this pattern "Optimized" and says get_posts() without a limit "could return thousands of posts". Both are wrong, as above. The same lesson builds a cache key from md5( serialize( $_GET ) ), which lets any visitor create unlimited cache keys. It also passes $response->get_data() (an array) to md5(), which throws a TypeError on PHP 8, and adds Cache-Control: public to every REST response, including authenticated ones.
Remember it as'fields' => 'ids' skips the post cache, so only use it when you need just the IDs.

Mastering the WordPress REST API

V38.Walk us through what happens inside WordPress when a REST request comes in. Where would you hook authentication, rate limiting and logging?

"WP_REST_Server authenticates the request, matches a route, validates and sanitises the args, runs the permission_callback, then the callback, and filters the response before sending it. Each step has its own hook, so I put each concern at the step that fits it."

  • 1. Authenticate: check_authentication() runs the rest_authentication_errors filter. Core's cookie and nonce check (rest_cookie_check_errors) is hooked here at priority 100. Who the user is was already decided earlier, on determine_current_user.
  • 2. Short-circuit: rest_pre_dispatch runs before route matching. Return a WP_Error or response here to stop early. This is the cheapest place for a rate limit.
  • 3. Match and validate: the route regex is matched, then has_valid_params() and sanitize_params() run the args schema.
  • 4. Authorise, then run: rest_request_before_callbacks, then permission_callback, then rest_dispatch_request, then your callback, then rest_request_after_callbacks.
  • 5. Respond: rest_post_dispatch has the final response. Core uses it for _fields filtering and the Allow header, and VIP uses it to set Cache-Control. Then rest_pre_serve_request (CORS headers) and rest_pre_echo_response.
  • Gotcha: args are validated before the permission check. A logged-out caller with a bad argument gets a 400, not a 401. Keep validate_callbacks cheap and don't leak data from them.
  • Where things go: custom auth on determine_current_user; rate limiting on rest_pre_dispatch; error logging on rest_post_dispatch, only for $response->is_error().
Remember it as"Who, stop?, valid?, allowed?, do it, tidy up": authentication, pre_dispatch, args, permission, callback, post_dispatch.

V39.You need extra data on /wp/v2/posts. Would you use register_rest_field(), register_post_meta() or a rest_prepare_{post_type} filter?

"If it's a stored value, register_post_meta with show_in_rest, because it's readable, writable and validated for free. If it's computed, register_rest_field with a schema. I'd only use rest_prepare_{post_type} to change or remove existing data, because it always runs and nothing documents it."

  • register_post_meta(): the value appears under meta. It's typed and sanitised, and editors can save it through the same endpoint. The editor and block bindings can use it too.
  • register_rest_field(): a top-level field with a get_callback, an optional update_callback and a schema. Core skips the get_callback when _fields leaves the field out (rest_is_field_included() in add_additional_fields_to_object()), so clients that don't need it don't pay for it.
  • rest_prepare_{post_type}: gets the WP_REST_Response, so you edit $response->data. It runs for every post even when _fields excludes your key, and it isn't in the schema, so clients can't discover it.
  • Performance: callbacks run once per post. A query inside one is an N+1: 100 extra queries on a per_page=100 request. Read from data that's already primed (post meta and terms are cached in bulk for the page), or cache the result.
  • Always give a schema: it documents the field in OPTIONS, and its context decides whether the field shows in view, embed or edit.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q24-headless/load.php registers one news_uk field with a schema and a get_callback that reads meta, the reading time and the canonical URL.
Course checkThe course's related_posts field runs a get_posts() with post__not_in inside the get_callback, with 'schema' => null. On a collection request that's one extra uncached query per post. Lesson 16 also does unset( $response['user_pass'] ) in rest_prepare_user. WP_REST_Response doesn't implement ArrayAccess, so that's a fatal error. Core never returns user_pass anyway.
Remember it as"Stored: meta. Computed: field. Changing core output: filter."

V40.Does WordPress validate your endpoint's response against its schema? What is the schema for?

"No. For a custom route, the schema callback is only called to describe the route in an OPTIONS request. It documents the endpoint and drives argument validation, but nothing checks what your callback returns. That's up to your code and your tests."

  • Where it's used: in WP_REST_Server::get_data_for_route() the schema callback is only called when the context is help, which means an OPTIONS request.
  • In core controllers the item schema does real work: get_endpoint_args_for_item_schema() turns it into request args, so input is validated, and filter_response_by_context() removes fields that don't belong in the current context. Output values are still not type-checked.
  • Input is validated: type, enum, minimum, maxLength and so on in args are enforced by rest_validate_request_arg().
  • So for output: build the response deliberately (an allow list of fields, cast types), and assert its shape in PHPUnit with rest_do_request().
Course checkLesson 17 says "WordPress automatically validates the response against the schema before returning it to the client". That's false: see wp-includes/rest-api/class-wp-rest-server.php line 1630, where the schema is only read for 'help' === $context.
Remember it as"Schema guards the door in, not the door out."

V41.Where does $request->get_param() read from, and what happens when a validate_callback fails?

"It checks the JSON body, then the form body, then the query string, then the URL (route) parameters, then the defaults, and the first match wins. A failed validate_callback doesn't send your error as it is: core collects every failure into one 400 rest_invalid_param error, with your messages under data.params and data.details."

  • Order: JSON (only with a JSON Content-Type), POST, GET, URL, defaults (WP_REST_Request::get_parameter_order()). A query-string id beats the (?P<id>) in the route, so use get_url_params() when you need the route value.
  • Not JSON only: form-encoded and multipart bodies work too. get_json_params() is empty unless the client sent Content-Type: application/json.
  • Validation: validate_callback only runs for parameters that were sent. Missing ones are handled by required. Returning false gives a generic message; returning a WP_Error puts your message in details.
  • Order of checks: required, then validate, then sanitise, then permission_callback, then the callback.
  • Prefer schema keywords (type, enum, minimum, maximum) to hand-written validators. They also appear in the route's OPTIONS output, so clients can read the rules.
Course checkLesson 6 says the REST API "exclusively uses JSON" for requests. It also accepts form data and query parameters. Lesson 9 writes a validator for count and a sanitiser that silently turns a bad order into asc. 'type' => 'integer', 'minimum' => 1 and 'enum' => [ 'asc', 'desc' ] do the same job, documented and with a proper 400.
Remember it as"JSON, Post, Get, URL, Default": the body beats the address.

V42.How do you add a custom filter, such as ?min_words= or ?author_login=, to a core collection like /wp/v2/posts?

"Two filters: rest_{post_type}_collection_params to register the parameter with a schema, so it's validated and documented, and rest_{post_type}_query to map it onto WP_Query arguments. The filter must translate to indexed query arguments, never raw SQL that scans the posts table."

  • Register: add_filter( 'rest_post_collection_params', fn( $p ) => $p + [ 'author_login' => [ 'type' => 'string' ] ] ). Unregistered parameters still reach your filter, but unvalidated and undocumented.
  • Map: add_filter( 'rest_post_query', function ( $args, $request ) { … return $args; }, 10, 2 ).
  • Scope it: never call add_filter( 'posts_where', … ) from inside it without removing it again. It would change every later query in that request.
  • Cost: LIKE '%x%' on meta_value or on post_content can't use an index. For text search or meta filters at news scale, send the query to Enterprise Search ('es' => true).
  • Caching: each new parameter value is a new URL, so a new edge cache entry. Cap the values with enum and maximum.
Course checkIn lesson 37, min_words adds an anonymous posts_where filter that is never removed and runs LENGTH(REPLACE(post_content…)) over every post, which is a full table scan. It also says author_name filters "by author display name" but calls get_user_by( 'login', … ). In lesson 38, adding a meta_query to search narrows the results (search AND meta). It doesn't widen them as the lesson claims. Lesson 38 also lists a filter parameter, which was removed when the content endpoints merged into core in 4.7.
Remember it as"Declare it, map it, index it."

V43.How are REST API responses cached on WordPress VIP, and how do you control it?

"VIP's edge caches logged-out GET and HEAD REST responses. If you don't send Cache-Control yourself, VIP adds max-age=60. I set my own header per route: longer for stable data, short or no-cache for live or personalised data."

  • Default: the mu-plugin vip-cache-manager/ttl-manager.php hooks rest_post_dispatch and sets Cache-Control: max-age=60 on non-error GET/HEAD responses to logged-out users. Change it with the wpcom_vip_rest_read_response_ttl filter.
  • Your own header wins: it doesn't override a Cache-Control already set on the response or sent with header(). So $response->header( 'Cache-Control', 'max-age=300' ) in the callback is respected.
  • Skipped: logged-in users, errors, and POST/PUT/DELETE requests.
  • Consequences: anything per-user in a public GET will be cached for everyone, and a PHP rate limiter or logger never sees cache hits.
  • Object cache too: cache the expensive part (IDs or prepared data, not whole WP_Query objects) in wp_cache_*, and delete the key on save_post.
Course checkLesson 41 sets public, max-age=300 on live-blog updates (five minutes is too stale for breaking news) and caches the whole WP_Query object. Its invalidation deletes md5( serialize( [ 'post_parent' => $id ] ) ), but the stored key was the md5 of the full merged argument array, so the delete never matches anything.
Remember it as"Logged-out GET: sixty seconds unless you say otherwise."

V44.How would you rate limit a REST endpoint on VIP?

"First at the edge or the platform, because blocking there costs no PHP. In WordPress, only for expensive or write endpoints: an atomic counter in Memcached keyed by user or IP, checked in rest_pre_dispatch, returning a 429 with Retry-After."

  • Atomic counter: wp_cache_add( $key, 0, 'rl', 60 ) then wp_cache_incr( $key, 1, 'rl' ). A get_transient() then set_transient() pair is a race: concurrent requests read the same count. It also resets the expiry on every write.
  • Key: the user ID when authenticated. Otherwise REMOTE_ADDR, which VIP already sets to the client IP. Never read X-Forwarded-For directly, because clients can forge it.
  • Scope: check only your namespace in rest_pre_dispatch. Public GETs are mostly answered by the edge cache, so the limiter mainly protects POSTs and uncached routes.
  • Response: new WP_Error( 'rate_limited', '…', [ 'status' => 429 ] ), plus a Retry-After header.
  • Abusive clients: VIP has VIP_Request_Block::ip() / ua() in vip-config.php to block them before WordPress loads.
Course checkThe transient examples in lessons 15 and 18 are not atomic. The lesson 15 version allows 12 requests while its comment says 10. Lesson 18's "use Memcached instead of transients" is muddled: on VIP, transients are stored in Memcached. Lesson 18's logger also does a $wpdb->insert() on every REST request, a database write on every read, logging all parameters, passwords included.
Remember it as"Edge first; in PHP, add then incr."

V45.How would you version a custom REST API, and retire an old version?

"Put the version in the namespace, like news-uk/v1, and only bump it for breaking changes. Additive changes stay in v1. To retire v1, I'd announce it, send Deprecation and Sunset headers, watch the traffic, and finally return 410 Gone."

  • Why in the URL: every cache layer and log keys on the URL. With a custom version header (X-API-Version), a cache that ignores it can serve v1 to a v2 client. You'd also need a Vary header and to add the header to rest_allowed_cors_headers for browsers.
  • Non-breaking: new optional parameters, new fields. Breaking: renaming or removing fields, changing types, tightening validation.
  • Run both: register v1 and v2 side by side and share the code underneath. A thin adapter keeps the v1 response shape.
  • Signal it: $response->header( 'Deprecation', … ) and 'Sunset' (standard headers), plus a Link to the replacement. Log v1 calls to see who still uses it.
  • Remove it: new WP_Error( 'rest_gone', 'Use /news-uk/v2/…', [ 'status' => 410 ] ), so clients get the standard error shape.
Course checkLesson 25 presents header versioning as a "viable alternative" with no word on caching, and its examples register routes without permission_callback (a _doing_it_wrong notice since 5.5). The query-parameter example uses the namespace myplugin with no version. The course's custom X-Deprecation-Warning header works, but standard Deprecation/Sunset headers are what client tooling understands.
Remember it as"Version in the path, add freely, break only in v2, say goodbye with 410."

V46.A headless front end on another domain calls the REST API and the browser reports a CORS error. How does WordPress handle CORS?

"Core already sends CORS headers on REST responses: it echoes the request's Origin with Allow-Credentials true. That's safe because cookie authentication also needs a nonce, which another site can't read. Most CORS errors come from a custom request header that isn't on the allowed list, or a proxy that strips the headers."

  • Core: rest_send_cors_headers() runs on rest_pre_serve_request. It sends Access-Control-Allow-Origin: {origin}, the allowed methods, Allow-Credentials: true and Vary: Origin.
  • Allowed request headers: Authorization, X-WP-Nonce, Content-Type, Content-Disposition, Content-MD5. Add your own with the rest_allowed_cors_headers filter.
  • Readable response headers: X-WP-Total, X-WP-TotalPages and Link. Add more with rest_exposed_cors_headers.
  • Restricting origins: replace core's callback on rest_pre_serve_request with one that checks an allow list. Don't call header() in rest_api_init: it also fires outside REST responses, for example when the editor preloads data, and core's header() calls later replace your Allow-Origin anyway.
  • Why it's safe: without a valid wp_rest nonce, core treats a cookie request as logged out (rest_cookie_check_errors()).
Course checkLesson 45 sends CORS headers with header() inside rest_api_init and treats CORS as something WordPress doesn't do. Core does it already. The same lesson passes { timeout: 10000 } to fetch(), but fetch has no timeout option; use signal: AbortSignal.timeout( 10000 ).
Remember it as"Core echoes the origin; the nonce is the real lock."

V47.A headless app needs readers to log in. Would you use JWT? What are the risks?

"Only if I had to. JWT isn't in core, the common plugin swaps a username and password for a long-lived bearer token, and you can't revoke one token before it expires. If the app shares a parent domain, I'd keep cookie authentication. For readers, I'd authenticate against the subscriptions identity provider (OAuth/OIDC) and keep tokens short-lived and out of localStorage."

  • Stateless cuts both ways: the server doesn't store the token, so you can't revoke it before expiry without a deny list, which brings back the state. Changing the signing secret logs everyone out.
  • Storage: localStorage can be read by any script that gets onto the page (XSS, a compromised ad or analytics tag). A news site runs a lot of third-party JavaScript. An HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite cookie keeps the token away from scripts.
  • Password grant: the app collects the WordPress password itself. That skips MFA and SSO and teaches users to type passwords into other sites.
  • Server-to-server (syndication, CI): use application passwords. They're in core, scoped to one user, revocable one by one, and record when and where they were last used.
  • Readers vs staff: readers usually belong to the subscription platform's identity provider, not to WordPress users. Validate that provider's token server side and map it to entitlements.
Course checkLessons 13 and 31 recommend the "JWT Authentication for WP REST API" plugin and store the token in localStorage. Lesson 13's syndication example hardcodes the username and password in PHP to fetch a token, where an application password would do. Lesson 7 calls OAuth "2.0" but describes the OAuth 1.0a request-token flow. Lesson 12 says application passwords aren't for browser JavaScript, then uses one in a React front end in its own example. The plugin's configuration details (JWT_AUTH_SECRET_KEY, the /jwt-auth/v1/token route, the 7-day default expiry) can't be checked here because the plugin isn't installed; confirm them against the plugin's current docs.
Remember it as"A JWT is cash: whoever holds it spends it until it expires."

V48.How do you plug a custom token scheme, such as an SSO access token, into the REST API correctly?

"Two filters. On determine_current_user, read the Authorization header, validate the token, and return the WordPress user ID it maps to. On rest_authentication_errors, return a 401 WP_Error if a token was sent but was invalid. After that, every route's permission_callback works as normal with current_user_can()."

  • determine_current_user: return a user ID, or pass on the value you were given. Core's application passwords use the same filter (wp_validate_application_password, priority 20).
  • rest_authentication_errors: if $result is already set, return it unchanged. Otherwise return your stored error, or null when no token was sent, so the request goes on as anonymous.
  • Validate cheaply: check a signed token's signature locally (JWKS keys cached in the object cache). If you must call the provider's introspection endpoint, cache the result by token hash for a short time and set a timeout on the call.
  • Only on API requests: don't let a bearer token log someone into wp-admin.
  • Headers: some servers strip Authorization; read $_SERVER['HTTP_AUTHORIZATION'] or REDIRECT_HTTP_AUTHORIZATION.
Course checkLesson 14's external_oauth_authenticate() is never hooked to anything, and returning true wouldn't log anyone in. Its callback route also hands the access token back to the browser and sends no state parameter, so the flow is open to CSRF. Lesson 22 registers its Salesforce redirect with do_action( 'admin_post_salesforce_authorize', … ), which should be add_action.
