Remember it as"Identify on determine_current_user, complain on rest_authentication_errors, authorise in permission_callback."

V49.How can a headless client make fewer, smaller REST requests?

"Ask only for the fields you need with _fields, pull related objects in the same response with _embed, group writes with the batch endpoint, and page through lists with per_page up to 100, using the X-WP-Total and Link headers."

  • _fields=id,title,link,meta.standfirst: trims the output (dot notation for nested keys) and skips register_rest_field callbacks that aren't requested.
  • _embed=author,wp:featuredmedia: includes linked resources under _embedded. Naming the relations avoids embedding everything. Each embed is an internal sub-request, so it's cheaper over the network, not free on the server.
  • POST /batch/v1: up to 25 requests in one call (rest_get_max_batch_size). Only routes registered with 'allow_batch' => [ 'v1' => true ] take part. Core's post and term routes do.
  • Pagination: per_page is capped at 100. Pass no_found_rows in your own queries when you don't need totals.
  • Other global parameters: _envelope wraps the status and headers into the body (debugging, or clients that can't read headers). _method or X-HTTP-Method-Override sends a PUT or DELETE through a POST.
  • In the editor: rest_preload_api_request() puts responses into the page so apiFetch doesn't make the first requests at all.
Remember it as"Fields to slim, embed to join, batch to bundle, page to bound."

V50.If you register post meta with show_in_rest, who can read it and who can write it?

"Anyone who can read the post can read the meta: published posts mean everyone. The auth_callback only controls writing. For editor-only values, I'd limit the field's schema to the edit context, or not expose it."

  • Read: WP_REST_Meta_Fields::get_value() returns every registered show_in_rest key with no capability check of its own.
  • Write: auth_callback is used for add/edit/delete_post_meta. If you leave it out, protected keys (starting with _) get __return_false and other keys __return_true, after the user's edit_post check.
  • Hide from readers: 'show_in_rest' => [ 'schema' => [ 'context' => [ 'edit' ] ] ]. context=edit needs edit rights, and the context filter removes the key from view.
  • Custom post types: the meta field only exists when the post type supports custom-fields (WP_REST_Posts_Controller::get_item_schema()).
  • Scope it: register_post_meta( 'book', … ), not register_meta( 'post', … ), which applies to every post type.
Course checkLesson 36 uses register_meta( 'post', 'isbn', … ), which registers the key for every post type, and presents auth_callback as access control without saying it only covers writes. Lesson 16 runs esc_html() on user_email and returns it to anyone with list_users. Escaping belongs where the value is rendered, not in JSON, and the email shouldn't be in the response at all.
Remember it as"auth_callback guards the pen, not the page."

V51.Is /wp/v2/users a security problem? What does it show logged-out visitors?

"It lists only users who have published posts in a REST-visible post type, with their name, slug, avatar and URL; never email or login. The slug is the user_nicename, which often matches the login, so it does help guess usernames. On a news site, author pages already show the same slugs, so I'd rely on strong passwords, 2FA and login rate limits, and keep the endpoint for the editor."

  • Listing: for users without list_users, the controller adds has_published_posts.
  • Single user: returns 401/403 (rest_user_cannot_view) unless that user has published posts or you're allowed to see them.
  • Hiding it for logged-out requests is possible with rest_endpoints or a rest_pre_dispatch check, but don't break the block editor, which uses it while logged in.
  • Real protection: usernames aren't secrets. Use 2FA (Two Factor is bundled on VIP) and login rate limiting.
Course checkLesson 15 says the endpoint "can expose usernames, even to unauthenticated users". It shows the slug (user_nicename), not user_login, and only for authors with published posts.
Remember it as"Bylines are public; logins are guessed, not leaked."

V52.Why use the REST API instead of admin-ajax.php for new features?

"REST gives me routes, schema validation, permission callbacks, standard status codes and cacheable GETs. admin-ajax takes every action through one URL, usually as a POST, which the edge cache can't hold, and boots the admin side of WordPress for each call."

  • Caching: a REST GET has its own URL, so the VIP edge can cache it (60s by default for logged-out users). admin-ajax requests share one URL and are mostly POSTs, so they reach PHP every time.
  • Overhead: admin-ajax defines WP_ADMIN, loads the admin includes and fires admin_init on every call, front-end calls included.
  • Contract: REST args with a schema, plus a permission_callback, versus hand-written $_POST handling and check_ajax_referer().
  • Nonces differ: admin-ajax reads the nonce from $_REQUEST (_ajax_nonce or _wpnonce) and needs action as a form field. REST reads the wp_rest nonce from X-WP-Nonce.
  • When admin-ajax is still used: legacy code that works. Migrate it when you touch it.
Course checkLesson 29 sends a JSON body to admin-ajax.php with the nonce in X-WP-Nonce. admin-ajax won't find action in a JSON body, and check_ajax_referer() never reads that header, so the request fails. Lessons 15 and 16 also check wp_verify_nonce( …, 'wp_rest' ) by hand inside a REST callback. Core already does that for cookie requests, and doing it again breaks application-password clients, which send no nonce.
Remember it as"One URL per action can be cached; one URL for everything can't."

Enterprise WordPress Security

V53.What is the difference between validating, sanitising and escaping, and where does each one happen?

"Validate and sanitise on the way in, escape on the way out. Validation rejects anything that isn't on a known-good list, sanitising cleans what we accept, and escaping makes the value safe for the exact place it's printed."

  • Validate: a yes/no test against an allow list, as early as possible. in_array( $value, [ 'asc', 'desc' ], true ), is_email(), absint() plus a range check. Strict comparison, because PHP's loose comparison has surprising edge cases.
  • Sanitise: changes the data to make it acceptable, so it can be lossy. sanitize_text_field(), sanitize_key(), sanitize_email(), sanitize_file_name(). Use it when an allow list isn't possible.
  • Escape: encodes for one output context, as late as possible, right at the echo. Late escaping means filters that run after you can't undo it, you pick the function for the real context, and you don't double-escape.
  • Why all three: sanitising is not a database or HTML escape. sanitize_text_field() leaves quotes and the words UNION SELECT untouched, so a sanitised value still needs $wpdb->prepare() and still needs escaping on output.
  • Allow list over block list: there are always more bad values than you can list.
Remember it as"Check it, clean it, then encode it where you print it."
Course checkThe course shows sanitize_text_field() "neutralising" a URL-encoded SQL payload because it strips %20 (it removes %xx sequences, wp-includes/formatting.php line 5670). That is a side effect, not SQL protection. The same payload with real spaces passes through unchanged, as the course itself then shows.

V54.How do you choose the right escaping function?

"By where the value lands: esc_html() for text between tags, esc_attr() inside an attribute, esc_url() for any URL in the page, wp_kses() or wp_kses_post() when some HTML is allowed, and a cast such as (int) for numbers."

ContextFunctionNote
Text nodeesc_html()Encodes < > & " '.
Attribute valueesc_attr()Same encoding; always quote the attribute. Escape the whole value, static parts included.
href, srcesc_url()Drops bad characters and unsafe protocols such as javascript:.
URL for a redirect, DB or HTTP callesc_url_raw() / sanitize_url()Doesn't turn & into &#038;.
Inline JS attribute (onclick)esc_js()Avoid inline handlers if you can.
Trusted rich textwp_kses_post(), wp_kses( $html, $allowed )Removes tags and attributes not on the list.
  • Translate and escape together: esc_html__(), esc_attr_e().
  • Allowed list for wp_kses(): lower-case tag names mapped to allowed attributes; 'em' => [] allows the tag with no attributes. Or pass a context string such as 'post'.
  • Cost: wp_kses_post() parses HTML, so it's much slower than esc_html(). Don't run it on every string in a loop if the string can't contain HTML.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q05-interactivity/render.php uses esc_attr() for IDs and wp_kses_post() for the panel body; plugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/faq-item/render.php uses wp_kses( $text, [] ) to strip all tags from a heading.
Remember it as"Where does it land?" decides the function, not "what is it?".
Course checkThe course table says esc_attr() "filters". It encodes: it calls _wp_specialchars( $text, ENT_QUOTES ) (wp-includes/formatting.php line 4716), the same as esc_html(). The difference between them is only the filter name and the context they're meant for. Also, esc_url_raw() now just calls sanitize_url(), which calls esc_url( $url, $protocols, 'db' ) (lines 4607–4628).

V55.How do you pass data from PHP to JavaScript safely?

"Encode it as JSON with wp_json_encode() and attach it with wp_add_inline_script() or wp_interactivity_state(), never by echoing strings into a script. If the JSON sits inside a <script> tag, add JSON_HEX_TAG so a </script> in the data can't close the tag."

  • Why JSON: it quotes and escapes strings, so data stays data and can't become code.
  • The </script> trap: the HTML parser ends a script block at </script> before JavaScript runs. json_encode() escapes / by default (<\/script>), which helps; JSON_HEX_TAG turns < and > into < and >, which is safer and still works if someone adds JSON_UNESCAPED_SLASHES.
  • esc_js() is not for this: it's for a string inside an inline event attribute.
  • Modern blocks: wp_interactivity_state() and wp_interactivity_config() serialise for you; script modules can read JSON from a <script type="application/json"> tag.
  • Reading it in JS: parse with JSON.parse(); never eval().
wp_add_inline_script(
    'news-uk-player',
    'window.newsUkPlayer = ' . wp_json_encode( $config, JSON_HEX_TAG | JSON_HEX_AMP ) . ';',
    'before'
);
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q08-core-first/render.php prints JSON-LD with wp_json_encode( $news_uk_schema, JSON_UNESCAPED_SLASHES | JSON_HEX_TAG ): slashes stay readable in URLs because JSON_HEX_TAG already blocks </script>.
Remember it as"JSON in, HEX_TAG on, never echo raw."
Course checkwp_json_encode() adds no flags of its own: it calls json_encode( $value, $flags, $depth ) with $flags = 0 by default (wp-includes/functions.php lines 4385–4386). The slash escaping the course relies on is PHP's default and disappears with JSON_UNESCAPED_SLASHES. The course's example also puts var = ... inside a type="application/json" block, which the browser never runs as JS.

V56.How do you avoid XSS in front-end JavaScript and React?

"Never turn untrusted data into HTML. Set text with textContent or JSX, set attributes one by one, and only use innerHTML or dangerouslySetInnerHTML with markup that has been sanitised, for example with DOMPurify, or that came from trusted server-side rendering."

  • Sinks to watch: innerHTML, outerHTML, insertAdjacentHTML, document.write, jQuery .html() / .append( string ), dangerouslySetInnerHTML, eval, and href set from user data (javascript: URLs).
  • Why innerHTML is risky without <script>: scripts inserted by innerHTML don't run, but <img src=x onerror=...> does.
  • Safe pattern: build nodes: a.href = url; a.textContent = title;. JSX escapes text by default, so plain {title} is safe.
  • When HTML is needed: sanitise in the browser with DOMPurify.sanitize(), or sanitise on the server with wp_kses_post() and treat the REST field as trusted only because of that.
  • Interactivity API: data-wp-text sets text, not HTML, so state values are safe there.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q05-interactivity/save-for-later/view.js turns the excerpt's HTML into plain text with DOMParser and .textContent, then shows it with data-wp-text, so no markup from the API is ever inserted as HTML.
Remember it as"Text goes in as text. HTML goes in only after a sanitiser."
Course checkThe course says setting innerHTML through jQuery's .html() doesn't run <script> tags. That's true for native innerHTML, but not jQuery: when the string contains a script, .html() falls back to .append(), and domManip() runs the script with DOMEval() (WordPress's bundled jQuery 3.7.1, wp-includes/js/jquery/jquery.js lines 6159 and 5951). So jQuery is more dangerous than the course says.

V57.How do you prevent SQL injection in WordPress?

"Use the APIs before raw SQL: WP_Query and the other query classes, then $wpdb->insert(), update() and delete(). When I must write SQL, every value goes through $wpdb->prepare() placeholders, and anything that can't be a placeholder, such as sort direction, comes from an allow list."

  • Placeholders: %d, %f, %s, and %i for table or column names (6.2+). Don't put quotes round %s; prepare() adds them.
  • LIKE: '%' . $wpdb->esc_like( $term ) . '%' as the value of a %s. esc_like() escapes % and _ so they're literal; it isn't SQL escaping by itself.
  • IN lists: build one placeholder per value: implode( ',', array_fill( 0, count( $ids ), '%d' ) ), then pass ...$ids.
  • ORDER BY: allow-list the column and direction ('ASC' or 'DESC'), or %i for the column. sanitize_sql_orderby() exists for whole clauses.
  • Validation still matters: prepare() stops injection, not nonsense values.
  • Never: string concatenation, addslashes(), or esc_sql() as the main defence (it doesn't quote, so it's easy to misuse).
$sql = $wpdb->prepare(
    "SELECT ID FROM %i WHERE post_status = %s AND post_title LIKE %s AND post_author IN ($in) LIMIT %d",
    $wpdb->posts, 'publish', '%' . $wpdb->esc_like( $term ) . '%', ...$author_ids, ...[ 20 ]
);
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q31-incident-cli/class-standfirst-repair.php uses $wpdb->prepare() with %d for a keyset-paginated batch query.
Remember it as"Values are placeholders, names are %i, keywords are an allow list."
Course checkThree slips. (1) There is no $wpdb->select() query method: wpdb::select() switches database (wp-includes/class-wpdb.php line 1198). (2) $wpdb->insert() is not "only safe if the format argument is used": without $format every value defaults to %s (or $wpdb->field_types), and the query always goes through prepare() (lines 2883–2890, 2620). The format controls the type, not the safety. What is not escaped is the table name and the array keys used as column names (line 2614), so never build those from user input. (3) The insert example writes a plain password straight into wp_users; use wp_insert_user(), which hashes it.

V58.How do roles and capabilities work, and where do access-control bugs usually come from?

"Roles are just named sets of capabilities, and code should only ever check capabilities, with current_user_can(), ideally the object-level ones like edit_post with an ID. Most bugs are a missing check on an AJAX, REST or form handler, or a check that tests the wrong thing, such as is_admin() or a nonce alone."

  • Primitive vs meta capabilities: edit_posts is "can edit their own posts". current_user_can( 'edit_post', $id ) is a meta capability: map_meta_cap() turns it into edit_posts, edit_others_posts, edit_published_posts and so on, depending on the post's author and status.
  • Common mistakes: is_admin() only means "this is an admin screen" (true for admin-ajax.php even when logged out); wp_ajax_nopriv_ handlers that change data; checking a role name instead of a capability; a nonce with no capability check (a nonce proves intent, not permission).
  • Custom roles: add_role() and WP_Role::add_cap() write to the wp_user_roles option, so run them once (activation or an upgrade routine), not on every request.
  • Custom post types: set capability_type and map_meta_cap => true so the type gets its own capabilities and object-level checks.
  • Least privilege: default role Subscriber, few administrators, and service accounts with only the capabilities they need.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q14-rest-api/load.php checks current_user_can( 'edit_post', (int) $request['id'] ), not a role.
Remember it as"Nonce = did they mean it. Capability = may they do it. You need both."
Course checkThe course defines edit_posts as "ability to edit published posts". It's the base capability for editing one's own posts; Contributors have it but not edit_published_posts (wp-admin/includes/schema.php lines 773–826; map_meta_cap() asks for edit_published_posts for published posts, wp-includes/capabilities.php line 255).

V59.What is SSRF, and how do you prevent it in WordPress?

"Server-side request forgery is when an attacker makes our server request a URL they choose, usually something internal like a metadata endpoint or a private service. In WordPress I use wp_safe_remote_get() for any URL a user can influence, and on top of that an allow list of hosts."

  • Where it appears: "fetch this URL" features: oEmbed or link previews, importers, webhooks, image sideloading, pingbacks.
  • wp_safe_remote_*(): sets reject_unsafe_urls, so wp_http_validate_url() allows only http/https, no user:pass in the URL, ports 80, 443 and 8080, and rejects hosts that resolve to loopback, private, link-local (cloud metadata) and other reserved ranges. Redirects are checked too.
  • Add an allow list: parse with wp_parse_url(), compare the host exactly (not with str_contains, or example.com.evil.net passes).
  • Limits: requests to the site's own host are allowed; the DNS lookup at validation and the real connection are separate (DNS rebinding); filters http_request_host_is_external and http_allowed_safe_ports can loosen it.
  • Also: short timeouts, don't return raw response bodies or errors to the user, cache results.
$host = wp_parse_url( $url, PHP_URL_HOST );
if ( ! in_array( $host, [ 'api.example.com' ], true ) ) {
    return new WP_Error( 'bad_host', 'Host not allowed', [ 'status' => 400 ] );
}
$response = wp_safe_remote_get( $url, [ 'timeout' => 3 ] );
Remember it as"User picks the URL? Then safe_ plus an allow list."
Course checkThe course never mentions wp_safe_remote_get(), and its "IP validation" example is not an SSRF defence: filter_var( $ip, FILTER_VALIDATE_IP ) accepts 127.0.0.1 and 169.254.169.254. You'd need FILTER_FLAG_NO_PRIV_RANGE | FILTER_FLAG_NO_RES_RANGE, and even then the hostname must be resolved. Core does this in wp_http_validate_url() (wp-includes/http.php lines 563–670) and re-checks every redirect (WP_Http::validate_redirects(), wp-includes/class-wp-http.php line 528). Both code samples in the course also have an extra ) and won't parse.

V60.How do you keep third-party plugins and libraries patched on a site with a read-only file system?

"Updates go through Git like any other code: a pull request that bumps the version, tested on a non-production environment, then merged. On VIP the Dashboard shows outdated and vulnerable plugins and can open the update PR for wordpress.org plugins; for everything else we use Composer and npm with automated dependency PRs and audits in CI."

  • Why not auto-update: code is read-only on VIP and every container must run the same code, so wp-admin updates can't work and shouldn't.
  • Find: VIP Dashboard Code panel (versions, known vulnerabilities); composer audit, npm audit, Dependabot or Renovate; WPScan or Patchstack feeds.
  • Fix: one PR per update, read the changelog, run tests, deploy to preprod first. Security fixes get a fast lane.
  • Reduce the surface: remove unused plugins and themes; prefer small plugins we understand; a forked or abandoned plugin is our code now.
  • Third-party code is your code: it gets the same PHPCS VIP scan and review as ours.
Remember it as"Find it, PR it, test it, ship it, delete what you don't use."

V61.Is XML-RPC a risk, and how do you turn it off properly?

"Yes: it's an old API that lets one request try many passwords through system.multicall and can be used for pingback abuse. The xmlrpc_enabled filter only turns off the methods that need a login; to stop pingbacks too, remove methods with the xmlrpc_methods filter or block xmlrpc.php at the web server or edge, unless Jetpack or an app needs it."

  • Brute force: system.multicall batches many calls into one POST, so rate limits by request count miss it.
  • Pingbacks: pingback.ping makes the server fetch a URL, so it's a DoS amplifier and an SSRF vector.
  • Check before blocking: Jetpack and some mobile and publishing tools use XML-RPC. On VIP, Jetpack is loaded by default and talks to WordPress.com, so block methods selectively rather than the whole file.
add_filter( 'xmlrpc_methods', function ( $methods ) {
    unset( $methods['pingback.ping'], $methods['pingback.extensions.getPingbacks'] );
    return $methods;
} );
Remember it as"xmlrpc_enabled closes the login door, not the pingback door."
Course checkThe course says add_filter( 'xmlrpc_enabled', '__return_false' ) disables XML-RPC "entirely". Core's own docblock says the opposite: it "does not control whether XML-RPC is fully enabled", only methods that require authentication (wp-includes/class-wp-xmlrpc-server.php lines 199–221). Use xmlrpc_methods (line 176) for the rest. The .htaccess sample uses Apache 2.2 syntax; Apache 2.4 uses Require all denied.

V62.Which security headers would you set, and where?

"HSTS, X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff, a Referrer-Policy, framing protection with frame-ancestors or X-Frame-Options, a Permissions-Policy, and a Content Security Policy rolled out in report-only mode first. On VIP, HSTS is set in the Dashboard; the rest can be sent from PHP with the wp_headers filter or send_headers action, keeping in mind cached pages keep the headers they were cached with."

  • HSTS: forces HTTPS for a year (max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains). Only add preload when every subdomain is HTTPS, because it's hard to undo.
  • Framing: core already sends X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN and frame-ancestors 'self' on wp-admin and the login page. Front-end pages are your choice; embeds and AMP may need framing allowed.
  • CSP: the strongest XSS defence but hard in WordPress because of inline scripts from core, plugins and ads. Start with Content-Security-Policy-Report-Only, collect reports, then tighten. Nonce-based CSP fights full-page caching.
  • Check: curl -I or securityheaders.com, on both a cached and an uncached response.
Remember it as"HSTS in the Dashboard, the rest in PHP, CSP report-only first."
Course checkThe course treats X-Frame-Options as the recommendation. It still works, but the current standard is CSP frame-ancestors, and WordPress 6.9 sends both on admin and login pages (send_frame_options_header(), wp-includes/functions.php lines 7154–7157).

Advanced WordPress Debugging

V63.A page is slow and you suspect the database. How do you find and fix the slow query?

"I find the query and its caller in Query Monitor or the slow query log, run EXPLAIN on it to see whether it uses an index, and then change the query, not the schema: narrow the WP_Query, drop what I don't need, and cache the result."

  • Find it: Query Monitor's Queries panel sorts by time and shows the caller and component. On VIP, the slow query log and Insights & Metrics in the dashboard, and New Relic traces, show the queries that are slow under real traffic.
  • Explain it: prefix the SQL with EXPLAIN. Look at key (the index used, or NULL), rows (how many rows MySQL expects to read) and Extra (Using filesort, Using temporary are warning signs).
  • Know the core indexes: wp_posts has type_status_date (post_type, post_status, post_date, ID), so a normal "latest published posts of a type" query is already indexed. wp_postmeta indexes meta_key but only the first 191 characters, and meta_value is not indexed at all. That is why a meta_query on a value, or ordering by meta, is slow on a big site.
  • Usual fixes: 'no_found_rows' => true when you don't paginate (skips the SQL_CALC_FOUND_ROWS count), 'fields' => 'ids', a bounded posts_per_page, a taxonomy instead of a meta value for anything you filter on, Enterprise Search for complex queries, and cache the result in the object cache.
  • Why not add an index: it can help on a custom table, but on core tables it is a schema change the platform owns, it slows every write, and it often hides a query that should not exist.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q18-performance/load.php builds a "latest posts in a category" query with no_found_rows and fields => ids.
Course checkThe course suggests ALTER TABLE wp_posts ADD INDEX idx_post_status (post_status). That index is redundant for normal queries, because type_status_date already covers post type plus status. The course also says WP Super Cache and W3 Total Cache "cache database queries": WP Super Cache is a page cache only, and neither is used on VIP, where the edge cache and Memcached do this job.
Remember it asFind, EXPLAIN, rewrite, cache. Index last.

V64.What does SAVEQUERIES do, and why don't you leave it on?

"It makes $wpdb keep every query, with its time and caller, in $wpdb->queries for the rest of the request. That is what Query Monitor reads. It costs time and memory on every query, so it should only be on when someone is looking."

  • What is stored: for each query, wpdb::_do_query() calls log_query() with the SQL, the time, the caller from wp_debug_backtrace_summary() and the start time. It all stays in memory until the request ends.
  • Why it hurts: a page with 200 queries is fine. A WP-CLI import that runs 500,000 queries keeps 500,000 entries, and that alone can exhaust memory.
  • How VIP handles it: the VIP Query Monitor loader defines SAVEQUERIES as true only when Query Monitor is enabled for the current user. The VIP WP-CLI setup defines it as false for CLI runs unless you set it yourself.
  • Tip: $wpdb->num_queries is counted even with SAVEQUERIES off, so a test can assert a query count without it.
Remember it asSAVEQUERIES is a diary of every query. Nice to read, heavy to carry.

V65.You see "Allowed memory size exhausted" in the logs. How do you find the real cause?

"The file and line in the fatal error are only where PHP failed to get the next bit of memory, not what used it up. So I measure memory at points along the request to find where it jumps, and the cause is usually an unbounded query or a large array built in a loop."

  • Why the trace misleads: PHP fails at whichever allocation crosses the limit. That can be a harmless core function that runs after the real problem.
  • Measure: log memory_get_usage() and memory_get_peak_usage() at key hooks. If the page still renders, Query Monitor's overview shows peak memory, and do_action( 'qm/start', 'name' ) / do_action( 'qm/stop', 'name' ) add a Timings row with time and memory for that block.
  • Profile locally: Xdebug in profile mode writes cachegrind files; open them in KCachegrind or QCachegrind to see which functions are called most and use most.
  • Usual causes: posts_per_page => -1 on a big site, loading full post objects where IDs would do, huge option or meta values, a recursive filter, or a function that is cheap once but runs thousands of times.
  • Raising the limit is a diagnostic step, not a fix. WP_MEMORY_LIMIT only ever raises memory_limit, and only if PHP allows it to be changed at run time. In wp-admin, core raises it further to WP_MAX_MEMORY_LIMIT (256M by default) through wp_raise_memory_limit( 'admin' ). That is why something can work in admin and fail on the front end.
Course checkIn the OOM case study, the course says new WP_Query( [ 'post_type' => 'any', 'p' => 0 ] ) loads all 200,000 posts into memory. In core it doesn't: p => 0 is ignored, and posts_per_page falls back to the "posts per page" option (10 by default). It would only load everything if something else set posts_per_page => -1 or nopaging, such as a pre_get_posts filter. The real bugs are a wrong found_posts check and a full-table count. The lesson (check IDs before you query) still stands.
Remember it asThe fatal names the last straw, not the camel's load.

V66.A WP-CLI migration over 200,000 posts dies with a memory error part way through. Why, and how do you write it so it finishes?

"In a long CLI process, memory builds up in places that a web request never runs long enough to notice: the object cache's in-memory copy and the saved query log. I process in small batches and clear those in-memory copies after each batch. On VIP that is vip_inmemory_cleanup()."

  • Object cache: the Memcached drop-in keeps a local PHP copy of every key it reads or writes during the request. Over 200,000 posts, that grows without limit. vip_reset_local_object_cache() empties the local copy only; Memcached is untouched.
  • Query log: if SAVEQUERIES is on, $wpdb->queries grows with every query. vip_reset_db_query_log() empties it. WPCOM_VIP_CLI_Command::vip_inmemory_cleanup() calls both (the old name was stop_the_insanity()).
  • Batching: page by ID (WHERE ID > $last_id ORDER BY ID LIMIT 100) rather than by offset. Offsets get slower as they grow, and they skip rows if the loop changes what matches.
  • Other bulk-run habits: wp_defer_term_counting( true ) during the run (VIP's start_bulk_operation()), a dry run by default, logging progress so it can resume, and a short sleep between batches to go easy on the primary database and replication.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/q31-incident-cli/class-standfirst-repair.php pages by ID in fixed-size batches and is dry run by default. It does not yet call vip_inmemory_cleanup() between batches, which is worth adding for a very large run.
Remember it asBatch, then forget: small pages, and empty the local cache and query log after each one.

V67.What exactly do WP_DEBUG, WP_DEBUG_LOG and WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY do, and what happens to errors when WP_DEBUG is off?

"WP_DEBUG only changes which errors PHP reports: everything, including notices and deprecations. WP_DEBUG_LOG and WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY decide where those go, and they do nothing unless WP_DEBUG is on. With WP_DEBUG off, fatals and warnings are still reported and still go to the server's PHP error log."

  • WP_DEBUG true: wp_debug_mode() calls error_reporting( E_ALL ). It does not log or display anything by itself.
  • WP_DEBUG_DISPLAY: defaults to true, which sets display_errors to 1. Set it to false to hide errors, or null to leave the server's setting alone. Core always turns display off for REST, AJAX and XML-RPC requests so errors don't break JSON.
  • WP_DEBUG_LOG: true writes to wp-content/debug.log. A string is used as the log file path (since 5.1), for example /tmp/wp-errors.log, which keeps the log out of the public web root.
  • WP_DEBUG false: core sets error_reporting to errors, warnings, parse errors and user errors/warnings. Notices and deprecations are dropped. Where they go is then up to PHP's own log_errors and error_log settings.
  • On VIP: you don't write debug.log. PHP errors and your error_log() calls go to the Runtime Logs (VIP Dashboard or vip logs).
Course checkThe course's production config comments WP_DEBUG_LOG false as "Do not log errors". With WP_DEBUG off, that constant has no effect, and fatal errors are still logged by PHP's own settings, which is what you want. The Debug Bar lesson also says WP_DEBUG "logs PHP Warnings and Notices"; it only reports them.
Remember it asWP_DEBUG chooses what is reported. LOG and DISPLAY choose where it goes, and only when WP_DEBUG is on.

V68.A bug only happens in production on VIP. How do you debug it safely?

"Start with what production already records: Runtime Logs, New Relic and Query Monitor as an admin. If I need more, I deploy small, gated logging through the normal pipeline, so it runs only when I ask for it, never changes the page, and is removed afterwards."

  • Read first: Runtime Logs (vip logs or the dashboard) for PHP errors and warnings around the time. New Relic for traces, slow external calls and error stack traces. Query Monitor for users with the view_query_monitor capability; VIP's loader only switches it on for them.
  • Gated logging: wrap extra logging in a check such as a secret query parameter or a specific user, so bots and readers don't trigger millions of log lines. A unique query value also bypasses the edge cache, and you can find that request in the logs.
  • Don't change the output: no die(), var_dump() or print_r() on a live page. Use error_log(), a response header for a gated request, or do_action( 'qm/debug', ... ).
  • Don't write to the database or files: logging into an option or table on every request turns read-only traffic into writes. The VIP filesystem is read-only apart from /tmp, and /tmp is local to one container, so a log file there is incomplete and short-lived.
  • Read-only checks from the CLI: vip @app.production -- wp option get ... and similar. Some commands, such as wp shell, are not available on VIP.
  • Through Git: debug code goes through a PR and deploy like any other change, so it is reviewed, auditable and easy to revert.
Remember it asRead the logs first, then add a gated, quiet probe through Git, and take it out afterwards.

V69.How do you read a PHP stack trace, and how do you print one from your own code?

"The message line gives the file and line where the error happened. Under that, frame #0 is the most recent call, and each frame reads 'in this file, at this line, this function was called'. To get a trace on demand in WordPress, I log wp_debug_backtrace_summary()."

  • Frame format: #0 wp-includes/class-wp-hook.php(310): my_custom_function('') means line 310 of class-wp-hook.php called my_custom_function(). The error itself was inside that function, at the file and line in the message.
  • Read past the hook layers: WP_Hook->apply_filters and do_action('after_setup_theme') frames tell you which hook ran your code. That is often the answer: something ran too early or too late.
  • wp_debug_backtrace_summary(): returns one comma-separated string of function names, oldest first, with hook names (do_action('init')) and included files shown. It has no line numbers. Use debug_backtrace( DEBUG_BACKTRACE_IGNORE_ARGS ) when you need files and lines.
  • Arguments: leave them out (DEBUG_BACKTRACE_IGNORE_ARGS) unless you need them; full arguments can be huge and can contain personal data.
Course checkThe course says wp_debug_backtrace_summary() returns "file:line -- function()". In core (wp-includes/functions.php) it returns function names only, reversed and joined with commas. The course's walk-through of the sample trace is also off by one frame: it puts frame #0 in functions.php, but #0 is the call made from class-wp-hook.php line 310.
Remember it asEach frame reads "this file and line called that function". #0 is the latest call.

V70.A URL redirects unexpectedly, or loops. How do you find where the redirect comes from?

"I look at the hops with curl -sIL. The X-Redirect-By header tells me whether WordPress sent it. If it did, I hook the wp_redirect filter and log a backtrace to find the caller. If there's no header, it came from the edge, the server or a raw header( 'Location: ...' )."

  • See every hop: curl -sIL https://example.com/path shows each status code and Location header, with no browser cache in the way. Browsers cache 301s, which can make a fixed redirect look like it's still there.
  • X-Redirect-By: wp_redirect() and wp_safe_redirect() send X-Redirect-By: WordPress by default. Plugins can pass their own name, and the x_redirect_by filter can change it.
  • Find the caller: add_filter( 'wp_redirect', fn( $location, $status ) => ..., 10, 2 ), log wp_debug_backtrace_summary(), and return $location unchanged. Gate it to one URL in production.
  • Usual suspects in core: redirect_canonical() on template_redirect (trailing slashes, www, old pagination, guessing a 404 URL), wp_old_slug_redirect() after a slug change, and a mismatch between home/siteurl and the request's scheme behind a proxy, which causes http/https loops.
  • Rewrite side: Query Monitor's Request panel shows the matched rewrite rule and query vars.
Remember it ascurl the hops, read X-Redirect-By, then backtrace the wp_redirect filter.

V71.A call to a third-party API from WordPress is slow or failing in production. Which hooks and tools do you use to debug it?

"Query Monitor's HTTP API Calls panel shows every request, its status and its time. For more detail I hook http_api_debug, which gets the response or WP_Error, the arguments and the URL for every request made through the WordPress HTTP API. To test failure handling, pre_http_request lets me fake a response."

  • http_api_debug: an action with five arguments: $response, $context ('response'), $class, $parsed_args, $url. Log the status code, time and URL, never the full args, because they can contain API keys.
  • pre_http_request: returns a fake response or a WP_Error before any network call. Useful to reproduce a timeout or a 500 locally and in tests.
  • Check the timeout: the default is 5 seconds. A slow API holds a PHP worker for that long on every uncached request. On VIP, vip_safe_wp_remote_get() uses a short timeout and stops calling a failing host for a while.
  • Code that doesn't use the HTTP API (raw cURL, file_get_contents(), Guzzle) is invisible to these hooks and to Query Monitor. Search the code for the API's host name to find it.
  • Reproduce outside WordPress: the same request with curl -v shows DNS, TLS and timing, and separates "the API is slow" from "our code is slow".
Remember it asQM to see it, http_api_debug to log it, pre_http_request to fake it.

V72.An option looks correct in the database but WordPress uses an old value. How do you check whether the object cache is stale?

"I compare the database row with what WordPress gets through the cache. I check the right cache key: an autoloaded option lives inside alloptions, not under its own key. If they differ, I log every write to that option with a backtrace to find who writes it and how."

  • Database: wp db query "SELECT option_value FROM wp_options WHERE option_name = 'x'", or $wpdb->get_var().
  • Through the cache: wp option get x uses get_option(), so it goes through the cache like a page does. wp cache get x options only finds options that are not autoloaded. For autoloaded ones, look inside wp cache get alloptions options.
  • Who writes it: add_filter( 'pre_update_option_x', fn( $value, $old_value ) => ..., 10, 2 ), log the change and wp_debug_backtrace_summary(), and return $value.
  • Usual causes: a direct SQL UPDATE that skips the cache, a migration or import run without cache invalidation, two requests doing a read-modify-write on the same option at the same time, or code that changes an option during a request for one user (the VIP case study: unregister_widget() on a GDPR condition saved a changed sidebars_widgets).
  • Fixing it: delete the one key (wp cache delete alloptions options, or the single option key). Never flush the whole cache on a busy production site: it causes a stampede.
Course checkThe case study runs wp cache get sidebars_widgets options, and the object cache lesson's comparison function calls wp_cache_get( $option_name, 'options' ). Both miss autoloaded options such as sidebars_widgets, because get_option() reads those from the alloptions array and only uses the per-option key for options that are not autoloaded (wp-includes/option.php, get_option()). They report "not in cache" when the cache is fine.
Remember it asAutoloaded options live in one suitcase (alloptions), not in their own drawer.

V73.How do you expose debug information in a response without changing the page, for both page requests and REST API requests?

"Response headers. For normal page requests I use the wp_headers filter; for REST I use rest_post_dispatch and add a header to the response object, because wp_headers never runs on REST requests. Query Monitor can also add its data to a REST response with ?_envelope."

  • Front end: WP::send_headers() applies wp_headers. It runs after parse_request in WP::main().
  • REST: rest_api_loaded() runs on parse_request, serves the request and then die()s, so send_headers and wp_headers never run. Use add_filter( 'rest_post_dispatch', fn( $result, $server, $request ) => ..., 10, 3 ) and call $result->header( 'X-Debug-Foo', $value ).
  • Mind the edge cache: a header on a cacheable anonymous response is cached with it and served to everyone. Gate debug headers to logged-in users or a cache-busting debug parameter.
  • Don't leak: no user names, IDs, tokens or internal paths on public responses.
  • Query Monitor and REST: with an authenticated request (nonce) and ?_envelope, the JSON body gains a qm property with queries, cache stats, HTTP calls and logs.
Course checkThe custom headers lesson says wp_headers is the better choice "particularly when debugging REST API responses". It does not run for REST requests at all (the REST lesson later uses rest_post_dispatch, which is correct). The same lesson treats WP Super Cache as an object cache backend; it is a page cache.
Remember it asPages: wp_headers. REST: rest_post_dispatch. Both: gate them, or the CDN shares your debug header.

V74.How do you log a warning properly from WordPress code? Is error_log() enough?

"error_log() writes a plain line with no severity, and it always writes. For a real PHP warning or notice I use wp_trigger_error(), which only fires when WP_DEBUG is on. For debugging a single page, I use Query Monitor's qm/debug family."

  • error_log( $message ): the second argument is a destination type (0 = system log, 3 = file, 4 = SAPI), not a severity. Passing E_USER_WARNING doesn't make it a warning; PHP treats unknown types as 0 and logs a plain line.
  • wp_trigger_error( $function_name, $message, $error_level = E_USER_NOTICE ): since 6.4. It returns early if WP_DEBUG is off, fires wp_trigger_error_run, then calls trigger_error(). With E_USER_ERROR it throws a WP_Exception.
  • Query Monitor: do_action( 'qm/debug', $message ) and qm/info, qm/warning, qm/error, which show in the Logs panel for that request only. They do nothing if Query Monitor isn't loaded, so they're safe to leave in during an investigation.
  • In production: log context, not dumps. A short message with a request ID and the key values is searchable in the logs. A print_r() of a whole object on every request fills the logs and hides the real errors.
Course checkThe custom logs lesson shows error_log( "Warning: ...", E_USER_WARNING ) and error_log( "Critical error: ...", E_USER_ERROR ) as "logging a warning message" and "logging a critical error". These calls just log a plain line; the second argument is ignored as a severity. Checked with PHP 8.4: error_log( 'hello', E_USER_WARNING ) logs "hello" and returns true.
Remember it aserror_log() is a pen, not a severity. wp_trigger_error() raises a real one, and only in debug.

V75.Under a traffic spike the site shows "Error establishing a database connection" or "Too many connections". What is usually going on?

"Usually the database isn't down. PHP workers are holding connections for too long. Each request keeps its connection open until it finishes, so slow queries, slow remote calls or a drop in cache hit rate fill every connection slot. I look for what made requests slow or uncached at that time, not for more database capacity."

  • Mechanism: connections in use is roughly requests per second reaching PHP times seconds per request. Double either and you can hit the limit.
  • Common triggers: a cookie or header that bypasses the page cache (for example a plugin that starts a PHP session on every request), a new uncached query on a hot page, an external API without a short timeout, or a cache flush causing a stampede.
  • Where to look: New Relic for request time and database or external time per transaction; edge cache hit rate in VIP Insights & Metrics; slow query log; Runtime Logs for the first errors.
  • Separate the real connection errors: wrong credentials or host give the error on every request, at any traffic level. On VIP you don't manage credentials, and reads go to replicas, so a constant error there is a platform ticket.
Remember it asToo many connections usually means requests are too slow or too uncached, not that MySQL is too small.

Development, Build, Test, and Deployment

V76.Your plugin needs npm run build and Composer packages. How do you get those files onto VIP?

"Either commit the built files and the vendor folder, or let CI build them and push the result to a separate -built branch that VIP deploys from. On a team I'd go for CI: reviewers see only source changes and nobody's laptop decides what ships."

  • Option 1, commit the output: simple and needs no pipeline. But build/ diffs are noisy, two branches that both rebuilt will conflict, and every developer needs the same Node and package versions.
  • Option 2, build in CI: on merge to develop or production, a GitHub Actions job runs npm ci, npm run build and composer install --no-dev --optimize-autoloader, then commits the result to develop-built or production-built. The VIP environment is set to deploy from the -built branch.
  • Lock files are the contract: commit package-lock.json and composer.lock. npm ci and composer install install exactly those versions, so the build is reproducible.
  • --no-dev: PHPUnit, PHPCS and other dev tools never reach production.
  • Ship the .asset.php files: @wordpress/scripts writes index.asset.php next to each bundle with its script dependencies and a content hash. PHP reads it when enqueuing, so it must be deployed with the JS.
  • Never edit the -built branch by hand: the next CI run overwrites it. Fixes go into the source branch.
Course checkLesson 13 says to commit composer.lock and keep vendor/ out of Git. That's right for hosts that run composer install on deploy. VIP doesn't, so on VIP a missing vendor/ means a fatal error, unless CI builds a -built branch that includes it.
In this repo.gitignore excludes /plugins/vendor/ and /plugins/news-uk-practice/build/. That's fine for a practice repo. On VIP it would need a -built branch built in CI, or those folders committed.
Remember it as"Source in production, artefacts in production-built."

V77.Which branching strategy would you use on a VIP project, and why?

"Short-lived feature branches off the production branch, merged by pull request once CI and review pass. That's close to GitHub Flow. A develop or preprod branch feeds a test environment. GitFlow's long-lived release branches add merge work that a site deploying several times a week doesn't need."

  • Short-lived: days, not weeks. Small branches mean small reviews, fewer conflicts and a clean revert if something goes wrong.
  • Unfinished work: merge it behind a feature flag instead of keeping a long branch open.
  • Test environment: a feature goes to develop (or preprod) for QA, then a pull request to production. Watch for drift: if things are merged to develop and never shipped, it stops matching production. Reset it from production now and then.
  • Hotfix: branch from production, pull request, deploy, then merge production back into develop so the fix isn't lost.
  • Traceability: the ticket ID goes in the branch name and PR title (fix/NUK-123-paywall-count), so any line of code leads back to the ticket.
  • Protect the deploy branches: required status checks, at least one approving review, no direct pushes.
  • When GitFlow fits: a product shipped as versioned releases, like a plugin with several supported versions. A single site doesn't need that.
Course checkLesson 6 says feature branches "typically have longer lifespans". Aim for the opposite: long-lived branches cause merge conflicts and huge reviews, and they're the main thing trunk-based development and GitHub Flow try to avoid.
Remember it as"Branch small, merge daily, flag the unfinished."

V78.How do you make code behave differently on production, preprod and local without separate builds?

"Build once and decide at runtime. I check wp_get_environment_type() (or VIP_GO_APP_ENVIRONMENT on VIP), and keep secrets and per-environment values in VIP environment variables, read with vip_get_env_var()."

  • VIP's constant: VIP sets VIP_GO_APP_ENVIRONMENT to the environment name: production, preprod, develop, local.
  • Core's function: wp_get_environment_type() returns production, staging, development or local. VIP's mu-plugins map their names to it: develop becomes development, and anything unrecognised, such as preprod, becomes staging. Use the core function in portable code.
  • Secrets: vip @app.env config envvar set PAYWALL_API_KEY, then vip_get_env_var( 'PAYWALL_API_KEY', '' ) in code. Behind the scenes it reads the constant VIP_ENV_VAR_PAYWALL_API_KEY. Nothing secret is in Git.
  • Early config: vip-config/vip-config.php loads straight after wp-config.php. Keep it to plain PHP constants, because most of WordPress isn't loaded yet.
  • Typical uses: sandbox payment endpoints outside production, noindex on test sites, no emails to real readers from preprod.
  • Front-end values: pass them from PHP at runtime (wp_add_inline_script(), wp_interactivity_config()), not baked into the bundle.
  • Why: the artefact tested on preprod is the one that goes live. Separate builds per environment mean production runs a bundle nobody tested.
Course checkLesson 14 builds a separate bundle per environment (build:staging, build:prod) and bakes the API and CDN URLs in with webpack's DefinePlugin. Then the staging bundle isn't the production bundle. On VIP, read the environment at runtime instead.
In this repovip-config/vip-config.php defines WP_ENVIRONMENT_TYPE as local whenever the environment isn't production. That works locally, but on real VIP it would also label preprod and develop as local, overriding VIP's own mapping. Checking ! defined( 'VIP_GO_APP_ENVIRONMENT' ) instead would be safer.
Remember it as"Build once, configure at runtime, secrets in envvars."

V79.How do you ship a database schema or data change safely?

"In steps that each work with both the old and the new code: add the new structure, write to both, backfill, switch reads, then remove the old one in a later release. This is called expand and contract. Each step can run twice without harm, and I never need to restore a backup to undo it."

  • Why steps: during a deploy, old and new code run side by side for a while (servers, caches, open editor tabs). If you need to revert the code, it must still work with the data.
  • Example, renaming a meta key: release 1 writes both standfirst and _news_uk_standfirst and reads the new key, falling back to the old one. Then a backfill copies old to new. Release 2 stops reading and writing the old key. Release 3 deletes it.
  • Backfills as WP-CLI commands: in batches, dry-run by default, safe to re-run, with progress output. On VIP: vip @app.production -- wp news-uk backfill --dry-run. Batching avoids timeouts and long locks.
  • Small upgrades: store a schema version in an option and compare it on admin_init. Run the upgrade only if it's behind, and use a lock so two requests don't run it at once.
  • Custom tables: dbDelta() creates tables and adds or changes columns, but never drops or renames anything, and it's strict about SQL formatting. Prefer core tables (posts, meta, terms) where you can: you keep core's caching, queries and export tools.
  • Roll forward, not back: reverting code is cheap. Restoring a database loses everything written since the backup, which on a news site means published stories.
Course checkLesson 22 says each migration should have a "down" method, and that dbDelta() "applies only necessary changes". A down migration on live content is rarely safe; expand and contract makes it unnecessary. And dbDelta() can't remove or rename columns, so it isn't a full migration tool.
Remember it as"Expand, migrate, contract." Every step works with the code on either side of it.

V80.How do you get realistic data onto preprod or a laptop without leaking readers' personal data?

"Code goes up, content comes down. On VIP I copy production to preprod with VIP's data sync, which also rewrites the domains, and pull a copy locally with VIP-CLI. Personal data is removed or masked, and anything that talks to the outside world is switched off outside production."

  • VIP tools: data sync (production to a non-production environment) from the VIP Dashboard; vip dev-env sync sql to pull a copy into a local dev-env. You don't get direct database access on VIP, so raw mysqldump scripts don't apply.
  • What to sanitise: subscriber emails and names, commenters' emails and IPs, user sessions, password reset keys, order and payment data, API keys stored in options.
  • Serialised data: rewrite URLs with wp search-replace, which unserialises safely. A plain SQL REPLACE() breaks serialised string lengths.
  • Turn off side effects by environment type: outbound email to real readers, payment and subscription APIs (use sandboxes), partner feeds, push notifications, analytics.
  • Never point local code at the production database, even read-only.
  • Why: a laptop with reader data on it is a GDPR incident waiting to happen, and a preprod that emails real subscribers is a real one.
Remember it as"Code up, content down, people out."

V81.How do you organise a large plugin so it stays readable and testable?

"One namespace, Composer PSR-4 autoloading, folders by feature rather than by type, and one place per feature that wires its classes together and registers its hooks. Business logic doesn't call add_action(), so it can be unit tested without WordPress."

  • Namespaces: avoid name clashes without a prefix on every function. Hook callbacks then need the full name: add_action( 'init', __NAMESPACE__ . '\\register' ).
  • Autoloading: "autoload": { "psr-4": { "NewsUK\\Paywall\\": "src/" } } in composer.json, so there's no require list to maintain. PSR-4 file names (Paywall.php) clash with the WordPress class-paywall.php rule, so pick one convention and set PHPCS to match.
  • By feature: src/Paywall/, src/Newsletter/, each holding its classes, blocks, REST routes and tests. Removing a feature means removing a folder.
  • Dependencies through the constructor: a class receives what it needs, so a test can hand it a fake. Constructors only store dependencies; they don't add hooks or query anything.
  • Hooks at the edge: each feature has a register() method, called once from the plugin's main file.
  • Avoid singletons with state: they're global variables in disguise and leak state between tests.
Course checkLesson 11's Singleton example runs table setup from the constructor and exposes a query( $query ) method that passes raw SQL to $wpdb->get_results() without $wpdb->prepare(). The lesson does warn about overusing singletons; take that warning over the example.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/news-uk-practice.php uses the News_UK_Practice namespace and one folder per feature under src/, each with its own load.php and tests/. src/mock-interview/class-paywall.php receives its Subscription_Service through the constructor.
Remember it as"Folders by feature, dependencies in, hooks at the edge."

V82.Walk us through your PHPCS ruleset for a VIP project. When is it acceptable to ignore a rule?

"A phpcs.xml.dist in the repo: WordPress-Extra and WordPress-Docs for style, WordPress-VIP-Go for platform rules, PHPCompatibilityWP for the PHP version, with the text domain and minimum WordPress version set. I ignore a rule only on one line, by its exact sniff code, with the reason written next to it."

  • Installed per project: wp-coding-standards/wpcs, automattic/vipwpcs, phpcompatibility/phpcompatibility-wp and dealerdirect/phpcodesniffer-composer-installer as Composer dev dependencies. The lock file means every laptop and CI run gets the same rules.
  • Settings that matter: text_domain (catches wrong or missing text domains), minimum_wp_version (flags functions deprecated before that version), testVersion for PHPCompatibility (e.g. 8.2-).
  • VIP-Go adjusts WPCS: for example, it silences the meta_key slow-query warning because VIP's database has an index for it.
  • Ignoring a rule: // phpcs:ignore WordPress.DB.SlowDBQuery.slow_db_query_meta_query -- Limited to 5 posts and cached for 10 minutes. Never a bare phpcs:ignore, and never phpcs:disable for a whole file.
  • When it's acceptable: the tool can't see the safety (output escaped elsewhere, a query that is bounded and cached), or a VIP rule knowingly doesn't apply. The comment lets a reviewer challenge it.
  • Legacy code: in CI, run PHPCS on changed files only, so new code is clean without first fixing thousands of old warnings.
Course checkLesson 10 installs automattic/vip-coding-standards; the Composer package is automattic/vipwpcs. It excludes WordPress.VIP.SlowDBQuery.slow_db_query, a sniff name WPCS dropped years ago; today it's WordPress.DB.SlowDBQuery.slow_db_query_meta_query (and _tax_query, _meta_key, _meta_value). It also says PHPCS checks JavaScript and CSS: that was deprecated in PHPCS 3.9 and is removed in 4.0. Use ESLint and Stylelint.
In this repophpcs.xml.dist: WordPress-Extra, WordPress-Docs and WordPress-VIP-Go, text_domain news-uk, minimum_wp_version 6.8. PHPCompatibilityWP isn't installed yet.
Remember it as"Ignore one line, name the sniff, say why."

V83.The CI pipeline takes 25 minutes. How do you make it faster without losing coverage?

"Measure first, then fail fast, cache and parallelise: cheap checks first, dependency caches keyed on the lock files, separate jobs for lint, unit, integration and E2E, Playwright split into shards, and old runs cancelled when a new commit arrives."

  • Order: lint and type checks (seconds), then Jest, then PHPUnit, then Playwright. With needs: between jobs, E2E only starts once the cheap checks pass, so a typo doesn't cost a 10-minute browser run.
  • Caching: actions/setup-node with cache: npm; Composer's cache keyed on hashFiles( 'composer.lock' ); Playwright browsers; Docker image layers.
  • Cancel superseded runs: concurrency: { group: ${{ github.workflow }}-${{ github.ref }}, cancel-in-progress: true }.
  • Shard Playwright: npx playwright test --shard=1/4 across a matrix. The @wordpress/scripts config uses workers: 1 because tests share one WordPress site, so each shard needs its own site, and tests must not depend on each other's data.
  • Split PHPUnit: unit tests without WordPress run in seconds. Integration tests can be split by --group into parallel jobs.
  • Skip what can't be affected: e.g. no E2E for docs-only changes. Careful: a required check that never runs blocks the merge, so keep a small job that always reports.
Course checkLesson 19's CircleCI example puts type: approval on the deploy-production job itself. In CircleCI an approval job is only a pause button and runs no steps, so the production deploy would never run. You need a separate hold job that deploy-production requires. Its GitHub Actions example also runs PHPCS after the E2E tests, so the cheapest failure is reported last. Lesson 18's CI starts WordPress with npx wp-env start &, which can start the tests before WordPress is ready, and it never installs the Playwright browsers.
In this repo.github/workflows/ci.yml runs lint, Jest, PHPUnit and Playwright in that order in one job, with npm caching. Next steps would be concurrency and separate jobs.
Remember it as"Cheap first, cache, split, cancel."

V84.Do you practise TDD? Walk us through it on a WordPress feature.

"For logic with clear rules, yes: write a failing test, make it pass with the simplest code, then refactor with the test as a safety net. For editor UI and spikes I write the tests straight after. Every bug fix starts with a failing test that reproduces it."

  • The cycle: red (a test that fails for the right reason), green (the least code that passes), refactor (clean up while the tests stay green). Small steps, minutes each.
  • Example, paywall rules: test 1, a free article is readable by anyone. Test 2, a premium article is blocked for a visitor. Test 3, a subscriber can read it. Each is a plain PHPUnit test with a stubbed subscription service, so each runs in milliseconds.
  • What it gives you: the code is designed to be testable from the start (dependencies passed in), the tests document the business rules, and no branch is left untested.
  • Where it fits less: visual layout, exploratory spikes, glue code that only calls WordPress. Say so honestly; interviewers trust that more than "always".
  • Bug-first: reproduce the bug as a failing test, fix it, keep the test. That bug can't come back unnoticed.
In this repoplugins/news-uk-practice/src/mock-interview/class-paywall.php and tests/class-paywall-unit-test.php next to it: rules like these suit TDD.
Remember it as"Red, green, refactor. Bugs start red."

V85.How do you organise PHPUnit so the fast unit tests stay fast?

"Two suites with separate bootstraps: unit tests extend PHPUnit's TestCase and load only Composer's autoloader; integration tests extend WP_UnitTestCase and load WordPress and a test database. I run the unit suite on every save, and CI runs both."

  • Separate configs: a bootstrap applies to the whole config file, so if it loads WordPress, every test pays for booting it. Use phpunit-unit.xml.dist and phpunit.xml.dist, or one config whose bootstrap only loads WordPress when asked.
  • Folders or names: tests/Unit and tests/Integration, or a suffix such as -unit-test.php.
  • Groups for slow tests: @group external, then --exclude-group external locally.
  • WordPress functions in unit tests: Brain Monkey, with Monkey\setUp() and Monkey\tearDown() in each test's setup and teardown.
  • No process isolation by default: processIsolation="true" starts a new PHP process for every test, which is many times slower. Only use it for a test that really needs a clean process, via @runInSeparateProcess.
  • Coverage only in CI: with PCOV, which is much faster than Xdebug for coverage.
Course checkLesson 16's unit test config sets processIsolation="true" "for better isolation" while calling the suite fast. Tests that share no globals don't need it, and it makes the suite much slower. Lesson 17's integration tests use $this->factory, deprecated since WordPress 6.1 (use self::factory()), and override setUp() rather than WordPress's set_up().
In this repophpunit.xml.dist has one suite and one bootstrap that loads WordPress, so even class-reading-time-unit-test.php (a plain TestCase) waits for WordPress to boot. A second config for *-unit-test.php files with a Composer-only bootstrap would fix that.
Remember it as"Two suites, two bootstraps: one never touches WordPress."

V86.Two plugins bundle different versions of the same Composer library. What goes wrong, and how do you prevent it?

"PHP loads whichever copy's autoloader finds the class first, so one plugin silently runs against the wrong version and breaks only on some pages. On a site we control, I keep one root Composer setup with one version of each library. For code that ships into sites we don't control, I prefix the bundled libraries with Strauss or PHP-Scoper."

  • Why it happens: a class name like GuzzleHttp\Client is global. The first plugin to load it wins, and the other plugin calls methods that may not exist in that version: a fatal error, or a subtle bug.
  • Why it's hard to spot: it depends on load order and which code path runs first, so it can pass on preprod and fail in production.
  • One root composer.json: conflicts show up as a failed composer update on a laptop, not as a fatal error in production.
  • Third-party plugins with their own vendor/: check what they bundle before adding them, and test the combination.
  • Prefixing: Strauss or PHP-Scoper rewrite the namespaces at build time (e.g. NewsUK\Vendor\GuzzleHttp\Client), so your copy can't collide with anyone's.
  • Security: composer audit in CI flags dependencies with known vulnerabilities.
In this repocomposer.json is a single root for the whole application, with vendor-dir set to plugins/vendor.
Remember it as"Class names are global. One version per site, or prefix it."
